3. What do we call the little door where a wire goes in?
4. Why must a cup of water stay away from a machine?


![A girl crouches beside a big flat board with four small boxes on it joined by straight lines, and little square doors on its edge.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/basic-digital-literacy/week5-the-joining-board.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The big flat board that joins the parts is the ____ . A) table cloth B) wall C) motherboard D) curtain
2. Which of these sits on the motherboard? A) the CPU B) a shoe C) a plate D) a ball
3. The little door on the board where a wire is put in is a ____ . A) port B) gate C) pocket D) lid
4. News travels between parts along thin lines called ____ . A) rivers B) roads like lines C) ropes D) belts
5. Fill in: The enemy of a board is ____ .
6. Mention three parts that sit on the motherboard.
7. Write two sentences on what a spilled cup can do to a machine.

**My own work**

Draw a big flat square board, put three small boxes on it and join them with straight lines, then draw a circle for a port.

---

### WEEK 6 — POWER SUPPLY: THE PART THAT BRINGS THE MACHINE TO LIFE

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Basic Digital Literacy · Third Term W6.*

**You will learn to**

• say what a power supply does
• name where the power for a machine comes from
• tell how a machine keeps working when light goes off
• say two safety rules about electricity

**Things to know**

• The **power supply** is the part inside the box that **takes electricity in** and gives it to the other parts.
• Without power the CPU cannot think, the screen cannot shine and the fan cannot turn.
• Power for a machine comes from the **socket in the wall**, from an **inverter**, a **generator** or a **battery**.
• A **plug** goes into the socket; the **cable** carries the power along to the machine.
• A **power bank** or **battery** keeps a phone or a small lamp working when NEPA takes the light off.
• A machine uses a **button** to be switched on and off — we press it **once**, gently, with a finger.
• We do **not** pull the cable to switch a machine off; we press the button and then remove the plug.
• **Electricity can kill**: never put a finger, a pin or water into a socket, and never touch a cut cable.
• A **surge** from lightning can spoil a machine, so during a storm we take the plug out of the wall.
• The power supply has a **fan** too, because the work of carrying power makes it **warm**.

**Words for my notebook**

The power supply takes electricity from the wall and gives it to the parts of the machine. We press the
button to switch on and off, never pull the cable, and keep fingers, pins and water far from a socket.

**Let us talk**

1. Where does a computer get its power from?
2. What part gives power to the other parts?
3. What do we do with the plug during a storm?
4. Name one thing that must never go into a socket.


![A plug sits in a wall socket and a cable runs across the floor to a box on a table, while a boy presses one finger on a single round button.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/basic-digital-literacy/week6-power-from-the-wall.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The part that brings power into the machine is the ____ . A) shoe box B) power supply C) lunch cup D) broom
2. A plug goes into the ____ in the wall. A) socket B) window C) shelf D) curtain
3. Which of these can keep a small lamp working when light goes off? A) a stone B) a leaf C) a battery D) a cup
4. To switch a machine off we ____ . A) pull the cable hard B) press the button C) pour water on it D) hit the screen
5. Fill in: During a storm we take the ____ out of the wall.
6. Mention three places power for a machine can come from.
7. Write two sentences on the safety rules for a socket.

**My own work**

Draw a wall socket with a plug in it, a cable running to a box, and a small switch with a finger pressing it.

---

### WEEK 8 — COOLING: HOW A MACHINE KEEPS FROM SWEATING

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Basic Digital Literacy · Third Term W8.*

**You will learn to**

• say why a machine needs cooling
• point to the places air goes in and out
• tell where a machine must stand to stay cool
• name one thing that stops a fan from turning

**Things to know**

• A machine **works hard**, and hard work makes **heat** — the same way running makes your legs warm.
• **Too much heat** will spoil the CPU, so the machine has a **cooling fan** inside it.
• A **fan** is a small spinning blade, the same idea as the **electric fan** in your class.
• Air goes **in at one side** of the box, passes over the hot parts and goes **out at the other side**.
• The **holes** you see on the box are the machine's nose; if we **cover them**, the machine gets too hot.
• A machine must stand on a **hard flat table**, not on a **soft mattress** or a cloth that shuts the holes.
• **Dust** and **hair** can choke a fan; a clean machine is a cool machine.
• A laptop on a **bed** can become hot because the bed closes the air way underneath it.
• Some big machines have **air like a room cooler**; a phone just rests and cools itself.
• If a machine becomes **too hot**, it **switches itself off** to rest — let it cool before you press again.

**Words for my notebook**

Hard work makes a machine hot, so it keeps a small fan and holes at the sides for air. We set the machine
on a hard flat table, leave the holes free, and let a hot machine rest before we switch it on again.

**Let us talk**

1. What makes your legs warm at playtime?
2. What part inside the box blows the heat away?
3. Why must we not cover the holes on the box?
4. Where is a bad place to keep a laptop, and why?


![A box stands on a hard table with rows of holes on one side and a four-bladed fan on the other, arrows showing the air going through, and a folded cloth left on the floor below.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/basic-digital-literacy/week8-air-in-and-out.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A machine gets hot because it ____ . A) works hard B) sleeps long C) sings D) waits
2. The part that blows heat away is a ____ . A) spoon B) fork C) cooling fan D) plate
3. Air goes in and out of the box through the ____ . A) holes at the side B) screen C) keyboard D) mouse
4. A bad place to put a machine is on a ____ . A) wooden table B) hard shelf C) stone slab D) soft bed
5. Fill in: If a machine becomes too hot, it ____ itself off.
6. Mention two things that can choke a fan.
7. Write two sentences on where you would keep a machine so it stays cool.

**My own work**

Draw a box with three lines of holes on one side and a round fan with four blades on the other, then draw arrows for the air.

---

### WEEK 9 — HOW THE PARTS WORK TOGETHER

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Basic Digital Literacy · Third Term W9.*

**You will learn to**

• tell the story of a letter from the keyboard to the paper
• name an input device and an output device
• say what hardware and software mean
• put three steps of computer work in order

**Things to know**

• **Input** means things that send news **into** the machine: a **keyboard**, a **mouse**, a
  **microphone**, a **scanner** and a **webcam**.
• **Output** means things that bring news **out**: a **screen**, a **printer**, a **speaker** and **headphones**.
• The story of one letter: you **press a key**, the news runs to the **CPU**, the CPU shows it on the
  **screen**, and it is kept in the **hard drive**.
• To get the letter on paper, the machine sends it out at the **printer**.
• **Hardware** is every part we can **see and touch** — the box, the board, the keyboard, the mouse.
• **Software** is the work inside we **cannot touch** — the writing program, the picture program, the game.
• A machine with hardware but no software is like a **mouth with nothing to say**.
• Order of work: **put the news in**, **let the machine think**, **bring the news out**, **keep it**.
• All the parts need the **motherboard** to sit on and the **power supply** to feed them.
• The parts are not one big boss; they are like a **class at work** — one writes, one reads out, one keeps the book.

**Words for my notebook**

Input things send news into the machine, output things bring news out, and the CPU thinks between the two.
Hardware is what we touch, software is what we cannot touch, and every part sits on the motherboard and
eats from the power supply.

**Let us talk**

1. Which part sends news into the machine when you want to write a name?
2. Which part brings the name out on paper?
3. Say two things that are hardware and two that are software.
4. Put this in order: think, print, type, keep.


![A keyboard, a tall box, a blank screen and a printer with a sheet of paper stand in a row joined by straight lines, while a boy presses one key.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/basic-digital-literacy/week9-parts-in-a-row.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A keyboard and a mouse send news ____ the machine. A) into B) under C) behind D) above
2. Which of these brings news out of the machine? A) the CPU B) the keyboard C) the screen D) the mouse
3. The parts we can see and touch are the ____ . A) soft words B) power C) air D) hardware
4. The work inside the machine we cannot touch is ____ . A) software B) the box C) the cable D) the stand
5. Fill in: First we type, then the ____ thinks, then the screen shows.
6. Mention two input devices and two output devices.
7. Write two sentences on what happens to a letter you press on the keyboard.

**My own work**

Draw a keyboard, a box, a screen and a printer in a row, then join them with three straight lines.


---

# Nigerian History

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. Week 7 is Mid-Term Break, Week 10 Revision, Week 11 Examination,
Week 12 Closing — those weeks carry no note.
*Depth: NERDC 2025 · Nursery 3 · Nigerian History · Third Term — traditional rulers and title holders, the
Local Government Chairperson, and leadership qualities, from the pack's off-by-one mapping.*


### WEEK 1 — TRADITIONAL RULERS IN OUR COMMUNITY

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Nigerian History · Third Term W1.*

**You will learn to**

• say who a traditional ruler is
• tell what a ruler does for the community
• say why we respect the throne
• name two things a ruler keeps safe for the people

**Things to know**

• A **traditional ruler** is the head of the people in his area, chosen from the ruling house by the elders.
• He is **not a man who was voted for** like the chairperson; his family has held the throne for a long time.
• A ruler **keeps the peace**: when two families are fighting, they are taken to him to be settled.
• He is the **custodian of our culture** — the festivals, the land, the names and the customs belong to his keeping.
• People **show respect** to the throne: they stand, they greet well, they do not shout in his presence.
• A ruler **gives titles** to good men and women who have helped the community.
• He speaks for the people to the government and brings news of the village to the town.
• The ruler's **staff, stool or horse** are the things that show the throne is there.
• Where there is no ruler in sight, the **family father** and the **compounds' elders** keep the same work of peace.
• We obey the ruler's good rules the way we obey our father and mother — because they keep us safe.

**Words for my notebook**

A traditional ruler is the head of the people in his area, kept from the ruling house by the elders. He
settles quarrels, keeps our festivals and customs safe, and gives titles to those who help the community.

**Let us talk**

1. Who is the head of the family at home?
2. What does a ruler do when two families are fighting?
3. How do people show respect to their ruler?
4. Name one thing the ruler keeps safe for the people.


![A ruler sits on a stool with a fly whisk while two people stand before him, bowing with their caps held in both hands.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/nigerian-history/week1-the-ruler-on-his-stool.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A traditional ruler is the ____ of the people in his area. A) head B) visitor C) trader D) child
2. A ruler is chosen into the throne by ____ . A) the children B) the elders of the ruling house C) the market D) the class monitor
3. When two families are fighting, they are taken to the ruler to be ____ . A) forgotten B) punished loudly C) settled D) divided
4. The ruler keeps our ____ safe for the people. A) customs and festivals B) old shoes C) broken cups D) school fees
5. Fill in: We ____ when the ruler is in front of us.
6. Mention two things that show a throne is present.
7. Write two sentences on a ruler you have seen and what he was wearing.

**My own work**

Draw a ruler on a stool holding a fly whisk, with two people standing on the other side with their caps in their hands.

---

### WEEK 2 — EMIR, OBA, IGWE: DIFFERENT RULERS IN NIGERIA

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Nigerian History · Third Term W2.*

**You will learn to**

• name the ruler of the North, the West and the East
• tell one thing each ruler is known by
• say that Nigeria has many thrones, not one
• point to where each ruler's people live

**Things to know**

• In the **North**, the traditional ruler is called an **Emir**; he is known by his **turban** and his **horse**.
• In the **Southwest**, the ruler is called an **Oba**; he is known by his **long coral beads** and his **crown**.
• In the **Southeast**, the ruler is called an **Igwe** or an **Eze**; he is known by his **red cap** and his **staff**.
• Other people have rulers with other names: the **Obi**, the **Olu**, the **Amanyanabo**, the **Shehu**, the **Sultan**.
• Different names do not mean different value — **every throne is as important as the other**.
• A child can say at once which ruler is which, because of the **cap, the beads, the turban and the staff**.
• The ruler of a town may sit in a **palace** with a big yard in front, where people gather on festival days.
• Nigeria has **many kingdoms and many thrones**, and that is one of the things that make the country wide.
• We do not say "my ruler is better than yours"; we say **my ruler is the one my people keep**.
• When two rulers meet, they **greet each other with honour**, and their people learn peace from that day.

**Words for my notebook**

The Emir rules in the North with his turban and horse, the Oba in the Southwest with coral beads and a
crown, and the Igwe or Eze in the Southeast with a red cap and a staff. Nigeria has many thrones, and each
one is honoured by its own people.

**Let us talk**

1. What do we call the ruler in the North?
2. Which ruler is known by long coral beads?
3. Name one thing you can tell a ruler by, without hearing his name.
4. Why is it wrong to say "my ruler is better than yours"?


![Three rulers stand in a row: one with a tall wrapped turban and a horse, one with a beaded crown, long beads and a staff, and one with a soft cap and a cloth over his shoulder.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/nigerian-history/week2-emir-oba-igwe.png)
**Worksheet**

1. An Emir is the traditional ruler of the ____ . A) rivers B) coast C) bush D) North
2. The Oba is known by his long ____ . A) beads of coral B) school bag C) fishing net D) broom
3. In the Southeast the ruler is called an Igwe or an ____ . A) Emir B) Eze C) pilot D) Oba
4. Many thrones in one country teach the people to ____ . A) forget one another B) fight C) honour one another D) sell the land
5. Fill in: When two rulers meet they greet each other with ____ .
6. Mention three rulers and the place each one rules.
7. Write two sentences on what you would see in a palace yard on a festival day.

**My own work**

Draw three rulers standing side by side: one with a turban, one with a crown and long beads, one with a red cap and a staff.

---

### WEEK 3 — TRADITIONAL TITLE HOLDERS

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Nigerian History · Third Term W3.*

**You will learn to**

• say what a chief is
• tell how a person is given a title
• name two ways a title holder helps
• say why a title is not a thing we buy

**Things to know**

• A **title holder** is a man or a woman given an **honourary name** by the ruler, to help govern the people.
• A **chief** is a title holder who **speaks for a group of families** and answers for them at the palace.
• Titles are given for **good work**: helping the community, telling the truth, giving time, keeping peace.
• A person may be **chosen by the people of his area** and then **confirmed by the ruler** with drum and blessing.
• A title is **not sold and not bought**; money cannot buy the honour of a good name.
• Some titles are **very old** and have been carried by the same family for many years.
• A title holder **wears what shows his title**: a cap, a bead, a staff, a horse or a special broom.
• He helps by **explaining the ruler's words** to the people and **carrying the people's words** to the ruler.
• A title holder who behaves badly **loses the respect of the people**, even when he keeps the name.
• We greet a titled person well, the way we greet an elder: with the **right hands**, not with a shout.

**Words for my notebook**

A title holder is a man or woman honoured by the ruler to help govern the people. Titles are given for
good work and cannot be bought, and a chief carries the words of the families to the palace and back.

**Let us talk**

1. What do we call a person given an honourary name by the ruler?
2. For what kind of work are titles given?
3. Name two things a title holder may wear.
4. Can a child buy a title for his father with money? Why not?


![A chief in a cap and robe holds a short staff at the palace door while two people greet him with their hands held flat and low.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/nigerian-history/week3-the-chief-at-the-palace-door.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A chief is a ____ who speaks for a group of families. A) trader B) stranger C) child D) title holder
2. Titles are mostly given to people who ____ . A) tell the truth and help the community B) shout loudest C) own many goats D) come from far away
3. A title is given first by the people and then ____ by the ruler. A) forgotten B) denied C) confirmed D) sold
4. Which of these is true about a title? A) it can be bought B) it cannot be bought C) it is a toy D) it is a market day
5. Fill in: A title holder carries the words of the families to the ____ .
6. Mention three things a chief may wear to show his title.
7. Write two sentences on how you greet an elder with your hands.

**My own work**

Draw a chief with a cap and a short staff standing in front of a palace door, and two people greeting him with both hands.

---

### WEEK 4 — BAALE, IYALOJA, OTUNBA: THE WORK OF TITLES

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Nigerian History · Third Term W4.*

**You will learn to**

• say what a Baale, an Iyaloja and an Otunba do
• tell how each one helps the community
• say why the ruler needs helpers
• name one way each title keeps the town working

**Things to know**

• The **Baale** is the **father of the compound or the village street**; he is the oldest and settles small quarrels.
• The **Iyaloja** is the **mother of the market**; she keeps the stalls in order and settles traders' disputes.
• The **Otunba** is a **chief who stands next in line to help the ruler**, and carries messages to the quarters.
• Titles work like the parts of a house: the **roof, the pillars and the floor** each hold something up.
• The ruler **cannot be everywhere at once**, so the title holders see to the streets, the market and the families.
• A title holder **calls meetings**, tells the people news, and **leads them in clean-up and safety work**.
• The Iyaloja knows **the price of yam and rice** and can tell when a seller is cheating a buyer.
• The Baale knows **where every family lives** and who is sick, who is new and who needs help.
• Title holders are to be **respected as institutions**, not as rich persons: the honour belongs to the chair they sit on.
• When the ruler and his chiefs **work together**, roads are watched, markets are open and festivals go well.

**Words for my notebook**

The Baale is the father of the street, the Iyaloja is the mother of the market, and the Otunba helps the
ruler with messages and the quarters. The ruler cannot be everywhere, so titles hold the town up together.

**Let us talk**

1. Who settles small quarrels in the village street?
2. Who keeps order in the market?
3. Why does a ruler need title holders to help him?
4. What does the Iyaloja know about the price of food?


![An old man sits on a stool settling two neighbours, a market woman stands behind her yam stall, and a chief walks with a rolled mat under his arm.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/nigerian-history/week4-baale-iyaloja-otunba.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The father of the village street, who settles small quarrels, is the ____ . A) Baale B) Iyaloja C) Otunba D) stranger
2. The mother of the market is called the ____ . A) Otunba B) Iyaloja C) Baale D) Baby
3. The chief who carries messages to the quarters for the ruler is the ____ . A) visitor B) child C) Otunba D) farmer
4. Title holders are respected as ____ , not as rich persons. A) institutions B) traders C) friends D) singers
5. Fill in: The ruler cannot be ____ at once, so titles help him.
6. Mention two things an Iyaloja does in the market.
7. Write two sentences on work the Baale does for your street.

**My own work**

Draw a market scene with a woman standing by a stall holding a fan, two children carrying small baskets, and a mat on the ground.

---

### WEEK 5 — THE LOCAL GOVERNMENT AND ITS CHAIRPERSON

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Nigerian History · Third Term W5.*

**You will learn to**

• say what a Local Government is
• name the head of a Local Government
• tell how a chairperson comes to office
• say why we count votes

**Things to know**

• Nigeria is divided big: **the country**, then **36 states and the FCT**, then **774 Local Governments**.
• A **Local Government** is the **nearest government to the people** — it is the one that touches your street.
• The head of a Local Government is called the **Executive Chairman**.
• A chairman is **chosen by voting**: the people mark the man or woman they want, and **the most votes wins**.
• Voting is how we settle who leads **without fighting** — the box of votes takes the place of the machete.
• To vote, one must be **eighteen years or more**; a child's work is to **behave well at the election**, not to queue.
• The chairman works with **councillors**, each one sent by a ward to speak for that ward.
• The **palace and the council are different**: the ruler sits on the throne, the chairman sits on an elected chair.
• A chairman's time is **limited** — four years, then the people choose again; a throne is for life.
• Our own things come from the Local Government: **the primary school, the health centre, the street drains, the market stalls**.

**Words for my notebook**

A Local Government is the nearest government to the people, and its head is the Executive Chairman, chosen
by voting. The most votes wins, so we settle who leads without fighting, and a chairman's time is limited
to a few years.

**Let us talk**

1. Which government is nearest to your street?
2. What do we call the head of a Local Government?
3. How do the people choose a chairman?
4. Name two things a Local Government brings to a community.


![A man in a queue puts a folded paper into a plain box with a slit on top, while others wait behind him and two children watch.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/nigerian-history/week5-the-vote-in-the-box.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The nearest government to the people is the ____ . A) palace B) church C) Local Government D) market
2. The head of a Local Government is called the ____ . A) king B) class teacher C) Babalawo D) Executive Chairman
3. A chairman comes to office by ____ . A) voting B) a coin toss C) a race D) buying the chair
4. A ruler sits on the throne while a chairman sits on ____ . A) a mat B) a stool C) a bed D) an elected chair
5. Fill in: To vote for a chairman, one must be ____ years or more.
6. Mention three things a Local Government gives a community.
7. Write two sentences on how the people choose a class monitor and how the people choose a chairman.

**My own work**

Draw two lines of children in front of a school gate, then draw a table with a box on it and a man putting a paper in the box.

---

### WEEK 6 — WHAT A GOOD CHAIRMAN DOES

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Nigerian History · Third Term W6.*

**You will learn to**

• name four services a chairman provides
• tell the difference between a ruler and a chairman
• say what makes a leader good
• tell how a ruler and a chairman work together

**Things to know**

• A chairman **builds and mends the roads**, so that our yam and palm oil can reach the town.
• He **sees to the primary schools** — rooms, benches, books and the teacher's pay.
• He keeps the **health centre** working, with a nurse, drugs and a place for a mother to rest.
• He clears **drains and gutters** before the rains, so that water will not enter our rooms.
• He opens **the market, the borehole and the street lamps**, and lights them at night.
• A **ruler** keeps the customs and settles the people at the palace; a **chairman** keeps the services and signs the papers.
• A ruler is **on the throne for life**; a chairman is **in his office for a fixed time**.
• A **good leader is honest**, listens before he decides, shares work fairly and does not take what is not his.
• When the ruler and the chairman **sit together**, the town gets both wisdom and money.
• If a chairman does not do the work, the people **do not vote for him again** at the end of his time.

**Words for my notebook**

A chairman mends roads, keeps the school and the health centre working, clears drains before the rain and
opens the market. A ruler keeps our customs and sits for life; a chairman keeps the services and stays a
fixed time, and both work together for the town.

**Let us talk**

1. What does a chairman do for the road before the market day?
2. Which of the two, a ruler or a chairman, sits for a fixed time only?
3. Name three services you can see in your community.
4. What happens to a chairman who does not work?


![A man fills a hole in the road with a shovel while a boy carries a bucket, with an open drain beside them and a school building with a bell beyond.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/nigerian-history/week6-roads-drains-school.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Who mends the road and clears the drains in a Local Government? A) the class monitor B) the trader C) the chairman D) the driver
2. Which of these is a service a chairman provides? A) rain B) sleep C) sunlight D) a working health centre
3. A ruler sits on the throne ____ , while a chairman stays a fixed time. A) for life B) for one week C) for one day D) for an hour
4. A leader who is ____ does not take what is not his. A) sleepy B) rich C) honest D) tall
5. Fill in: When the ruler and the chairman sit ____ , the town gains wisdom and money.
6. Mention four services a good chairman provides.
7. Write two sentences on the state of the road to your school and what should be done to it.

**My own work**

Draw a road with two holes in it, a man with a shovel mending it, and a boy carrying a bucket of tar behind him.

---

### WEEK 8 — WHAT MAKES A GOOD LEADER

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Nigerian History · Third Term W8.*

**You will learn to**

• name five qualities of a good leader
• tell why a leader must listen
• say what fairness looks like in my class
• show responsibility with one small job

**Things to know**

• A **leader** is anybody who **goes in front** — a ruler, a chairman, a monitor, a mother, the one holding the rope.
• The first quality is **honesty**: a leader tells the truth even when the truth will bring him trouble.
• **Truthfulness** means he does not say one thing to the ruler and another thing to the people.
• **Kindness** is a leader's second hand: he corrects without insulting, and helps before he shouts.
• **Fairness** means the same rule for every child, not a sweet for the one he likes best.
• A good leader **listens to both sides** before he decides; a leader who only talks will decide wrongly.
• **Responsibility**: if he is given the key, the key is not lost; if he is given the chalk, the chalk is finished well.
• **Hard work**: a leader is the first to arrive and the last to drop the broom.
• A leader **admits his own mistake**; only a small man hides behind "it was not me".
• A leader **does not use his position to take** the class biscuits for his own brothers.

**Words for my notebook**

A good leader is honest and truthful, kind and fair, listens before he decides, keeps what he is given and
works hard. He owns his own mistake and never uses his position to take what belongs to all.

**Let us talk**

1. Who are the leaders you know besides the ruler and the chairman?
2. What does a fair teacher do with two children who quarrelled?
3. Show me how a leader listens before he decides.
4. What do we call a leader who takes the class things for his brothers?


![A taller girl with one hand raised and a book under her arm keeps four children standing in a line behind her.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/nigerian-history/week8-the-class-monitor.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A leader is anybody who ____ . A) sleeps late B) goes in front C) eats first D) stands behind
2. Telling the truth even when it brings trouble is called ____ . A) honesty B) sleeping C) running D) hiding
3. Fairness means the rule is ____ . A) only for small children B) only for boys C) the same for every child D) changed every day
4. Before he decides, a good leader ____ . A) listens to both sides B) runs away C) closes his ears D) hits the table
5. Fill in: A leader who is given the key must not ____ it.
6. Mention five qualities of a good leader.
7. Write two sentences on a time you were given a small job and finished it well.

**My own work**

Draw a monitor standing at the head of a line of four children, one hand up to keep them in order, carrying the class book.

---

### WEEK 9 — LEADERSHIP IN MY OWN DAY

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Nigerian History · Third Term W9.*

**You will learn to**

• say what a class monitor does
• tell how a leader leads by example
• name three ways I can lead today
• say how a leader shows respect to others

**Things to know**

• A **class monitor** is a child chosen to **keep order**, to carry a note to the office and to stand at the head of the line.
• A **prefect** does the same for the whole school; he is a leader **before he is a boss**.
• **Leading by example**: if I want the class to be quiet, **my own mouth must be quiet first**.
• If I want others to keep the room clean, **I pick my own paper up** without waiting to be called.
• A child can lead in small ways: **share a chalk**, **help a friend to read**, **line the class up**, **hold the gate**.
• A leader **says please and thank you**, because his position does not make him bigger than anybody.
• A leader **respects those who lead before him** — he greets the monitor, the teacher and the ruler's chief.
• Leadership skills are **learnt young**: the child who keeps one promise becomes the man who keeps many.
• In **role play**, one child sits on the stool as the Baale and another brings him the quarrel of two dolls; the class watches how the case is settled.
• A leader is **known by what he does when nobody is watching**, not by the cap on his head.

**Words for my notebook**

A class monitor keeps order and stands at the head of the line; a prefect does it for the whole school. A
leader leads by example — my own mouth quiet before I ask for quiet, my own paper picked up before I call
to others — and leadership is learnt young.

**Let us talk**

1. Who is the monitor of this class?
2. What must I do first before I ask the class to be quiet?
3. Name two ways you can lead your small brother or sister today.
4. What does a leader say when he wants somebody to pass him a book?


![A small boy in an oversized cap sits on a stool with a fly whisk while two girls bring their dolls before him and three children watch.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/nigerian-history/week9-the-village-court.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A child chosen to keep order in the class is the ____ . A) visitor B) class monitor C) trader D) baby
2. A prefect is a leader for ____ . A) the whole school B) one house C) his own friends only D) the market
3. To lead by example, before I ask for quiet I must be ____ myself. A) loud B) singing C) quiet D) running
4. A leader shows respect by saying ____ . A) nothing at all B) please and thank you C) go away D) I am the king
5. Fill in: A leader is known by what he does when ____ is watching.
6. Mention three ways a child can lead in this class today.
7. Write two sentences on the part you took in the role play of the Baale settling a quarrel.

**My own work**

Draw a small court with a child sitting on a stool as the ruler and two other children standing before him, each holding one doll.


---

# Physical and Health Education (PHE)

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. Week 7 is Mid-Term Break, Week 10 Revision, Week 11 Examination,
Week 12 Closing — those weeks carry no note.
*Depth: NERDC 2025 · Nursery 3 · Physical and Health Education · Third Term, pp 558–563 (sports
facilities and equipment, the ball games, table tennis, chess, video games), and Primary 1 PHE
motor-skills weeks for W8–W9.*


### WEEK 1 — PLACES WE PLAY AND THINGS WE PLAY WITH

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · PHE · Third Term W1 (pp 558–563).*

**You will learn to**

• say what a sport place is
• name four places where sport is played
• tell the difference between a facility and an equipment
• say three things we do to care for the class equipment

**Things to know**

• A **sport facility** is the **place or the building** where a game is played.
• A **sport equipment** is the **thing you hold or wear** to play the game.
• Places we know: the **playground** for running and football, the **court** for basketball and tennis, the
  **field** for the big matches, and the **pool** for swimming.
• Equipment we know: a **ball**, a **whistle**, a **stopwatch**, **bibs** to mark the two teams,
  **boots**, **shin guards**, **nets**, **goal posts**, **cones** to mark a line and **mats** to fall on.
• Facilities **make the game possible** and keep us from being hurt by a hole or a stone.
• Equipment **must be used well**: we do not kick a football at a window, and we do not sit on the mat stack.
• After play, things go **back to their box or their store**, and the monitor **counts them**.
• A thing that is **torn or broken is reported**, not hidden under the bench.
• Not every school has a pool or a gym; a good class **plays well with what it has** — a rope, four stones
  for a goal, and a flat ground.

**Words for my notebook**

A facility is the place we play in and an equipment is the thing we play with. We use both well, put every
thing back in the store after play, and report the one that is torn or broken.

**Let us talk**

1. Where does this school play football?
2. Name two things you hold when you play.
3. Why do we put the ball back in the box?
4. What do we do if a net is torn?


![Two children kneel at an open store box holding a ball, a rolled mat, a rope and a whistle, with a goal and two bibs behind them.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/physical-and-health-education/week1-the-store-box.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The place where a game is played is a sport ____ . A) facility B) trouble C) dream D) song
2. Which of these is an equipment? A) the field B) the court C) a ball D) the pool
3. We keep the class things safe by putting them back in the ____ . A) fire B) store C) bag of a stranger D) water
4. When the school has no court, the class ____ . A) does not move again B) sells the rope C) sleeps D) plays on the flat ground it has
5. Fill in: A torn net must be ____ , not hidden.
6. Mention four places sport can be played.
7. Write two sentences on how you cared for the class ball today.

**My own work**

Draw a football field with two goals made of stones, and beside it a box holding a ball, a whistle and a rope.

---

### WEEK 2 — FOOTBALL: KICK IT, KEEP IT, SCORE IT

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · PHE · Third Term W2 (pp 558–563).*

**You will learn to**

• pass a ball with the inside of the foot
• dribble the ball close to your feet
• shoot at a goal
• name four things a football team wears

**Things to know**

• Football is played with a **round ball** and **two teams**; the team that puts the ball in the goal wins.
• **Passing** is kicking the ball to a mate; the **inside of the foot** is the truest pass a child can make.
• **Dribbling** is running the ball along while you keep it **close to your feet** and look up to see your mates.
• **Shooting** is kicking at the goal — not the loudest kick, but the **steady** one.
• **No hand** for a player in the field; only the **goalkeeper** may use his hands, and only inside his own box.
• A goal is made of **two posts and a bar**, with a net behind to catch the ball.
• What a player wears: a **jersey**, **shorts**, **socks**, **boots** and **shin guards** inside the socks.
• The **referee** carries the **whistle**; one short blast means play, a long one means a foul was done.
• When we **lose**, we shake hands; when we **win**, we do not mock the other side — that is sportsmanship.
• A small field, a size-3 ball and two piles of stones for posts make a real match for our class.

**Words for my notebook**

In football we pass with the inside of the foot, dribble with the ball kept close, and shoot steadily at the
goal. Only the goalkeeper may touch the ball with his hands, and a good team shakes hands after the game.

**Let us talk**

1. Which part of the foot makes the truest pass?
2. Who alone may use his hands in the game?
3. What does the referee blow?
4. What do we do to the other team when we have beaten them?


![A boy kicks a ball with the inside of his foot towards a netted goal where a goalkeeper crouches with both hands out.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/physical-and-health-education/week2-the-inside-of-the-foot.png)
**Worksheet**

1. In football, only the ____ may touch the ball with his hands. A) referee B) striker C) goalkeeper D) runner
2. A truest pass is kicked with the ____ of the foot. A) inside B) top C) heel D) toe nail
3. While dribbling, the ball is kept ____ . A) far ahead B) close to the feet C) in the hands D) under a chair
4. The referee starts and stops play with a ____ . A) drum B) bell C) book D) whistle
5. Fill in: A goal is made of two posts and a ____ .
6. Mention four things a football player wears.
7. Write two sentences on the last match you played and how it ended.

**My own work**

Draw a boy kicking a ball with the inside of his foot towards two stones laid on the ground for a goal.

---

### WEEK 3 — BASKETBALL: BOUNCE IT AND THROW IT IN

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · PHE · Third Term W3 (pp 558–563).*

**You will learn to**

• bounce a ball on the spot with one hand
• pass a ball from the chest with two hands
• throw a ball towards a hoop
• say one rule of basketball

**Things to know**

• Basketball is played with a **big bouncy ball** and **two teams**; the ball is **thrown**, not kicked.
• The hoop is **raised on a board**, and into it the ball must go to give a point to the team.
• **Dribbling** is bouncing the ball with the **finger tips of one hand**, keeping it **low**, and looking up.
• The ball is **not carried** while the feet run: **bounce it or pass it** — that is the first rule a child learns.
• A **chest pass** is pushed from the chest with both hands, thumbs ending down.
• A **bounce pass** is thrown to the floor so the ball jumps up into your mate's hands.
• To shoot, we **balance, keep our eyes on the hoop, bring the elbow under the ball and follow through** with
  the wrist.
• A **foul** is pushing, holding or hitting the other child's arm; two free throws are given for it.
• In our class the hoop is **put low**, and a ring painted on the wall will do just as well.
• Basketball makes the legs springy, the hands quick and the eyes steady.

**Words for my notebook**

In basketball the ball is bounced with the finger tips, passed from the chest with two hands and thrown at a
raised hoop. We do not run while we hold the ball — we bounce it or we pass it.

**Let us talk**

1. Which part of the hand pushes the ball down when we dribble?
2. What do we do instead of running with the ball in our hands?
3. Where must the ball go to give our team a point?
4. What is a foul in this game?


![A girl bounces a big ball with one hand while a boy waits with both hands raised near a hoop fixed low on a board.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/physical-and-health-education/week3-bounce-and-catch.png)
**Worksheet**

1. In basketball the ball is thrown at a raised ____ . A) net on the ground B) hoop C) wall D) ladder
2. To move with the ball you must ____ it on the floor. A) roll B) hide C) bounce D) kick
3. A chest pass is pushed out with ____ hands. A) one B) two C) three D) no
4. While dribbling, the head stays ____ . A) down on the shoe B) under the ball C) turned away D) up to see the mates
5. Fill in: In our class the hoop is put ____ so that children can reach it.
6. Mention two kinds of pass in basketball.
7. Write two sentences on the game where you bounced the ball best.

**My own work**

Draw a girl bouncing a big ball with one hand, and a hoop on a board above a low stand at the other side of the page.

---

### WEEK 4 — TABLE TENNIS: THE BALL THAT MUST NOT DROP

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · PHE · Third Term W4 (pp 558–563).*

**You will learn to**

• hold a bat the right way
• serve so the ball bounces twice
• hit the ball back over the net
• name three things needed for the game

**Things to know**

• Table tennis is **like tennis on a table**, and that is how it got its name.
• It is played by **two children** or by **four**, two on each side of the net.
• The **bat** is held like a **handshake** — the flat part in the palm, the handle between the fingers.
• To **serve**, the ball is thrown up lightly and hit **underhand**, so that it bounces on **your own side
  first** and then on the other.
• During play the ball may **bounce only once** on a side; the second bounce is a point for the other pair.
• We hit **forehand** on the side the hand holds out, and **backhand** across the body.
• What the game needs: a **table with a net across it**, **two bats** and a **small light ball**.
• A table needs **no big ground**, so it can be played **in the rain or in the shade** — one of its kindnesses.
• The eye must stay on the ball the whole time; a child who watches the ball **cannot miss it for long**.
• The ball is **not blown at** to make it move, and the bat is **not raised above the table edge** to hit down
  at the other child's face.

**Words for my notebook**

Table tennis is tennis on a table. We hold the bat like a handshake, serve so the ball bounces once on our
side and once on theirs, and hit it back before it bounces twice.

**Let us talk**

1. What is the small net on the table for?
2. Show me how you hold a bat like a handshake.
3. How many times may the ball bounce on one side during play?
4. Why can this game be played when it is raining?


![Two children stand at the ends of a table with a low net across it, bats in hand, a small ball dropping into one half.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/physical-and-health-education/week4-one-bounce-only.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Table tennis is played on a ____ . A) big field B) table C) road D) roof
2. At serve, the ball bounces first on ____ . A) your own side B) the floor C) the roof D) the bag
3. The eyes of a good player stay on the ____ . A) shoe B) window C) ball D) net post
4. How many children may play one game of table tennis? A) four only B) one C) ten D) two or four
5. Fill in: The ball may bounce only ____ on a side during play.
6. Mention three things needed to play table tennis.
7. Write two sentences on how you held the bat when you played.

**My own work**

Draw a table with a low net across it, a ball above one half, and two children at the ends holding bats.

---

### WEEK 5 — CHESS: THE GAME PLAYED WITH THE HEAD

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · PHE · Third Term W5 (pp 558–563).*

**You will learn to**

• say what chess is
• point to the two colours on a board
• name four chess men
• say one thing chess teaches a child

**Things to know**

• **Chess** is a **thinking game** for **two players**; nobody runs and nobody shouts.
• It is played on a **checkered board** of **light and dark squares**, and each player takes the **light
  corner on his right hand**.
• Each side begins with the same family of men: **a king, a queen, two castles, two jumpers and eight small
  men in front**.
• The **king** is the most important man on the board; the game is ended when he **cannot escape**.
• The **queen** is the mightiest: she goes as far as the board is long, straight or crooked.
• The **castle** goes straight, the **jumper** leaps over a man in an **L** shape, and the small **man** goes
  one square at a time.
• We learn chess for **thinking ahead**, for **patience** and for **sitting still to look well**.
• **Touching a man means playing it** — a child who has touched must move, so look before the hand goes out.
• If a man is moved by mistake, we **say nothing** and play it; the game is also a school for truth.
• Chess is played all over the world, in houses, in clubs and in big halls where grown-ups sit very quiet.

**Words for my notebook**

Chess is a thinking game for two on a board of light and dark squares. Each side keeps a king, a queen, two
castles, two jumpers and eight small men, and the game ends when the king cannot get away.

**Let us talk**

1. Does chess need running or thinking?
2. Which man on the board must be kept safe above all others?
3. What do the two colours of squares look like?
4. What must you do before your hand goes out to a man?


![A boy and a girl sit on the ground on either side of a big checkered board with tall pieces on one row and short ones on the other.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/physical-and-health-education/week5-the-thinking-board.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Chess is a game of ____ . A) running B) sleeping C) thinking D) singing
2. A chess board is made of squares that are ____ . A) light and dark B) all black C) all white D) red and green
3. Which man may go straight forward or sideways as far as the board is long? A) the small man B) the queen C) the jumper D) the baby
4. The game ends when one king can no longer ____ . A) sing B) sleep C) escape D) count
5. Fill in: When you touch a man you must ____ it.
6. Mention four men found on a chess board.
7. Write two sentences on what chess teaches a child to do.

**My own work**

Draw a board of sixteen squares, light and dark, with four big men standing on the top row and four small ones below.

---

### WEEK 6 — VIDEO GAMES: FUN IN LIMITS

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · PHE · Third Term W6 (pp 558–563).*

**You will learn to**

• say what a video game is
• name two games a small child may play
• say why play on a screen is limited
• tell what must come before the screen

**Things to know**

• A **video game** is a game played on an **electronic thing**: a computer, a tablet, a phone or a box joined
  to the television.
• Video games are **indoor play** — the child sits, and the fingers work.
• Games a small child may play: a **card game** where the cards are matched, a **running game** where the
  figure jumps the holes, a **puzzle** of pictures, a **counting** or **word** game, and a football game on
  the screen.
• Some games are made for **big young men**, with fighting and bad language; **a child of five does not play
  those**, and if he sees them on a screen he says to a grown-up at home.
• A screen is **fun in a little amount**; it is **not** the first thing for the day.
• **Outside play comes first**: running, jumping, throwing, and helping at home — then a short time on the
  screen, if it is allowed.
• Eyes get tired and the head becomes stiff when we sit long; that is why the time is **limited**.
• We do **not** take a screen to bed, and we do not play while food is on the table.
• If our school or house has no machine, we are not less: a rope, a ball and two lines on the ground carry the
  same joy.

**Words for my notebook**

A video game is played on a computer, a tablet or a phone, and it is indoor fun kept in a small amount.
Outside play, lessons and sleep come first, and games made for big people are not for a child of five.

**Let us talk**

1. What do we call a game played on a tablet?
2. Which comes first, the screen or play outside?
3. Why is our time on a screen kept short?
4. What do you say when a big brother shows you a fighting game?


![A tablet with a blank screen is left on a stool inside while two children run out through the open door towards a ball on the ground.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/physical-and-health-education/week6-outside-first.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A video game is played on a(n) ____ . A) wooden stool B) cooking pot C) tablet or phone D) bucket
2. Time on a screen should be kept ____ . A) for the whole day B) short C) till morning D) during class
3. Games with fighting and bad words are made for ____ . A) big people B) babies C) our class D) the dog
4. Before the screen, a child first ____ . A) plays outside and does his lesson B) sleeps C) eats sweets D) hides
5. Fill in: We do not take a screen to ____ .
6. Mention two games a small child may play on a screen.
7. Write two sentences on the outdoor game you like better than a screen game.

**My own work**

Draw a tablet lying on a table with two children running past it towards a football on the ground outside.

---

### WEEK 8 — MOVING OUR BODY PARTS

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · PHE · motor skills, moving our body parts (part 1).*

**You will learn to**

• name ten parts of the body
• move each part by itself
• stretch the arms, the legs and the back
• do the actions of a body song

**Things to know**

• The parts we name: **head, neck, shoulders, chest, back, waist, arms, hands, fingers, legs, feet and toes**.
• **Nodding** the head means yes; **shaking** it means no; both move the **neck**.
• **Shrugging** lifts the shoulders up to the ears and drops them — the way a man carries a heavy load.
• **Waving** moves the whole arm from the shoulder, with the open hand going left and right.
• **Stretching** the arms above the head makes the body long, like a plant reaching for the sun.
• Bending to touch the **toes** stretches the back and the legs; the knees stay straight, and we do not jerk.
• Turning the **waist** from side to side with the hands on the hips wakes up the middle of the body.
• Shaking out the **hands** after writing makes the fingers ready again.
• In a **body song** we do what the song says — head, shoulders, knees and toes — and we get faster each time.
• Every movement is done **slowly at first**, on a **clear space**, with **no child within reach** of your legs.

**Words for my notebook**

We name the parts of the body and move them one at a time: nodding the head, shrugging the shoulders,
waving the arms, stretching up and bending to the toes, all done slowly in a clear space.

**Let us talk**

1. Point to your waist and then to your neck.
2. What movement do we do when we want to say yes?
3. Why must nobody stand near you when you stretch your legs?
4. Sing the body song once with the actions.


![Five children in a row nod the head, shrug the shoulders, wave one hand, stretch both arms up and bend to reach the toes.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/physical-and-health-education/week8-five-movements.png)
**Worksheet**

1. When we want to say yes we ____ the head. A) nod B) hide C) wash D) paint
2. Lifting the shoulders to the ears and dropping them is called ____ . A) waving B) shrinking C) shrugging D) singing
3. Waving moves the whole ____ . A) toe B) ear C) tooth D) arm
4. To stretch the back we bend down and touch our ____ . A) toes B) cap C) bag D) book
5. Fill in: Before we stretch, we look for a clear ____ .
6. Mention six parts of the body you can name.
7. Write two sentences on the body song you did today and what you touched.

**My own work**

Draw a child with both arms stretched high above the head, feet flat on the ground, and a small friend beside her waving one hand.

---

### WEEK 9 — MOVING WELL IN EVERYDAY WORK

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · PHE · motor skills, daily activity movements.*

**You will learn to**

• pick a thing up from the floor the right way
• carry a light thing safely
• push and pull without hurting the back
• go up and down stairs well

**Things to know**

• To pick up a thing from the floor, **bend the knees** and keep the back near straight — do not fold the back.
• A thing is held **close to the body** when carrying; the further it is from you, the heavier it feels.
• Two small loads are **easier than one big one**: carry in two trips rather than fall with one.
• **Pushing** is better than pulling for a heavy box: push with the **legs and the shoulder**, not the back.
• When we **pull**, the hands come in low and the body leans **forward**, so we will not be dragged down.
• On the **stair** we go **one after another**, hold the rail, and do not look at the child below.
• Nothing is **thrown down the stair** and no child **slides the rail**.
• An **obstacle course** trains the body: walk the line, step over the low bar, crawl under the rope, jump the
  two marks, and run back to touch the last child's hand.
• In a **relay**, the stick or the bean bag is passed **in the zone**, not thrown from far.
• These same moves keep us safe at home — for a child who lifts well **spills less, drops less and aches less**.

**Words for my notebook**

We pick things up by bending the knees, hold the load close, push with the legs and lean forward when we
pull. On the stair we go one after another and hold the rail, and in a race we pass the bag in the zone.

**Let us talk**

1. Which part of you should do the work when you lift a bucket — the back or the legs?
2. Why is two small loads better than one big one?
3. Show me how you hold the rail on the stair.
4. Where do we pass the stick in a relay?


![A girl bends her knees to lift a box, a boy pushes a crate leaning with bent legs, and three children go up the steps one after another holding the rail.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/physical-and-health-education/week9-lift-push-and-stairs.png)
**Worksheet**

1. To lift a thing from the floor we bend the ____ . A) neck B) ears C) knees D) fingers
2. A heavy box is best moved by ____ it with the legs. A) pushing B) kicking C) smelling D) hiding
3. When we pull a rope we lean the body ____ . A) up B) sideways C) off D) forward
4. On the stair a child goes ____ . A) one after another B) three at a time C) with his eyes closed D) running fast
5. Fill in: A load is carried ____ to the body, not far from it.
6. Mention four things an obstacle course makes you do.
7. Write two sentences on how you carried water or firewood at home without dropping it.

**My own work**

Draw a boy bending his knees to lift a box, then a line of children going up a stair one after another with hands on the rail.


---

# Prevocational Studies (PVS)

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. Week 7 is Mid-Term Break, Week 10 Revision, Week 11 Examination,
Week 12 Closing — those weeks carry no note.
*Depth: NERDC 2025 · Nursery 3 · Prevocational Studies · Third Term — farm tools, their care and
storage, and safety on the farm and in the kitchen, from the pack's off-by-one mapping.*


### WEEK 1 — THE TOOLS WE WORK WITH, AND WHERE THEY REST

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Prevocational Studies · Third Term W1.*

**You will learn to**

• name six common farm tools
• say what each tool is used for
• tell the difference between a spade and a hoe
• say where tools rest when work is over

**Things to know**

• A **spade** digs and lifts soil; a **hoe** opens the ground and clears small weeds; a **cutlass** cuts
  grass and stick.
• A **rake** pulls soil smooth and gathers dry leaves; a **watering can** carries water to the young plant;
  a **basket** and a **wheelbarrow** carry what we have cut or dug.
• A spade has a **flat wide blade** and a long handle; a hoe has a **short flat blade** set at an angle.
• A rake has **teeth**; a spade has none — that is how you know them at a glance.
• Tools are **for work**, not for play: a cutlass and a hoe are for a grown-up's hand, and a child carries
  the small watering can or the light basket.
• After work, a tool is **cleaned of mud**, dried, and **hung on the rack** or laid on a shelf with the
  blade down.
• A tool left on the ground in the path is a **trap for the next foot**; a tool on the rack is a tool kept.
• Rust spoons an edge, so tools are kept **dry** and under a roof, not on the wet soil overnight.
• A **damaged handle** is not carried back to the rack; it is shown to a grown-up and mended first.

**Words for my notebook**

A spade digs, a hoe clears weeds, a cutlass cuts, a rake smooths, a watering can brings water, and a basket
or a wheelbarrow carries. After work every tool is cleaned, dried and hung on the rack with its blade down.

**Let us talk**

1. Which tool do we use to pull dry leaves together?
2. Show me how a hoe's blade is different from a spade's.
3. Where does a tool rest when the work is over?
4. Which two things may a child carry on the farm?


![Four tools hang on a rack above a wheelbarrow: a rake with five teeth, a short hoe, a watering can and a plaited basket. Nothing lies loose on the floor.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/prevocational-studies/week1-tools-on-the-rack.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A rake is used to ____ . A) cut grass B) pull leaves and smooth soil C) carry water D) dig a hole
2. The tool with a flat wide blade for lifting soil is a ____ . A) basket B) can C) spade D) rake
3. A cutlass and a hoe are carried by ____ . A) anybody who wants B) the smallest child C) the class monitor D) a grown-up at work
4. Where should a tool rest after work? ____ . A) in the path B) on the wet ground C) under the tree D) dry, on the rack
5. Fill in: A tool with a cracked handle is shown to a ____ before it is used.
6. Mention three tools that carry or hold things instead of cutting.
7. Write two sentences on a tool you have held and what you did with it.

**My own work**

Draw a wall with a rack on it, then draw a rake, a hoe, a watering can and a basket hanging neatly in a row.

---

### WEEK 2 — WHAT WE MEAN BY SAFETY

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Prevocational Studies · Third Term W2.*

**You will learn to**

• say what safety means
• name three rules we keep on the farm
• tell why the ground is cleared before work
• say what we do with a thing that can hurt

**Things to know**

• **Safety means keeping ourselves and other people from being hurt while we work or play.**
• Safety is not a thing we wear once; it is the **way we do everything** — walking, carrying, putting down.
• Before work begins we **look at the ground**: a stone, a stick, a hole or a peel can send a person down.
• A tool is **pointed away from people** and never swung over a head; the space behind is looked at first.
• Nothing that can cut is **thrown, pushed into the soil, or left in the grass** where a foot will find it.
• When we carry something sharp, a grown-up carries it; the child's hands hold the **watering can, the cup,
  the basket**.
• **Reporting** is part of safety: a broken thing, a spill, or a wasps' nest by the fence is told, not hidden.
• The **store and the fire** have rules too: the insect medicine stays shut and high, and never in a bottle
  that once held water to drink.
• A safe farm is a **working farm**: a cut hand keeps a family from the market for a week.

**Words for my notebook**

Safety means keeping ourselves and others from being hurt. We clear the ground before we work, we point
a tool away from people, we carry sharp things with a grown-up, and we report what is broken or dangerous.

**Let us talk**

1. What does the word safety tell you?
2. What do we look at before we start work on the ground?
3. Who carries a cutlass, and who carries the watering can?
4. Name one thing you would report to a grown-up at once.


![A boy carries a watering can with both hands and a girl follows with an empty basket. The path between them is flat and clear, with no stone and no tool lying about.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/prevocational-studies/week2-the-clear-path.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Safety means keeping people from being ____ . A) hurt B) paid C) called D) counted
2. Before work starts on the ground we ____ . A) run across it B) look for stones and holes C) water it D) sing on it
3. A tool is carried with its sharp end ____ . A) up over a head B) towards a friend C) away from people D) in the grass
4. A wasps' nest near the fence is ____ . A) kicked B) hidden C) waited for till next year D) reported to a grown-up
5. Fill in: A safe farm is a ____ farm.
6. Mention three rules we keep when tools are about.
7. Write two sentences on how you cleared a place before you played or worked.

**My own work**

Draw two children in a garden, one carrying a small watering can with both hands, with no tool or stone lying on the path between them.

---

### WEEK 3 — WHY SAFETY MATTERS SO MUCH

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Prevocational Studies · Third Term W3.*

**You will learn to**

• say three good things that come from safe work
• tell what one carelessness can cost
• name the people who are hurt by our carelessness
• say why a careful child is trusted with more

**Things to know**

• Safe work **keeps the body whole** — eyes for reading, hands for holding, legs for running.
• It **keeps the work going**: a farm with a hurt worker loses days, and a kitchen with a burnt pot loses a meal.
• It **saves money**: a cloth for a cut and medicine for a fever are dearer than the care that prevented them.
• It **keeps the family glad**: a child who comes home unhurt is the reason the day was good.
• Carelessness does not hurt only the careless one: a swung hoe can reach a smaller child standing behind.
• A careful child is **trusted with more** — first the basket, then the small rake, then bigger work.
• Being quick is good; being **quick and careless** is the slowest way to do a thing.
• Safe work is also **neat work**: things in their place, the ground cleared, the tool cleaned after use.
• We do not brag about a danger we have passed; we tell the class the rule that kept us whole.

**Words for my notebook**

Safety keeps the body whole, keeps the work going, saves money and keeps the family glad. Carelessness can
hurt the one standing behind, so a careful child is the one who is trusted with more work.

**Let us talk**

1. Name two good things that come from working safely.
2. Who else can be hurt by your carelessness?
3. What does a family lose when a worker hurts a hand?
4. Which comes first, being quick or being careful?


![Three people work the same plot with room to swing: a man with a hoe, a boy carrying tomatoes away in a basket, a woman taking water from a bucket.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/prevocational-studies/week3-room-to-move.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Safe work first of all keeps the ____ . A) body whole B) house painted C) fire bright D) dog fed
2. A farm that loses a worker to a hurt hand will ____ . A) earn more B) lose days of work C) rest and prosper D) plant twice
3. Who can be hurt by the person swinging a tool? A) nobody B) only the worker C) a child standing behind D) the birds only
4. A child who works carefully is ____ . A) sent away B) forgotten C) given the biggest hoe D) trusted with more work
5. Fill in: Being quick and careless is the ____ way to do a thing.
6. Mention four good results of safe work.
7. Write two sentences on a time you were careful and it kept you unhurt.

**My own work**

Draw a family working on a small farm plot: a grown-up with a hoe, a boy carrying a basket of tomatoes and a girl drawing water, all standing clear of one another.

---

### WEEK 4 — WHAT HAPPENS WHEN A TOOL IS MISUSED

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Prevocational Studies · Third Term W4.*

**You will learn to**

• name four dangers on a farm or in a home
• tell what a cracked handle can do
• say why a hole in the ground is marked
• tell what we do with a tool that is broken

**Things to know**

• A **cracked or loose handle** can slip off while the tool is swinging, and the blade goes where nobody
  meant it.
• A **tool left in the grass** is seen by nobody until a foot steps on it; a blade turned down in the soil is
  the same trap.
• An **open hole** — a post hole, a burrow, a washed gully — turns an ankle; the rule is to fill it or
  **mark it with stones or a stick** until it is filled.
• A **wet floor, a peel, or a stone in the path** is a fall waiting; the quick child picks it up, not jumps it.
• A **blade used for the wrong work** — a cutlass to split wood, a knife to open a tin — chips, and a chipped
  blade cuts worse and slips more.
• **Fire and hot ash** are not moved by a child; a pot handle is turned in on the stove, not out into the path.
• A broken tool is **taken up and told about**, not hidden under the rack; the mending is small, the hiding is
  not.
• First aid for a small cut: **clean water on it, a clean cloth over it, and a grown-up told** — no dust and no
  leaf pressed into the wound.

**Words for my notebook**

A cracked handle flies off, a tool in the grass waits for a foot, and a hole in the ground turns an ankle.
We mark the hole, pick up the stone, take the broken tool to be mended, and wash and cover a small cut.

**Let us talk**

1. What can a loose handle do while a man is swinging?
2. What do we do with a hole we cannot fill today?
3. Why is a chipped blade more dangerous than a good one?
4. What are the three steps for a small cut?


![A boy points at a hoe with a crack across its wooden handle, and beside them a hole in the ground is ringed with stones so that no foot falls in.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/prevocational-studies/week4-the-cracked-handle.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A tool with a cracked handle may ____ . A) lose its handle while it swings B) work faster C) keep better D) stay clean longer
2. A hole in the ground that cannot be filled today is ____ . A) left for the goats B) marked with stones C) hidden with grass D) jumped by everyone
3. A chipped blade is dangerous because it ____ . A) shines less B) is heavier C) slips and cuts worse D) costs more
4. The three steps for a small cut are clean water, a clean cloth and ____ . A) a leaf pressed in B) dust from the floor C) the finger hidden D) a grown-up told
5. Fill in: A broken tool is taken up and ____ , not hidden.
6. Mention four dangers you can find on a farm or in a kitchen.
7. Write two sentences on a danger you saw and what was done about it.

**My own work**

Draw a boy pointing at a hoe with a cracked handle lying on the ground, and beside them a small hole ringed with stones to warn the feet.

---

### WEEK 5 — THE KITCHEN, AND WHY A GROWN-UP IS NEEDED

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Prevocational Studies · Third Term W5.*

**You will learn to**

• say what a kitchen is for
• name four dangers found in a kitchen
• tell which jobs need a grown-up
• say how a child can still help in the kitchen

**Things to know**

• The **kitchen** is the room where food is kept, washed and **cooked**; it feeds the whole house.
• Its dangers are quick: **fire, hot water, a hot pot, a sharp knife, a wet floor, and a covered pot that
  spits when the lid is lifted**.
• A **hot surface does not wait** for a small hand; the pot's handle is turned **in**, so a sleeve will not
  catch it.
• Knives are **laid on a shelf or in a rack**, blade away from the edge, never in a basin of water you cannot
  see through.
• The jobs of **fire, boiling, cutting and lifting a hot pot belong to a grown-up**; a child stands back and
  carries what is cool.
• A child helps well by **washing vegetables, carrying a cool cup, setting things on the table, sweeping, and
  fetching** what is asked for.
• **A grown-up is needed** not because a child is slow but because a spill, a burn or a cut is bigger than a
  child's hand can put right.
• If a fire starts in a pot, **the lid is put on and the air taken away**; nobody carries a burning pot
  across the room.
• The floor is wiped at once; **a wet floor sends a person to the wall** — and the wall sends the pot down.

**Words for my notebook**

The kitchen cooks the food and keeps it, and it holds fire, hot water and sharp knives. Those jobs belong to
a grown-up; a child washes, carries what is cool, sweeps, and keeps the floor dry.

**Let us talk**

1. What do we do in a kitchen?
2. Name two things in a kitchen that can hurt quickly.
3. Which kitchen jobs must a child wait for a grown-up to do?
4. Name two ways you help in the kitchen today.


![A woman lifts a pot lid with a folded cloth over the fire while a girl waits back with a cool empty cup. A knife and fork rest high on a shelf, out of small hands.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/prevocational-studies/week5-the-kitchen-fire.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A kitchen is the room where food is kept, washed and ____ . A) sewn B) cooked C) sold only D) buried
2. The handle of a pot on the fire is turned ____ . A) out into the path B) up in the air C) on the floor D) in, away from the path
3. Boiling, cutting and lifting a hot pot are jobs for ____ . A) a grown-up B) the smallest child C) the visitor D) a class friend
4. A child helps in the kitchen by ____ . A) carrying the burning pot B) washing vegetables and sweeping C) playing with the lid D) running with a knife
5. Fill in: A wet floor is wiped ____ , so nobody falls.
6. Mention four dangers found in a kitchen.
7. Write two sentences on one kitchen job you do at home and why it is safe for you.

**My own work**

Draw a woman lifting the lid of a pot on a fire with a cloth in her hand, a girl bringing a cool cup to the table, and a knife lying high on a shelf.

---

### WEEK 6 — THE RULES WE KEEP WHEN TOOLS ARE ABOUT

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Prevocational Studies · Third Term W6.*

**You will learn to**

• state five rules we keep with tools
• show how a sharp tool is carried
• say why we watch the space behind us
• tell how tools are shared in a class

**Things to know**

• **Never run with a tool**; walk, and keep the blade or the teeth **pointing down**.
• **Never throw a tool** — even to a friend who is near; it is **put into the hand** and the hand is waited for.
• **Never swing a tool over anybody.** Before you swing, look behind you — the handle comes round too,
  and it can hit a person standing there.
• **One tool, one work**: a hoe for the soil, a cutlass for grass, a knife for food.
• **A tool is passed by the handle**, so the receiver's hand is where the grip goes.
• Space is kept: at least **the length of two arms** between anybody working and anybody watching.
• A tool is **not stood in the soil** where it can fall on a foot; it is laid down or hung up.
• In a class we **take turns with the watering can** and never grab it mid-pour.
• Medicine for insects, kerosene and soap are **kept shut, kept high and kept apart from drink and food**.
• A rule is only good when it is **said out loud to the one who forgot it**, kindly and before the accident.

**Words for my notebook**

We walk with a tool and point it down, we pass it by the handle, we put it into a hand instead of throwing
it, and we look behind before we swing. One tool does one work, and two arms' space is kept from anybody
watching.

**Let us talk**

1. Which way should a tool point when it is carried?
2. Why must we look behind before we swing a hoe?
3. How do we give a tool to another person?
4. What do we say when a friend forgets a rule?


![A man walks with a cutlass low at his side, point to the ground. A boy follows well behind with a hoe on his shoulder and its blade hanging down where nobody stands.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/prevocational-studies/week6-carried-point-down.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A sharp tool is carried with its blade ____ . A) pointing down B) up over a head C) on the shoulder D) in a pocket
2. To give a friend a tool, you ____ . A) throw it B) leave it in the grass C) put it into his hand D) stand it in the soil
3. The space kept between a worker and a watcher is about ____ . A) one finger B) the width of a mat C) two arms' length D) a whole field
4. A hoe is for the soil and a knife is for ____ . A) the roof B) the fence C) the drum D) food
5. Fill in: Medicine for insects is kept shut, kept ____ , and kept apart from drink.
6. Mention five rules we keep when tools are about.
7. Write two sentences on how your class shares one watering can.

**My own work**

Draw a man carrying a cutlass with its point down and a boy walking behind him with a hoe on his shoulder, blade down and well apart.

---

### WEEK 8 — CLOTHES AND THINGS THAT PROTECT THE BODY

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Prevocational Studies · Third Term W8.*

**You will learn to**

• name four things worn to keep us safe at work
• say what each one protects
• tell when they must be worn
• say how a child dresses for the farm

**Things to know**

• The things we wear to keep the body safe are called **protective gear**: **gloves, boots, a hat and an
  overall** (the big loose cloth that covers the clothes).
• **Gloves** keep the hands from thorns, blisters from a rough handle, and wet medicine off the skin.
• **Boots** keep the feet from a nail, a stone, water in the gutter, and a tool that slips.
• A **hat** keeps the sun off the head and neck, so the head does not become hot and painful.
• An **overall** keeps the school clothes free of mud and oil, and loose cloth keeps branches from a hand.
• Gear is worn **before** the work begins, not after the first scratch.
• It is **clean and whole**: a glove with a hole lets in what the glove was for.
• A child on the farm wears a **cap, sandals or shoes, and clothes that are not too long**, so the feet will
  not trip on the hem.
• Gear is **not a toy to play with**; the gloves and the hat are for work and are put back on the peg.
• Eyes are kept safe too: nobody looks at the sun, and nobody points a stick where a face is.

**Words for my notebook**

Gloves for the hands, boots for the feet, a hat for the head, and an overall for the clothes are the gear we
put on before work. The gear is clean and whole, and it goes back on its peg when the work is over.

**Let us talk**

1. Which thing keeps a nail or a stone from the foot?
2. Why must a glove have no hole in it?
3. What does a hat keep off the head?
4. What do we do with the gear when work is finished?


![Four pegs hold the things that keep the body safe: a broad hat, a pair of gloves, a loose overall and tall boots. A boy in his cap stands up to look at them.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/prevocational-studies/week8-four-pegs.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Boots are worn to keep the ____ safe. A) eyes B) head C) feet D) hands
2. Gloves are worn to keep the hands from ____ . A) thorns and blisters B) water only C) the sun D) the basket
3. The loose cloth we wear over our clothes on the farm is the ____ . A) apron B) overall C) umbrella D) school cap
4. Protective gear is put on ____ the work begins. A) long after B) when it is over C) if it rains D) before
5. Fill in: A glove with a ____ lets in what the glove was for.
6. Mention four things worn to keep us safe at work.
7. Write two sentences on what you wear on your head and feet when you go to the farm.

**My own work**

Draw a peg on a wall with a hat, a pair of gloves, an overall and a pair of boots hanging on it, and a boy standing beside them in his cap.

---

### WEEK 9 — PUTTING THINGS AWAY, TELLING WHAT IS BROKEN, AND FIRST AID

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Prevocational Studies · Third Term W9.*

**You will learn to**

• say how tools and chemicals are stored
• tell who is told about a broken thing
• give first aid for a small cut and for a burn
• say what a store place must keep out

**Things to know**

• A **store** is a dry place, off the ground, where tools, cans and baskets are kept **with the blade down**
  and the sharp edges in.
• Tools are **cleaned of mud and dried** before they go in, or the mud will keep the rust alive.
• Insect medicine, kerosene and acid are kept **shut, high, and marked**; they never go into a bottle that a
  child drinks from.
• A **broken thing is reported** at once to the grown-up in charge, and it is **not used again** until it is
  mended.
• A **small cut**: wash with clean water, press a clean cloth on it, keep the hand up; a grown-up puts a
  cover on it. Dust, leaf and ash are never rubbed into a wound.
• A **small burn**: hold the place under **cool clean water** for a while, cover loosely with a clean cloth,
  and tell a grown-up. No oil, no paste and no leaf is put on a burn.
• A **nosebleed**: sit up, lean **a little forward**, and pinch the soft part of the nose; the head is not
  thrown back.
• First aid is what we do **before** the clinic or the hospital; a deep cut, a bad burn or a fall where the leg
  will not bear weight is taken to a grown-up and to the clinic at once.
• The store and the first-aid box are kept **clean, closed and known** — everybody in the house can point to
  both of them.

**Words for my notebook**

Tools are cleaned, dried and stored with the blade down; medicine is kept shut and high; a broken thing is
reported and not used. A cut is washed and covered, a burn is cooled with clean water, and a grown-up is
told at once.

**Let us talk**

1. What is done to a tool before it is put on the rack?
2. Who is told about a broken rake?
3. What is put on a small burn first?
4. Why is a nosebleed child made to lean forward?


![A girl on tiptoe hangs a rake beside a hoe and a watering can, then carries a broken rake handle to a man with a hammer while a lidded box waits on a high shelf.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/prevocational-studies/week9-hung-up-and-reported.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Tools are stored in a place that is ____ . A) wet and open B) under the tree C) in the path D) dry and off the ground
2. Insect medicine is kept shut, high, and ____ . A) beside the drinking cups B) marked C) left on the floor D) poured in a drink bottle
3. A broken tool must be ____ . A) used carefully B) hidden C) reported and not used D) thrown in the bush
4. For a small cut we first use ____ . A) ash from the fire B) a leaf C) dust from the floor D) clean water and a clean cloth
5. Fill in: A small burn is held under ____ clean water.
6. Mention three things a store place must keep out or keep away.
7. Write two sentences on where the first-aid box is kept in your house and who knows it.

**My own work**

Draw a rack with three tools hanging on it and a box on a high shelf below a notice that is left blank; then draw a girl showing a broken rake handle to a man with a hammer.


---

# Social and Citizenship Studies (SCS)

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. Week 7 is Mid-Term Break, Week 10 Revision, Week 11 Examination,
Week 12 Closing — those weeks carry no note.
*Depth: NERDC 2025 · Nursery 3 · Social and Citizenship Studies · Third Term — our place and its weather,
the animals about us, the naira and saving, being a citizen, our community, its leaders and the wide world,
from the pack's off-by-one mapping.*


### WEEK 1 — THE PLACE WE LIVE, AND THE WEATHER THAT CHANGES

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Social and Citizenship Studies · Third Term W1.*

**You will learn to**

• keep the place where we live clean
• put waste where it belongs and never throw it about
• name the kinds of weather we know
• say what we can do when the weather changes too much

**Things to know**

• A **bin with a cover** is where paper, peels and wrappers go; the ground is not a bin.
