• A **wrapper or a tin left on the ground** is called litter; it makes the place ugly and it can cut a foot.
• When the gutter is choked with litter, rain water has no way out and the **water comes into the compound**.
• A broken fence, a deep hole or a pool of dirty water is **told to a grown-up** so that it is mended.
• Weather is what the sky does today: **sun, rain, wind, hot and cold**.
• **Climate** is the weather a place has over many years — we know rain will come in its season.
• When rain, heat and wind change from what they used to be, we call it **climate change**.
• Too much heat dries the soil and the young plants die; too much rain brings a **flood**; a long time
  without rain is a **drought**.
• Our own part is small but real: **plant a tree**, shut the tap when water is not wanted, keep our place clean.

**Words for my notebook**

We keep our place clean by putting waste in a covered bin and never on the ground, and a broken or dangerous
thing is told to a grown-up. Weather is what the sky does today — sun, rain, wind, hot or cold — and when it
changes for many years we call it climate change; a tree planted and water saved both help.

**Let us talk**

1. Where do paper, peels and wrappers go?
2. What happens when a gutter is choked with litter?
3. Name three kinds of weather you know.
4. What are two things you can do when the heat grows too much?


![A boy drops a banana peel into a bin with a lid while a girl brings crumpled paper to it; the ground is tidy and a young tree stands beside them.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/social-and-citizenship-studies/week1-the-covered-bin.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Waste like paper and peels should be put ____ . A) into a covered bin B) on the ground C) in the gutter D) under a leaf
2. A wrapper left on the ground is called ____ . A) a gift B) litter C) soil D) water
3. When the gutter is choked with litter, rain water ____ . A) goes away faster B) dries at once C) has no way out D) becomes clean
4. A broken fence at the edge of the road is best ____ . A) kicked B) hidden C) painted by a child D) told to a grown-up
5. Fill in: The weather a place has over many years is its ____ .
6. Mention three kinds of weather you know.
7. Write two sentences on two things you can do to help the place where you live.

**My own work**

Draw a boy and a girl putting banana peel and paper into a bin with a cover on it, and a young tree beside
them; colour the leaves of the tree green.

---

### WEEK 2 — THE ANIMALS THAT LIVE BY THEMSELVES

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Social and Citizenship Studies · Third Term W2.*

**You will learn to**

• say what wildlife means
• name animals that live in our forests and grasslands
• tell where each of them lives
• say why we do not harm them

**Things to know**

• **Wildlife** are the animals and birds that live in nature by themselves, in the bush, by the river and on
  the grassland.
• Animals we find in Nigeria are the **monkey, antelope, bushbird, lizard, snake and butterfly**.
• A monkey lives in the **trees of the forest**; an antelope feeds on the **grassland**; a snake keeps to the
  long grass and the holes in the ground.
• Fish and frogs live in or beside the **river**, and a butterfly goes from flower to flower for the drink in
  the flower.
• Animals are **part of nature**: the bird eats the insect that would spoil our farm, and the bee helps a
  flower become a fruit.
• Some animals are **nearly gone** — that is called endangered — because their homes are cut down or they are
  killed for food.
• **Conservation** means keeping nature and its animals safe, so that a child yet to come will still see a
  monkey in a tree.
• We do not **hurt them or pull down their homes**: no burning the bush, no cutting every tree, no stone
  thrown at a bird.
• A **zoo** is a place where animals are kept and fed so that people may learn about them; there we look
  quietly and we do not throw anything in.

**Words for my notebook**

Wildlife are animals that live in nature by themselves — a monkey in the tree, an antelope on the grassland,
a fish in the river. Conservation means keeping them and their homes safe: we do not burn the bush, cut
every tree or stone a bird.

**Let us talk**

1. What does wildlife mean?
2. Where does a monkey live, and where does an antelope feed?
3. Name two animals you have seen with your own eyes.
4. Why should we not burn the bush?


![A monkey sits on a branch, a bird flies above, a lizard rests on a stone and a butterfly waits by a flower. Nothing in the forest is hurt.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/social-and-citizenship-studies/week2-animals-living-free.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Animals that live in nature by themselves are called ____ . A) pets B) wildlife C) crops D) tools
2. An antelope feeds on the ____ . A) grassland B) sea C) roof D) market
3. A monkey lives in ____ . A) a hole in the path B) the long grass C) the trees of the forest D) the river
4. Keeping nature and its animals safe is called ____ . A) littering B) digging C) burning D) conservation
5. Fill in: Some animals are nearly gone because their ____ are cut down.
6. Mention three animals we find in Nigeria.
7. Write two sentences on one animal you like and the place it lives in.

**My own work**

Draw one forest home: a monkey on a branch, a bird in the air, a lizard on a stone and a butterfly by a
flower; colour the leaves and do not hurt anything in the picture.

---

### WEEK 3 — THE MONEY WE USE IN NIGERIA

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Social and Citizenship Studies · Third Term W3.*

**You will learn to**

• say what money is used for
• name the money of Nigeria
• tell a coin from a note
• say why money has value

**Things to know**

• **Money** is the thing we use to buy what we need: food, cloth, a pencil and a bus ride.
• The money of **Nigeria is the naira**, and its mark is the letter **N with two strokes** through it.
• Round money made of metal is a **coin**; the ₦1 and the ₦2 coins are still carried in a pocket.
• Money made of paper is a **note**: ₦5, ₦10, ₦20, ₦50, ₦100, ₦200, ₦500 and ₦1000.
• A note carries a **number, a face and a shining strip**, and one note is not the same colour as another.
• Money has **value**: a ₦100 note will buy more than a ₦5 note buys.
• Money is **got by work** — the grown-up who works is paid money for the work done.
• A note is not folded into a plane and not torn: it is kept **clean, flat and shut** in a purse or a box.

**Words for my notebook**

The money of Nigeria is the naira. Round metal money is a coin and paper money is a note; money is got by
work, it has value, and it is kept clean, flat and shut.

**Let us talk**

1. What do we use money for?
2. What is the name of the money of Nigeria?
3. Show me with your hands how a coin is different from a note.
4. Why is a note not for folding into a plane?


![One clean folded note, three round coins standing in a row and an open empty purse lie on the table. The note carries no writing at all.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/social-and-citizenship-studies/week3-the-note-and-the-coins.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The money of Nigeria is called the ____ . A) pesewa B) cedi C) naira D) kobo
2. Round money made of metal is a ____ . A) note B) coin C) card D) cup
3. Money made of paper is called a ____ . A) coin B) cup C) plate D) note
4. People are paid money because they ____ . A) sleep B) work C) play D) wait
5. Fill in: A ₦100 note will buy ____ than a ₦5 note buys.
6. Mention four things money is used to buy.
7. Write two sentences on the way a note should be kept.

**My own work**

Draw one folded note and three round coins lying on a table beside an empty purse, then colour the note green
and the coins gold.

---

### WEEK 4 — SPENDING WISELY AND SAVING A PART

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Social and Citizenship Studies · Third Term W4.*

**You will learn to**

• name what money buys for a family
• tell a need from a want
• say where money for later is kept
• say how we behave with money that is not ours

**Things to know**

• Money buys **food, cloth and shoes**; it pays for **school**, for the **bus**, and now and then for a toy.
• A **need** is a thing we must have — garri, oil, a uniform. A **want** is a thing that would be nice — a
  second toy.
• Buying what we need **first** is spending wisely; when the money is finished there is nothing left for food.
• **Saving** is keeping part of the money for later use — a piggy bank, a box with a slit, or the bank.
• Money saved little by little grows: **two coins today and two coins tomorrow** make a heavier box.
• Money that is not ours is **never taken** — not from a bag, a drawer, a table or a friend's pocket.
• If we find money on the floor we **give it to the person who lost it**, or take it to the class teacher.
• Being **honest with money** is worth more than any toy bought with money that is not ours.
• We **thank** the grown-up who buys for us and we do not cry for everything we see in the shop.
• In the market we **count what we are given** and say thank you; a buyer and a seller are both doing a work.

**Words for my notebook**

Money buys what we need first — food, cloth, school and the bus — and a part of what is left is saved in a
box or a bank. Money that is not ours is never taken, and we thank the grown-up who buys for us.

**Let us talk**

1. Name three things money buys for a family.
2. What is the difference between a need and a want?
3. Where can money kept for later be put?
4. What do you do if you find money on the floor?


![A woman sits behind a heap of oranges laid on a cloth and takes one coin from a boy's hand while his empty basket waits on the ground.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/social-and-citizenship-studies/week4-one-coin-at-the-market.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A thing we must have, like garri or a uniform, is a ____ . A) want B) need C) toy D) gift
2. Keeping part of the money for later use is ____ . A) saving B) losing C) hiding D) spending
3. Money that is not ours is ____ . A) put in a pocket B) spent at once C) never taken D) shared with friends
4. In the market, money given to us is ____ . A) thrown B) counted C) buried D) torn
5. Fill in: A box used for keeping money for later is a ____ bank.
6. Mention four things money is used for in a family.
7. Write two sentences on what you do when something is bought for you.

**My own work**

Draw a market scene: a woman sitting behind a heap of oranges, a boy handing her one coin, and an empty
basket on the ground beside them.

---

### WEEK 5 — BEING A GOOD CITIZEN

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Social and Citizenship Studies · Third Term W5.*

**You will learn to**

• say what a citizen is
• name the rights of a child
• name the things we must do for others
• say how a good citizen behaves every day

**Things to know**

• A **citizen** is a person who belongs to a country; every child born in **Nigeria is a citizen of Nigeria**.
• A good citizen is a **good person in the place where they live**: they greet, they keep the rules, they help.
• Our **rights** are these: to learn, to play, to be safe, to be treated fairly, and to be fed and cared for.
• Our **responsibilities** are the other half: to be kind, to help others, to keep the rules and to respect
  everybody.
• A right is mine and it is my friend's too, so I do not push to the front of the line.
• **Fairness** means a girl and a boy, a small child and a big one, a Yoruba child and a Hausa child all get
  the same turn.
• When a thing is wrong — a child being bullied, a tap left running — we **speak up** and tell a grown-up.
• We take care of the community: the bin is used, the bench is kept clean and the classroom is swept.
• Being **proud to be Nigerian** means knowing the colours of the flag, greeting in the tongue of our people
  and singing our song well.
• A good choice is a small thing done many times: sharing a pencil, saying sorry, holding a door.

**Words for my notebook**

A citizen is a person who belongs to a country, and we are all citizens of Nigeria. A good child has rights —
to learn, to play, to be safe, to be treated fairly — and duties too: to be kind, to help, to keep the rules
and to respect everybody.

**Let us talk**

1. What is a citizen?
2. Name three rights of a child.
3. What is the other half that comes with a right?
4. When is the right time to speak up?

**Worksheet**

1. A person who belongs to a country is a ____ . A) visitor B) citizen C) baby D) leader
2. One right of a child is the right to ____ . A) stay home alone B) take a friend's pen C) shout in class D) learn and play
3. Treating a girl and a boy the same is being ____ . A) fair B) slow C) quiet D) afraid
4. When a small child is being bullied we ____ . A) look away B) laugh C) speak up and tell a grown-up D) join in
5. Fill in: Every child born in Nigeria is a ____ of Nigeria.
6. Mention four things a good citizen does in the place where they live.
7. Write two sentences on one good choice you made today.

**My own work**

Draw two children sitting on one bench with one book between them, and a flag on a pole behind them; the flag
has three tall strips of colour and nothing is written on it.

---

### WEEK 6 — OUR COMMUNITY: THE TOWN AND THE VILLAGE

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Social and Citizenship Studies · Third Term W6.*

**You will learn to**

• say what a community is
• tell a town from a village
• name things a village sends to a town
• say what a community values

**Things to know**

• A **community** is people living and working together in one place: the houses, the shops, the school, the
  market and the stream.
• Our community is **part of who we are** — where we are from is known by our tongue and by our food.
• A **town** is a busy place: many buildings, many cars, big markets and street lights.
• A **village** is a quiet place: farms around the houses, animals going about, and nature close by.
• The two are **different but connected** — the village sends food to the town, and the town has the big
  hospital, the good road and the larger school.
• When a flood comes, the town sends cloth and food to the village and the village sends baskets of yam
  back; **communities help one another**.
• The values of a good community are **sharing, helping and working together** — a meeting, a road swept by
  all, a neighbour taken to the clinic.
• Our own part: greet an elder, keep the path clear, help with a heavy load, and do not fight over a piece of
  land.
• No community is better than another; one is **busy** and one is **quiet**, and both are needed.

**Words for my notebook**

A community is people living and working together in one place. A town is busy with many buildings and a
village is quiet with farms near the houses, and both need each other: food comes from the village and the big
school and hospital are in the town.

**Let us talk**

1. What is a community?
2. Name two things a town has that a village has few of.
3. What does a village send to the town?
4. Mention two ways people in a community help one another.


![A busy town of tall houses, two stalls and a small bus stands on one side, a quiet village with two round houses, a farm and a well on the other, and one road joins them.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/social-and-citizenship-studies/week6-town-and-village.png)
**Worksheet**

1. People living and working together in one place are a ____ . A) class B) market C) community D) family
2. A busy place with many buildings and markets is a ____ . A) village B) town C) farm D) bush
3. A place with farms around the houses and quiet roads is a ____ . A) town B) city C) village D) port
4. A village sends ____ to the town. A) food B) street lights C) tall buildings D) buses
5. Fill in: A good community shares, helps and works ____ .
6. Mention three values of a good community.
7. Write two sentences on one special thing about the place where you live.

**My own work**

Draw one page with a town on the left — three houses close together, a market stall and a bus — and a village
on the right with two houses and a farm, then draw the road that joins the two.

---

### WEEK 8 — THE LEADERS IN OUR COMMUNITY

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Social and Citizenship Studies · Third Term W8.*

**You will learn to**

• say what a leader does
• name four kinds of leader we know
• say the things a good leader is
• name the ways a child may lead

**Things to know**

• A **leader** is a person who guides people and **serves** them, not one who only shouts orders.
• The qualities of a good leader are these: **honest, kind, fair, brave, helpful and a good listener**.
• **Traditional leaders** in our land are the **Oba**, the **Emir**, a **chief** and a **village head**.
• **Religious leaders** teach people the way of their faith: a **pastor** or an **imam**.
• **Community leaders** keep order in a place: a **Baale**, a ward head or the **chairman** of the local
  government.
• **School leaders** are the **head teacher**, the **class captain** and the **prefects**, and **family
  leaders** are a father, a mother and a grandparent.
• We **respect and support** our leaders by listening to them, keeping their rules and greeting them well.
• Leaders serve the community by settling quarrels, calling the people to work on the road and looking after
  the school.
• A child can lead in small ways: a **line leader**, giving out the books, saying the prayer, or helping two
  friends make peace.
• A child's leadership skills are **speaking clearly, listening to others, being fair** and helping to settle
  a quarrel.

**Words for my notebook**

A leader guides people and serves them. We have traditional leaders like an Oba or an Emir, religious leaders
like a pastor or an imam, community leaders like a Baale or a chairman, school leaders like a head teacher and
a class captain, and family leaders at home.

**Let us talk**

1. What does a leader do besides give orders?
2. Name two traditional leaders in our land.
3. Who are the leaders of your school?
4. What are two ways a child can lead in the class?


![A class captain stands at the front with one hand raised while four children sit and listen and the teacher puts a hand to her ear to hear her well.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/social-and-citizenship-studies/week8-the-class-captain.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A leader is a person who ____ people. A) guides and serves B) frightens C) counts D) follows
2. A traditional leader in our land is a ____ . A) driver B) chief C) seller D) pupil
3. The head of a school is the ____ teacher. A) sports B) junior C) head D) music
4. Which of these is a quality of a good leader? ____ . A) Cheating B) Laziness C) Anger D) Being a good listener
5. Fill in: A good leader is ____ — he never takes what is not his.
6. Mention four kinds of leader in a community.
7. Write two sentences on one thing you lead in your class.

**My own work**

Draw a class captain standing in front of the class with one hand raised, four children seated on benches in
front of her, and a teacher standing at the side, listening.

---

### WEEK 9 — THE BIG WORLD WE ARE ALL IN

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Social and Citizenship Studies · Third Term W9.*

**You will learn to**

• say what we learn about in this subject
• name the tongues and foods of our own land
• say why we learn about other countries
• name two ways we show that we are part of the world

**Things to know**

• We learn about **people, places and how they live** — our family, our community, our country and the world.
• Learning about others helps us **know ourselves better** and stops us laughing at what is different.
• Nigeria has **many cultures and many tongues**: Yoruba, Igbo, Hausa, Tiv, Kanuri, Efik and about four
  hundred more.
• Our foods are many too: **amala and efo, garri, tuwo, pounded yam and pepper soup**, and each people has
  their own.
• A greeting is different as well — "ẹ káàárọ̀", "good morning", "sannu" and "eto" all say respect; say your
  own one well.
• Nigeria is one country in a **world of many countries**, and other children go to school, work at home and
  play, just as we do.
• We are all part of the world: the rain that falls here, the air we breathe and the sea are **shared** with
  people far away.
• We **value what is like ours and what is not**: a friend from another place may eat a different food and
  still be a friend.
• Asking a question and then looking for the answer — in a book, from an elder, by watching — is how we learn
  the world.

**Words for my notebook**

We learn about people and places so that we can live well with them. Nigeria has many cultures and many
tongues, and it is one country in a world of many countries where children go to school and play as we do.

**Let us talk**

1. What do we learn about in this subject?
2. Name three tongues spoken in Nigeria.
3. How do you greet an elder in your own tongue?
4. What do children in other countries do, just as you do?


![Four children in four different kinds of dress stand hand in hand around a big round world that carries three shapes of land and no writing at all.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/social-and-citizenship-studies/week9-the-round-world.png)
**Worksheet**

1. In this subject we learn about people, places and ____ . A) how they live B) the price of yam C) the way to count D) birds only
2. Our country has ____ tongues and cultures. A) two B) one C) many D) no
3. Amala and efo, garri and pounded yam are ____ of our people. A) tools B) games C) songs D) foods
4. A child in another country ____ . A) never plays B) goes to school and plays as we do C) has no tongue D) does not eat
5. Fill in: A child from another place may eat a different food and still be my ____ .
6. Mention three things that are different between people of various places.
7. Write two sentences on one thing you want to learn about the world.

**My own work**

Draw a big round ball of the world with three shapes of land on it and no writing anywhere, then draw four
children holding hands around it, each in different clothes; colour the land green and the sea blue.

# Yoruba

Weeks 1 to 6, 8, 9 and 11. No note for Week 7 (Mid-Term Break) or Week 10 (Revision);
**Week 11 carries a note** because the school's own table prints it as a teaching row
("Exam Preparation"), not as an examination week. Week 12 is Closing.
*Depth: this subject is school-generated — there is no NERDC Yoruba scheme to floor it against. The
extent of each week is the three strands the school itself prints (Ẹ̀dẹ̀ · Àṣà · Lítíréṣọ̀). The master
corpus prints Yoruba without tone marks; tone marks are added in the body of the note so that the
child reads the word correctly, and the topic line is left exactly as the school prints it. Where the
school names a song or a poem by its first words only, the note keeps that one line and teaches the
form, the answer and the meaning — it does not invent the rest.*


### WEEK 1 — Ede: Sise nnkan (ase oluko) / Asa: Itesiwaju ikini ni orisirisi akoko / Litireso: Orin ere idaraya

*Depth: school-generated — three strands as printed in the master corpus for N2 T3 W1.*

**You will learn to**

• do what you hear in Yoruba and say the action back
• greet at the right time of the day and answer a greeting
• sing a play song, move with it and stop when it stops

**Things to know — Ẹ̀dẹ̀ (ṣíe nǹkan — the actions the teacher names)**

• A word that tells us to do a thing is a **command**: **ẹ dú!** (stand), **ẹ jó!** (sit down), **ẹ rìn!** (walk).
• More commands we use in class every day: **ẹ ka** (read), **ẹ kọ́** (write), **ẹ wo** (look), **ẹ gbọ́** (listen), **ẹ dáhùn** (reply).
• To be polite we put **jọ̀wọ́** in the middle: **"Ẹ jọ̀wọ́, ẹ dú."** — stand, please.
• Saying **ẹ** before the word is how we speak to a teacher or to many people; **má ṣe** stops a thing: **"Ẹ má ṣe sọ̀rọ̀."**
• Each action has its own body: **fọ́** — wash, **ṣí** — open, **tì** — shut, **fi sí** — put on, **yọ** — take off.
• **Ẹ wò ó, ẹ sì tún un ka** — look at the line on the board and then read it again.
• Game: an action is called out and we do it; when **"Dúró!"** (stop!) is called, nobody moves.
• We answer in a whole line, not one word: **"Mo ń kà á."** (I am reading it), **"Mo ń kọ́ ọ́."** (I am writing it).
• New words go in the notebook with the action drawn small beside each — a hand, a book, a foot.

**Things to know — Àṣà (ìtẹ̀síwájú ìkíni — greetings at their times)**

• Morning: **"Ẹ káàárọ̀."** — the answer is **"Ẹ ṣe àárọ̀ rere."**
• Afternoon: **"Ẹ ká ọ̀sán."** — the answer is **"Ẹ ṣe ọ̀sán rere."**
• Evening: **"Ẹ káàlẹ́."** — the elder answers **"Ọ́ àáṣẹ̀."**
• To ask how people are: **"Báwo ni?"** — the answer is **"Àà báwo ni."** (it is well).
• For a thing done for us we say **"Ẹ ṣé."**; the answer is **"À ń ṣé."**
• A boy greets an elder by lying full length (**ìdòlẹ̀kọ̀**); a girl kneels (**ìkúnbálẹ̀**); both rise when they are told.
• We greet the first person we meet in the morning, and we greet all of them in turn — nobody is skipped.
• Time changes, the greeting changes; the respect stays the same.

**Things to know — Lítíréṣọ̀ (orin eré ìdárayá — the play song)**

• A play song is not sung sitting down: it carries the step, the clap and the turn.
• The leader sings a line; the class **answers the same line back** — that is how a play song is built.
• Our rule this week: **while the song goes on we move; when the song stops, we stand still.**
• We sing in a ring, and the child in the middle picks the next line.
• The song teaches the ears: a child who can answer the line can answer the teacher too.

**Words for my notebook**

Ẹ dú · ẹ jó · ẹ ka · ẹ kọ́ · ẹ wo · ẹ gbọ́. Ẹ káàárọ̀ · ẹ ká ọ̀sán · ẹ káàlẹ́ · ọ́ àáṣẹ̀ · báwo ni — àà báwo ni · ẹ ṣé.

**Let us talk**

1. What do I say when I want the whole class to sit down?
2. Add the polite word to "ẹ dú".
3. Which greeting belongs to the evening, and what does the elder answer?
4. How does a girl greet an elder, and how does a boy greet?

**Worksheet**

1. "Ẹ jó!" tells the class to ____ . A) stand B) sit down C) read D) wash
2. The polite word we put inside a command is ____ . A) ṣé B) jọ̀wọ́ C) àáṣẹ̀ D) báwo
3. "Ẹ ká ọ̀sán" is the greeting for ____ . A) afternoon B) morning C) night D) Monday
4. When the elder is greeted in the evening, he answers ____ . A) ẹ ṣé B) báwo ni C) ọ́ àáṣẹ̀ D) àà báwo ni
5. Fill in: The answer to "Báwo ni?" is "Àà ____ ni."
6. Say four actions the teacher names in Yoruba, and do them.
7. Sing one line of the play song and answer it back as the class does.

**My own work**

Write the six actions in your notebook and draw a small hand, book or foot beside each; then colour the one
you like best.

---

### WEEK 2 — Ede: Siso oro onileta po (F+K+F) / Asa: Orisirisi eso ile wa / Litireso: Alo apamo ati alo apagbe

*Depth: school-generated — three strands as printed in the master corpus for N2 T3 W2.*

**You will learn to**

• say a Yoruba word sound by sound and then say it at once
• name the fruits our land carries and where they come from
• tell an àlọ́ àpamọ́ from an àlọ́ àpàgbé

**Things to know — Ẹ̀dẹ̀ (sọ̀rọ̀ onílétà pọ̀ — joining the letters)**

• A Yoruba syllable is short and clean: **à**, **kà**, **rà** — one vowel with one consonant, and the tone rides on it.
• The school writes the drill **fawẹ̀lì + kọ́nsonantì + fawẹ̀lì**: a vowel, a consonant, a vowel — **ọ + kọ́ = ọkọ́**.
• Say it slowly first, then at once: **à** (pause) **kà** (pause) **rà** (pause) — **àkàrà**, bread.
• More words we join: **bà** + **tà = bàtà** (shoes) · **ò** + **gò** + **gò = ògò́gò** (a pot) · **ẹ̀** + **kẹ́ = ẹ̀kẹ́** (beads).
• The dots change the word: **ọ** is not **o**, **ẹ** is not **e**; **àkàrà** without its dots is not the word.
• Tone is part of the letter: **ọkọ́** (husband) and **òkọ̀** (hoe) are written with the same shapes and say two things.
• We count the sounds on our fingers: **à-kà-rá** is three sounds, three fingers up.
• Every day this week, one new word is joined on the board before we read it.

**Things to know — Àṣà (oríṣiríṣi ẹsọ́ ilé wa — the fruits of our land)**

• **ọ̀gẹ̀dẹ̀** — banana · **áńbà** — mango · **ọ̀pẹ̀** — palm fruit · **bílíbilí** — watermelon · **àmùtì** — pineapple.
• Ọ̀gẹ̀dẹ̀ and áńbà come from a tree in the compound; ọ̀pẹ̀ comes from the palm and bílíbilí from the ground.
• A fruit is ripe when it gives us its own sign: the banana turns yellow, the mango softens, the watermelon knocks hollow.
• The palm fruit is boiled and pressed and it becomes the red oil our soup is made with.
• When a child picks a fruit from a neighbour's tree, he asks first; a Yoruba home shares what it has and gives the first of the season to an elder.
• Fruits wash before they are eaten, and the peel goes into the bin, not on the path.

**Things to know — Lítíréṣọ̀ (àlọ́ àpamọ́ àti àlọ́ àpàgbé)**

• An àlọ́ begins with a call and an answer: **"Àlọ́ o!"** — **"Àpamọ́!"**
• **Àlọ́ àpamọ́** hides a lesson inside it; when it ends, the hearer can say what he learnt.
• **Àlọ́ àpàgbé** is the light one: it is told to make us laugh and to carry the evening away (gbàgbé is to forget).
• At an àlọ́ we sit, we listen, and we do not finish another person's story with a lie.
• The teller may stop in the middle and ask the children a question; the one who answers goes on with the story.

**Words for my notebook**

à-kà-rá = àkàrà · bà-tà = bàtà · ò-gò-gò = ògò́gò · ọ-kọ́ = ọkọ́. Ọ̀gẹ̀dẹ̀, áńbà, ọ̀pẹ̀, bílíbilí, àmùtì.
Àlọ́ o — Àpamọ́!

**Let us talk**

1. Join the three sounds of à-kà-rá and say the word.
2. Which two fruits in our list come from a tree?
3. What do we say to answer "Àlọ́ o!"?
4. Tell me one thing an àlọ́ àpamọ́ does that àlọ́ àpàgbé does not.

**Worksheet**

1. "à-kà-rá" joined together says ____ . A) kàrà B) àkàrà C) ráà D) àrákà
2. The word "bàtà" is made of ____ sounds. A) one B) three C) four D) two
3. "ọ̀pẹ̀" is the ____ fruit. A) mango B) banana C) palm D) watermelon
4. When somebody says "Àlọ́ o!", we answer ____ . A) Àpamọ́! B) Ẹ ṣé! C) Báwo ni! D) Àà ṣé!
5. Fill in: The dots under **ẹ** and **ọ** change the ____ of the word.
6. Name five fruits of our land and say where one of them comes from.
7. Say two lines about an àlọ́ you have heard and what it taught.

**My own work**

Write five joined words, and under each one draw the thing it names; colour the fruit you would like to eat.

---

### WEEK 3 — Ede: Isorongbesi laarin oluko ati akekoo / Asa: Imototo: ara fifo, enu fifo / Litireso: Orin kiko

*Depth: school-generated — three strands as printed in the master corpus for N2 T3 W3.*

**You will learn to**

• ask the teacher a question and answer one in a full line
• keep the body and the mouth clean at the two set times
• sing a working song with the leader and the answer

**Things to know — Ẹ̀dẹ̀ (íṣọ̀rọ̀ńbẹ̀sí — teacher and pupil talking)**

• The pupil opens with respect: **"Báwo ni, olùkọ́?"** — the teacher: **"Ó dára, ọmọ mi."**
• To ask for a thing: **"Ẹ jọ̀wọ́, fi ìwé sí mi."** — to be given: **"Ẹ ṣé, olùkọ́."**
• When a question is asked and you know, say **"Mi ò, mi ò"** (me, me) with the hand up, not a shout.
• Answer in a line, not one word: **"Kí ni ẹ̀ ní?"** — **"Mo ní àkọ̀wé àti pencil."**
• When you did not hear, say **"Ẹ jọ̀wọ́, tún un sọ"** (say it again) — that is not rudeness.
• Asking about a thing: **"Ṣé èyí jẹ́ ti mi?"** (is this mine?) · Answer: **"Bẹ́ẹ̀ni"** (yes) or **"Rárá"** (no).
• At the end of the talk both say **"À ń ṣé."**
• When **"Ẹ sí i"** (go on) is said, the next child speaks and the class listens to the end.

**Things to know — Àṣà (ìmọ́tótó: ara fọ́, ẹnu fọ́)**

• The body is washed twice a day — **òwúrọ̀** before school and **ìṣẹ́** (evening) before rest.
• Hands wash with soap before food and after the toilet; the nails are cut short, not bitten.
• The mouth is washed in the morning and at night; that is when **ẹnu fọ́** is done, with the brush or the twig.
• Cloth for school is clean and pressed; a cloth worn yesterday for play is not today's cloth.
• Feet wash and dry before the sandals go on; a wet shoe spoils and the foot itches.
• Food is covered, the compound is swept, and the water in the bucket is for washing, not for drinking.

**Things to know — Lítíréṣọ̀ (orin kíkó — the carrying song)**

• A carrying song is sung when hands are busy: it keeps the step of the load and the step of the feet together.
• One person **leads the line**, everybody else **carries the answer**: "ọ̀wọ́ ọ̀wọ́ — ẹẹ!"
• The song does not hurry the back; it gives the back a rhythm to rest on.
• When the load comes down, the song ends with the last line — it does not just stop.

**Words for my notebook**

Báwo ni, olùkọ́? — Ó dára, ọmọ mi. Ẹ jọ̀wọ́, tún un sọ. Bẹ́ẹ̀ni · rárá · À ń ṣé. Ara ń fọ́ òwúrọ̀ àti ìṣẹ́; ẹnu ń fọ́ ní méjèèjì.

**Let us talk**

1. How do you greet your teacher in the morning?
2. What do you say when a word was too fast for you?
3. At what two times is the body washed?
4. Who leads a carrying song, and who answers?

**Worksheet**

1. "Báwo ni, olùkọ́?" is said by ____ . A) the pupil B) the guard C) a baby D) the cook
2. When we did not hear a word, we say ____ . A) ẹ ṣé B) rárá C) tún un sọ D) mi ò
3. "Bẹ́ẹ̀ni" means ____ . A) maybe B) no C) please D) yes
4. The mouth is washed ____ a day. A) twice B) never C) once in a month D) only on Friday
5. Fill in: Before food and after the toilet, the ____ wash with soap.
6. Say four things a clean child does every day.
7. Sing one line of a carrying song and let the class answer it.

**My own work**

Draw a cup, a brush and a bar of soap in three boxes on one page, and write one Yoruba word under each box.

---

### WEEK 4 — Ede: Onka 21-40 (aropo ati ayokuro) / Asa: Ojo inu ose (itan ojo kookan) / Litireso: Ewi kika

*Depth: school-generated — three strands as printed in the master corpus for N2 T3 W4.*

**You will learn to**

• count from twenty-one to forty in Yoruba
• name the seven days and say the meaning one day carries
• read a poem aloud with the voice up and the pause in

**Things to know — Ẹ̀dẹ̀ (oníka 21–40 — àròpọ̀ àti àyọkúrò)**

• Twenty is **ogún**; from there we keep adding with **àti**: **ogún àti ọ̀kan** (21), **ogún àti èjì** (22), **ogún àti ẹ̀ta** (23).
• Thirty has its own name: **ọ̀rúnmọ́lẹ́dógún** — and it is ogún àti ẹ́wàá (20 + 10).
• Forty is **ogótà**; fifty is **ọ̀ọ́dúnrún** — the two big steps we say without counting up.
• Àròpọ̀ (adding): **ogún àti ọ̀kan** plus **ọ̀kan** = **ogún àti èjì** (21 + 1 = 22).
• Àyọkúrò (taking away): **ogún àti ẹ̀ta** less **ọ̀kan** = **ogún àti èjì** (23 − 1 = 22).
• We count with the fingers and with the pebbles: ten pebbles make one line on the board.
• Say the ladder out loud: 21, 22, 23 … 40; then come back down from 40 to 21 without falling.
• The marks of the number are written small in the notebook: **ogún àti ẹ̀rin = 24**.

**Things to know — Àṣà (ọjọ́ inú ọ̀ṣẹ́ — the story of each day)**

• **Ọjọ́ Àìkú** — Sunday: the day of thanks and rest; "àìkú" is not-dying, so we sing and we visit.
• **Ọjọ́ Ayé** — Monday: the day the world opens; market, farm and school all begin again.
• **Ọjọ́ Ìṣẹ́gun** — Tuesday: the day of victory; the work that began on Monday is pushed through.
• **Ọjọ́rú** — Wednesday: the day in the middle; the week is half gone and we do not drop.
• **Ọjọ́bọ̀** — Thursday: the day of the cow and the big market; things are bought for the weekend.
• **Ọjọ́ Ẹtì** — Friday: the last edge of the working week; Muslims worship on this day.
• **Ọjọ́ Àbámẹ́ta** — Saturday: the day we meet again — compound swept, hair combed, play allowed.
• Seven days make one ọ̀ṣẹ́; the week has no day we sleep through.

**Things to know — Lítíréṣọ̀ (èwì kíkà — reading a poem out)**

• A poem is not read like a tale: it has room inside it for the breath.
• One line, one breath; a comma is a stop, a full stop is a rest.
• The voice goes **up** at a question and **down** at the end of a line.
• We read the line once slowly, once at the right speed, and once from memory.
• A hard word is not skipped: the class says it alone, then puts it back in the line.

**Words for my notebook**

Ogún àti ọ̀kan (21) · ogún àti èjì (22) · ogún àti ẹ̀ta (23) · ọ̀rúnmọ́lẹ́dógún (30) · ogótà (40).
Ọjọ́ Àìkú, Ayé, Ìṣẹ́gun, Ọjọ́rú, Ọjọ́bọ̀, Ẹtì, Àbámẹ́ta.

**Let us talk**

1. Say 21, 22 and 23 in Yoruba.
2. Which day is ọ̀rúnmọ́lẹ́dógún built from two numbers?
3. Name the day the market is big and the day of thanks.
4. What do we do with a hard word in a poem?

**Worksheet**

1. "Ogún àti ẹ̀ta" is the number ____ . A) 23 B) 30 C) 13 D) 32
2. Forty in Yoruba is ____ . A) ọ̀ọ́dúnrún B) ogótà C) ogún D) ẹ́wàá
3. The first day of the week, the day of thanks, is ____ . A) Ọjọ́bọ̀ B) Ọjọ́ Ayé C) Ọjọ́ Àìkú D) Ọjọ́rú
4. Ọjọ́ Àbámẹ́ta is ____ . A) Wednesday B) Friday C) Sunday D) Saturday
5. Fill in: Ogún àti ọ̀kan plus ọ̀kan is ogún àti ____ .
6. Write the seven days of the week in order.
7. Read one line of this week's poem twice: once slowly, once at the right speed.

**My own work**

Write 21 to 30 in figures and in Yoruba on one folded sheet, then cut the sheet into ten cards and shuffle them for the class to read.

---

### WEEK 5 — Ede: Awon awo olorisirisi / Asa: Iwa rere ati iwa ti ko to / Litireso: Ewi: Ise ni oogun ise

*Depth: school-generated — three strands as printed in the master corpus for N2 T3 W5.*

**You will learn to**

• name the colours of things around us in Yoruba
• tell an ìwà rere from an ìwà tí kò dára
• say the poem of work with its meaning

**Things to know — Ẹ̀dẹ̀ (àwọ̀ olóríṣiríṣi — the many colours)**

• **funfun** — white · **dúdú** — black · **púpà** — red · **àwọ̀ ọ̀sàn** — orange · **àwọ̀ ewé** — green · **àwọ̀ òfurufú** — blue.
• The colour of the leaf is **àwọ̀ ewé** because it is the colour of ẹwẹ̀; the colour of the sky is **àwọ̀ òfurufú**.
• **àwọ̀ ilẹ̀** is brown, the colour of the road after rain, and **àwọ̀ omi** is the pale colour of water in a calabash.
• Ask and answer: **"Kí ni àwọ̀ bọ́ọ̀lù yìí?"** — **"Púpà ni."** (it is red).
• A uniform has its colour, a cap has its own; we say both words: **"àwọ̀ ọ̀sàn àti funfun."**
• Things may share a colour and not a name: the milk and the paper are both **funfun**.
• **àwọ̀ wúnì** is purple, and **fàdákà** is the pale shine of a new coin.
• The colour comes after the thing: **bọ́ọ̀lù púpà** (a red ball), **ìwé funfun** (a white book).

**Things to know — Àṣà (ìwà rere àti ìwà tí kò dára)**

• **Ìwà rere** is good character: **ọ̀wọ̀** (respect), **ìrẹ̀lẹ̀** (humility), **òtítọ́** (truth), **ìpín** (sharing), **kíkó nǹkan ṣe ní títò** (doing a thing well).
• **Ìwà tí kò dára** is the bad kind: **èké** (a lie), **ìjìgbe** (taking what is not ours), **ìpaniyaníyàn** (making a child afraid), **àìmọ́tótó** (dirtiness) and **sísọ̀rọ̀ àyò** (tale-bearing).
• An **ọmọlúàbí** is the child the whole street trusts: he greets, he tells the truth, he leaves a place better than he found it.
• A good act done once is a start; done every day, it becomes ìwà — the character the elders name.
• When we do wrong, we say **"Ì jẹ́ mi, ẹ̀ṣẹ́ mi ni"** (it is my fault) and put the thing right.

**Things to know — Lítíréṣọ̀ (èwì: ìṣẹ́ ni òògùn ìṣẹ́)**

• The line the school prints is: **"Iṣẹ́ ni òògùn ìẹ́, tí kò bá ṣiṣẹ́, òògùn wo ló ń lò ó?"**
• It says: work is the medicine for shame; a child who will not work has no medicine left.
• The first half answers the question, and the second half asks it back — that is how èwì moves.
• We say it with the hand on the chest, once slowly and twice at speed, and then we say what it means.

**Words for my notebook**

Funfun · dúdú · púpà · àwọ̀ ọ̀sàn · àwọ̀ ewé · àwọ̀ òfurufú · àwọ̀ ilẹ̀. Ìwà rere: ọ̀wọ̀, òtítọ́, ìrẹ̀lẹ̀, ìpín. Iṣẹ́ ni òògùn ìẹ́.

**Let us talk**

1. What colour is the sky in Yoruba?
2. Name three things of the colour funfun.
3. Which word is the opposite of òtítọ́?
4. What does the poem say is the medicine for shame?

**Worksheet**

1. The Yoruba for red is ____ . A) funfun B) dúdú C) púpà D) àwọ̀ ewé
2. "àwọ̀ ewé" is the colour of ____ . A) a leaf B) the sky C) milk D) a stone
3. Which of these is ìwà rere? A) èké B) òtítọ́ C) ìjìgbe D) ìpaniyaníyàn
4. "Iṣẹ́ ni òògùn ìẹ́" teaches the value of ____ . A) sleeping B) play C) work D) wealth
5. Fill in: A child the whole street trusts is called ọmọ____ .
6. Name six colours in Yoruba and say the colour of your school bag.
7. Say the poem line and write what it means in one English sentence.

**My own work**

Colour one leaf green, one ball red, one cup white and one sky blue, and write the Yoruba colour word on each.

---

### WEEK 6 — Ede: Apeko (gbo, ko sile) / Asa: Ojuse ebi: egbon ati aburo / Litireso: Orin iwa (Kini n o fi ole se)

*Depth: school-generated — three strands as printed in the master corpus for N2 T3 W6.*

**You will learn to**

• listen once, hear the word, and write it down with its marks
• do the duty of an ẹ̀gbọ́n and the duty of an àbúrò
• answer the character song's question in a full line

**Things to know — Ẹ̀dẹ̀ (Apeko: gbọ́, kọ́ sílẹ̀ — hear it, write it)**

• Sit straight, hold the pencil, and keep both hands on the book — that is how the ear opens.
• The word is said **twice**: the first time we hear it, the second time we catch its marks.
• We write the dots and the tones as we hear them: **ẹ̀kẹ́**, not "eke".
• Nobody looks at a friend's book; a wrong word written is a word we will fix together after.
• When all have written, the class reads the line back and the board is checked.
• Words this week: **àkàrà · bàtà · ìwé · ọkọ́ · ògò́gò · ẹ̀kẹ́** — six words, six sounds.
• A wrong word is not rubbed hard: cross it with one clean line and write it again.
• The last thing is reading our own line back; a missing mark is added before the book goes up.

**Things to know — Àṣà (ojúṣe ẹbí: ẹ̀gbọ́n àti àbúrò)**

• **Ẹ̀gbọ́n** is the elder sibling; his duty is to lead by the hand, to share what he has, and to be the example.
• An ẹ̀gbọ́n does not raise his hand on a smaller child; he carries the load and calls the younger one to wash.
• **Àbúrò** is the younger; her duty is to listen, to follow, and to ask before she takes.
• A younger child does not hide an elder's book, and an elder does not eat the younger's share.
• When the parents are away, the ẹ̀gbọ́n keeps the house quiet and the àbúrò keeps the compound clean.
• The two are one hand: one cannot comb the other's hair alone, and both greet the same elder at the same time.

**Things to know — Lítíréṣọ̀ (orin ìwà: "Kí ni n ń fi ọ̀lẹ̀ ṣe?")**

• The song the school prints is called by its question: **"Kí ni n ń fi ọ̀lẹ̀ ṣe?"** — what do we do with a thief?
• A character song asks the class something and waits: the answer is the song's other half.
• Our answers this week: we do not beat him, we ask him why, we take the thing back, and we tell a grown-up.
• Sing the question; answer it in a line; the class that answers loudest sings the next question.

**Words for my notebook**

Ẹ̀gbọ́n ń dá àpẹẹrẹ, ó sì ń pín; àbúrò ń gbọ́, ó sì ń béèrè. Orin ìwà ń béèrè pé: Kí ni n ń fi ọ̀lẹ̀ ṣe?

**Let us talk**

1. How many times is a dictation word said, and for what?
2. What are two duties of an ẹ̀gbọ́n?
3. What does an àbúrò do before she takes a thing?
4. What does our class answer to the song's question?

**Worksheet**

1. In a dictation, the word is said ____ . A) twice B) never C) five times D) only once for the whole year
2. The elder sibling is called ____ . A) àbúrò B) ẹ̀gbọ́n C) ọmọ ìyá D) àgbàlagbà
3. Before a younger child takes a thing, she ____ . A) runs B) hides it C) asks D) sells it
4. "Kí ni n ń fi ọ̀lẹ̀ ṣe?" is a question from ____ . A) a market song B) a counting card C) a number line D) a character song
5. Fill in: We write the dots and the ____ as we hear them.
6. Name four duties shared by ẹ̀gbọ́n and àbúrò in one house.
7. Sing the song's question and write the class's answer in one line.

**My own work**

Write the six dictation words with their marks, then draw two hands holding one basket between them.

---

### WEEK 8 — Ede: Akosoro (ka, pe iro, fa koko yo) / Asa: Oge sise ni ile Yoruba / Litireso: Orin kiko (Ori ewe ma paya)

*Depth: school-generated — three strands as printed in the master corpus for N2 T3 W8.*

**You will learn to**

• read a hard line slowly, then say it fast without breaking
• tell the steps of cassava work in a Yoruba home
• sing the carrying song and answer its line

**Things to know — Ẹ̀dẹ̀ (akọ̀sọ̀rọ̀: kà, sọ ọ́ yarayara, fa kọ́kọ́ yọ)**

• An **akọ̀sọ̀rọ̀** is a line that is hard on the mouth; we practise it until the mouth is free.
• Step one: **kà á pẹ́lẹ́** — read it slowly, one word at a time, with the eyes on the board.
• Step two: **sọ ọ́ yarayara** — say it fast three times, and do not bend the tones.
• **Fa kọ́kọ́ yọ** is to pull out the knot: find the one word that trips you and say only that word ten times.
• Our lines this week: **"Ẹyin ẹyẹ kan, ẹyin ẹyẹ méjì."** · **"Ọ̀wọ́ọ̀n mẹ́ta, ẹran mẹ́ta."** · **"Àkàrà mẹ́fà, ọ̀gẹ̀dẹ̀ mẹ́rin."**
• Nobody laughs at a mouth that is learning; we clap the one who tries again.
• In **ẹyin ẹyẹ** the first two sounds are alike, and the mouth must not slide them into one.
• A hard name is said by the whole class slowly once before anybody says it fast.

**Things to know — Àṣà (ọ̀gẹ̀ ṣíṣe ní ilé Yorùbá — cassava work at home)**

• **Ọ̀gẹ̀** is cassava, and the work of it is the work of the whole house.
• **A ń wa ọ̀gẹ̀** — we dig it out of the ground, and the long root is cut from the stem.
• **A bo ọ̀gẹ̀, a fọ́ ọ** — it is peeled and washed until the white inside shows.
• **A ge é gege** — it is grated, put into a bag, and **a fún un** — pressed, so that the water goes out.
• The wet flour is **ṣíṣẹ́** (sieved) and then **ìfọ̀** (fried) in a dry pot; that is **gari**.
• From the same ọ̀gẹ̀ we get **ẹ̀kọ́**, **àmàlà** from the dried chips, and **ẹ̀bà** from gari — nothing of it is thrown away.
• The work needs the big hands of a grown-up at the fire and at the press; a child carries the basin and counts the cups.

**Things to know — Lítíréṣọ̀ (orin kíkó: "Ori ewe ma paya")**

• The school prints this carrying song by its first line only — **Orí ẹwẹ̀, má ṣe payà** — so we learn that line and its answer.
• The leader carries the line; the class carries the answer after him, the way a load is shared by two.
• The song keeps the feet: one step to one word, and the load does not shake.
• When we do not know the rest of a song, we do not stop — we sing the line we know twice.

**Words for my notebook**

Ẹyin ẹyẹ kan, ẹyin ẹyẹ méjì. Ọ̀gẹ̀: a wa á, a bo ó, a fọ́ ọ, a ge é gege, a fún un, a ṣe gari. Orí ẹwẹ̀, má ṣe payà.

**Let us talk**

1. What are the two speeds of an akọ̀sọ̀rọ̀?
2. Say the cassava work in order, from the ground to the pot.
3. Who leads a carrying song and who answers?
4. What do we do when we do not know the rest of a song?

**Worksheet**

1. "Fa kọ́kọ́ yọ" means ____ . A) eat the food B) sweep the yard C) pull out the knot D) fetch the water
2. The first thing done to ọ̀gẹ̀ in the house is ____ . A) frying B) peeling and washing C) selling D) drying in rain
3. Gari is made by frying the ____ flour. A) sieved B) burnt C) frozen D) sweetened
4. A carrying song is answered by ____ . A) the dog B) one child alone C) the class after the leader D) the drum only
5. Fill in: Before the pot goes on the fire, the wet flour is pressed so that the ____ goes out.
6. Say five steps of cassava work in order.
7. Say one akọ̀sọ̀rọ̀ line slowly, then fast, and write what was hard for your mouth.

**My own work**

Draw five small boxes and put one cassava step in each box — dug, peeled, washed, grated, fried — then number the boxes 1 to 5.

---

### WEEK 9 — Ede: Didaruko eya ara / Asa: Oba alade ni ile Yoruba / Litireso: Aroso lori eye

*Depth: school-generated — three strands as printed in the master corpus for N2 T3 W9.*

**You will learn to**

• name the parts of the body in Yoruba and point to them
• say who the Oba is and how people greet him
• make a short àròsọ line about a bird

**Things to know — Ẹ̀dẹ̀ (dídárúkọ̀ ẹ̀yà ara — the parts of the body)**

• **orí** — head · **ojú** — eye · **etí** — ear · **imú** — nose · **ẹnu** — mouth · **ahọ́n** — tongue.
• **ọwọ́** — hand · **ìka** — finger · **ẹsẹ̀** — leg · **àṣọ̀ ẹsẹ̀** — the shoe · **ọ̀rùn** — neck · **ẹ̀yìn** — back · **ikùn** — stomach.
• The teeth are **ehín**, the hair is **ìrìn**, and the ear's inside keeps the sound — **eti** hears.
• Game: **"Ẹ fi ọwọ́ kan ..."** (put the hand on ...) — orí, ọ̀rùn, ikùn, ẹsẹ̀ — fast, and nobody looks at a friend.
• Two eyes see, two ears hear, two hands work; one mouth, so we speak less than we hear.
• A body part that hurts is **told** the same day: "Orí mi ń ṣà àìlẹ̀" (my head pains me).
• **ejín** is the knee and **ìkún ọwọ́** is the elbow; the two of them bend so that we can sit and reach.
• Most of them come in pairs — ojú méjì, etí méjì, ọwọ́ méjì — and one orí, ẹnu kan.

**Things to know — Àṣà (Òba aláde ní ilẹ̀ Yorùbá — the crowned king)**

• The **Òba** is the ruler of a Yoruba town, and **àdé** is the beaded crown he wears.
• The crown's beads hang before his face, so that the king is seen by all and looks on all with patience.
• Under the Òba are his **chiefs**, and over a village is the **Baale**.
• Great titles among the Yoruba: **Ọọni** (Ìlé-Ifẹ̀), **Aláàfin** (Ọ̀yọ́), **Àwújàlẹ̀** (Ìjẹ̀bú), **Olúbadan** (Ìbàdàn).
• A man greets the Òba by lying down (**ìdòlẹ̀kọ̀**), a woman by kneeling (**ìkúnbálẹ̀**); the words are **"Kábíyèsí o."**
• The Òba does not run a market or settle a small quarrel; he keeps the town together and is the father of it.
• A child's part is small: when the procession passes, we stand, we do not shout, and we follow with the eyes.

**Things to know — Lítíréṣọ̀ (àròsọ lórí ẹyẹ — a riddle line on a bird)**

• An **àròsọ** is a short line said fast, with a thing hidden in it; the class guesses.
• It begins with a call — **"Àròsọ o!"** — and the answer is **"Jẹ́ kó o!"** (let it come).
• Our line about a bird goes by what the bird does, not by its name: **"Ẹyẹ tí ń fò, tí kò ṣẹ̀ṣẹ̀ wọ ilẹ̀ — ẹnu ńlá, ẹsẹ̀ kékeré."**
• Then we say what the riddle means, and the class makes up a line of its own about a bird they know.

**Words for my notebook**

Orí · ojú · etí · imú · ẹnu · ahọ́n · ọwọ́ · ìka · ẹsẹ̀ · ọ̀rùn · ẹ̀yìn · ikùn. Òba ń ṣàdé; a ń kí i: Kábíyèsí o.

**Let us talk**

1. Point to three parts of the body and say them in Yoruba.
2. What is the crown of the Òba called, and what hangs before his face?
3. How does a woman greet the Òba?
4. What do we say to answer "Àròsọ o!"?

**Worksheet**

1. "Ahọ́n" is the ____ . A) ear B) nose C) tongue D) neck
2. The beaded crown of the Òba is the ____ . A) àdé B) ìka C) ẹ̀kẹ́ D) asọ̀
3. The head of a village, under the Òba, is the ____ . A) Ọọni B) Olówọ́ C) Aláàfin D) Baale
4. "Ẹ fi ọwọ́ kan ẹsẹ̀" tells you to touch the ____ . A) foot B) back C) head D) hand
5. Fill in: The riddle call "Àròsọ o!" is answered "Jẹ́ kó o!" — a short line with a thing ____ in it.
6. Name eight parts of the body in Yoruba.
7. Make one àròsọ line about a bird and write what it means.

**My own work**

Draw a boy from the top of the head to the feet, and write the Yoruba name beside the head, the hand, the foot and the mouth.

### WEEK 11 — Exam Preparation

*Depth: school-generated revision row; it revises Weeks 1–9 of this term. Week 12 is Closing.*

**You will learn to**

• say everything of this term out loud without looking at the book
• write the Yoruba words with their dots and their tones
• answer in a full Yoruba line, not one word

**What to revise — Ẹ̀dẹ̀**

• the actions: dú, jó, rìn, ka, kọ́, wo, gbọ́, dáhùn, fọ́, ṣí, tì — and the polite **jọ̀wọ́**
• joining the letters: **à-kà-rá = àkàrà**, **ọ-kọ́ = ọkọ́**, and why the dots and tones matter
• the teacher-and-pupil lines: **Báwo ni? · Àà báwo ni · Tún un sọ · Bẹ́ẹ̀ni · Rárá · À ń ṣé**
• counting **ogún àti ọ̀kan (21)** to **ogótà (40)**, àròpọ̀ and àyọkúrò in the same ladder
• **àwọ̀**: funfun, dúdú, púpà, àwọ̀ ọ̀sàn, àwọ̀ ewé, àwọ̀ òfurufú, àwọ̀ ilẹ̀
• dictation: sit, hear it twice, write the marks, read it back
• **ẹ̀yà ara**: orí, ojú, etí, imú, ẹnu, ahọ́n, ọwọ́, ìka, ẹsẹ̀, ọ̀rùn, ẹ̀yìn, ikùn
• the six dictation words — **àkàrà · bàtà · ìwé · ọkọ́ · ògò́gò · ẹ̀kẹ́** — written with their marks

**What to revise — Àṣà**

• the greetings and their times: **káàárọ̀ · ká ọ̀sán · káàlẹ́ · ọ́ àáṣẹ̀**, and ìdòlẹ̀kọ̀ / ìkúnbálẹ̀
• the fruits of the land and where each one comes from
• **ìmọ́tótó** at the two times of day, and the hands before food
• the seven days of the week and the meaning of four of them
• ìwà rere and ìwà tí kò dára, and the word **ọmọlúàbí**
• the duties of **ẹ̀gbọ́n** and **àbúrò**; the steps of **ọ̀gẹ̀ ṣíe**; who the **Òba aláde** is

**What to revise — Lítíréṣọ̀**

• the play song's rule: we move while it goes, we stand when it stops
• **àlọ́ àpamọ́** and **àlọ́ àpàgbé**, and the call **"Àlọ́ o! — Àpamọ́!"**
• the carrying song, the leader and the answer; **Orí ẹwẹ̀, má ṣe payà**
• the poem **Iṣẹ́ ni òògùn ìẹ́** — its two halves and what it says about work
• **orin ìwà** and its question: **Kí ni n ń fi ọ̀lẹ̀ ṣe?**
• **akọ̀sọ̀rọ̀** at two speeds, and how to begin an **àròsọ** line

**Words for my notebook**

Mo mọ àwọn ìṣe, ìkíni, oníka 21–40, àwọ̀, ẹ̀yà ara, ìwà rere àti àlọ́. Mo lè kọ ọ́, mo sì lè sọ ọ́ ní ọ̀rọ̀ tí ó kún.

**Let us talk**

1. Say the eleven actions of the term and do three of them.
2. Count from 21 to 40, then back to 21.
3. Greet me at the three times of the day and answer yourself.
4. Say the poem line, then say what it means in one sentence.

**Worksheet**

1. "Ẹ tì ìlèkùn" tells you to ____ the door. A) shut B) open C) wash D) knock
2. The number 30 in Yoruba is ____ . A) ogótà B) ogún C) ọ̀rúnmọ́lẹ́dógún D) ọ̀ọ́dúnrún
3. The colour of the sky is ____ . A) àwọ̀ ewé B) púpà C) funfun D) àwọ̀ òfurufú
4. A king of Ọ̀yọ́ is called ____ . A) Olúbadan B) Aláàfin C) Baale D) Àwújàlẹ̀
5. Fill in: The answer to "Àlọ́ o!" is "____!"
6. Write five fruits, six colours and four parts of the body in Yoruba.
7. Say two lines you will not forget in the examination and write what each one teaches.

**My own work**

Fold a sheet into six boxes. Write one thing of this term in each box — the actions, the numbers 21–40, the colours, the days, the body parts and one song line. Read the box you know best to the class.

# Mathematics & English

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. Week 7 is Mid-Term Break, Week 10 Revision, Week 11 Examination,
Week 12 Closing — those weeks carry no note.
**Stream: LITERACY — LANGUAGE DOMAIN.**
*Depth: NERDC 2025 · Nursery 3 · Literacy (Language Domain) · Third Term — the sound patterns reviewed,
naming words, doing words, the two put together, longer sentences, reading on our own, writing a story
of ours and then the whole of it in one page, from the pack's off-by-one mapping.*

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. Week 7 is Mid-Term Break, Week 10 Revision, Week 11 Examination,
Week 12 Closing — those weeks carry no note.
**Stream: LITERACY — LETTER WORK.**
*Depth: NERDC 2025 · Nursery 3 · Literacy (Letter Work) · Third Term — sight words and sentences, five-
letter words, compound words, story reading, describing words, action words, writing our own story and
the marks that end a sentence, from the pack's off-by-one mapping.*

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. Week 7 is Mid-Term Break, Week 10 Revision, Week 11 Examination,
Week 12 Closing — those weeks carry no note.

## LITERACY — LANGUAGE DOMAIN

### WEEK 1 — THE PATTERNS WE ALREADY KNOW: BLENDS, DIGRAPHS, THREE-LETTER SOUNDS

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Language Domain) · Third Term W1.*

**You will learn to**

• say a word that begins with two consonants run together
• read words where two letters make one sound
• read words with three letters held to one sound
• use the three new sight words in a line of our own

**Things to know**

• A **blend** is two consonants that keep their own sounds and still run together: **bl**ue, **fl**ag,
  **cl**ap, **gr**een, **pl**ant, **tr**ip, **sk**ip, **st**amp, **sn**ail, **sw**im.
• A **digraph** is two letters that make **one** sound between them: **sh**op, **ch**air, **th**ree,
  **wh**en, **ph**one, and the **ck** at the end of clock and duck.
• A **trigraph** is three letters held to one sound: the **tch** in catch and match, the **air** in hair and
  chair, the **are** in share and square, the **ure** in pure.
• Say them slowly and then at once: **sh… irt** — **shirt**; **c… l… o… c… k** — **clock**;
  **h… air** — **hair**.
• Our three sight words for this week are **shirt**, **clock** and **hair**; each one hides a pattern we
  know — the **sh** in shirt, the **cl** and **ck** in clock, the **air** in hair.
• Read the lines with your finger on them: **"The child in the red shirt looks at the clock."** —
  **"She has long hair and a new brush."**
• Fluency is the same words read again, only smoother: once with the finger, once with the eyes.
• Word hunt in the class: a thing with **sh**, a thing with **ck**, a thing with **air** — say it and put it in
  the box on the board.
• Do not guess a word from its first letter; sound the whole of it and then say it.

**Words for my notebook**

A blend keeps both sounds: fl, gr, st, sn. A digraph is two letters, one sound: sh, ch, th, wh, ph, ck. A
trigraph is three letters, one sound: tch, air, are, ure. Our words this week: shirt, clock, hair.

**Let us talk**

1. Which sound do the two letters at the end of "clock" make?
2. Name a thing in this room that begins with a blend.
3. Say the three-letter sound in "hair" and find it in one more word.
4. Read the line about the red shirt, once slow and once smooth.


![Three boxes hold our three words: a shirt on a hanger, a clock with twelve marks and no numbers, and a comb beside a girl with one long plait.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-language/week1-shirt-clock-hair.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Two consonants that keep their own sounds and run together are called a ____ . A) digraph B) sight word C) blend D) full stop
2. The letters **sh** in "shirt" make ____ . A) one sound B) two sounds apart C) no sound D) a long line
3. The three-letter sound in "catch" is ____ . A) cat B) tch C) atc D) ach
4. Which word has the **air** sound in it? ____ . A) clock B) shirt C) dress D) chair
5. Fill in: In "clock" the two letters at the end are the digraph ____.
6. Say six words that begin with a blend of two consonants.
7. Read the two lines about the shirt and the clock out loud, then write one line of your own with either word.

**My own work**

Draw a shirt on a hanger, a clock with twelve marks and no numbers, and a comb beside a girl's long hair — one
thing in each of three boxes.

---

### WEEK 2 — NAMING WORDS: THE WORDS THAT CALL THINGS BY NAME

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Language Domain) · Third Term W2.*

**You will learn to**

• tell a naming word (a noun) from a word that is not one
• name people, places, things and animals as nouns
• put a big letter on a proper name
• say one thing for many, and more than one thing

**Things to know**

• A **noun** is a **naming word** — it is the word we call a person, a place, a thing or an animal by.
• People: **boy, girl, teacher, man, woman**. Places: **school, home, park, shop**. Things: **book, pen,
  car, chair**. Animals: **dog, cat, bird, fish**.
• A **proper noun** is the name of one single person or place — **Ada**, **Tunde**, **Lagos**,
  **Friday** — and it always begins with a **big letter**.
• Test a word by putting "the" before it: **the book**, **the dog**, **the school** work; "the run" does
  not, because run is not a naming word.
• One **singular** noun is one thing; more than one is **plural**, and we usually add **s**: cat / cats,
  book / books, pen / pens.
• Some plurals change a little and we do not add s to them: one child, two **children**; one foot, two
  **feet**; one tooth, three **teeth**.
• Our three sight words are **old**, **young** and **should**; old and young are each other's opposite, and we
  put them before a naming word: **an old man**, **a young boy**.
• **Should** tells us a thing is right to do: **"You should greet your teacher."**
• Sort a page of words into two hoops: naming words in one, all the others in the other.

**Words for my notebook**

A noun names a person, a place, a thing or an animal. A name like Ada or Lagos is a proper noun and begins with
a big letter. One cat, two cats; one child, two children. Old and young are opposites, and should tells us what
is right.

**Let us talk**

1. Name four things in this room and say what kind of noun each one is.
2. Which words in this list are proper nouns: friday, Lagos, book, Ada?
3. Say the plural of child, and of foot.
4. Make a line with "old" and one with "young" before a naming word.


![Four boxes, four kinds of naming word: a person standing, a school with its plain flag, a wooden chair, and a goat with horns and a short tail.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-language/week2-four-kinds-of-noun.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A noun is a word that ____ . A) names a thing B) tells a size C) ends a line D) shows time
2. Which of these is a proper noun? A) school B) river C) Bola D) chair
3. "One child, two ____" . A) childs B) children C) childes D) childrens
4. The opposite of "an old man" is ____ . A) a young boy B) a tall tree C) a big house D) a new book
5. Fill in: A naming word that begins with a big letter is called a ____ noun.
6. Sort these into people, places, things and animals: teacher, park, pen, goat, woman, shop.
7. Write two lines, one with the word "should" and one with the word "young".

**My own work**

Draw four boxes and put in them one person, one place, one thing and one animal, then write the naming word
under each box.

---

### WEEK 3 — DOING WORDS: THE WORDS THAT MOVE THE LINE

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Language Domain) · Third Term W3.*

**You will learn to**

• find the doing word (the verb) in a line
• show a doing word with the body
• say the doing word again when one person is spoken of
• ask with "What" and answer in a whole line

**Things to know**

• A **verb** is a **doing word**: it tells what somebody or something does — **run, jump, walk, sit, eat,
  drink, sleep, play, read, write, sing, dance**.
• More we add today: **swim, fly, hop, skip, catch, throw, kick, clap**.
• Find it by asking **what is happening**: in "The baby sleeps", the doing word is **sleeps**.
• Say the word and **do** it: the class becomes the picture of the word, and the body remembers what the ear
  heard.
• When we speak of one person — he, she, Ada, the dog — the doing word usually takes an **s**: **I run**, but
  **she runs**; **they play**, but **he plays**.
• **What** is our asking word: **"What do you do at play?"** — and we answer in a line, not one word:
  **"I jump and I clap."**
• **Upon** is the old-time word for **on**: "the book is upon the table" says the same thing.
• **Little** tells a size before the naming word: **a little cat**, **a little bird**.
• Write three lines about your own day, each carrying one doing word.

**Words for my notebook**

A verb is a doing word: run, jump, eat, sing, read, write, hop, skip, catch, clap. We say "I run" but "she
runs". What is the asking word, little tells a small size, and upon means on.

**Let us talk**

1. What is the doing word in "The dog barks"?
2. Show us "hop" and "skip" with your body — are they the same?
3. Why does "run" become "runs" when we say "she"?
4. Ask the class a line that begins with "What" and let one child answer fully.


![Four children, four doing words: one hops on a foot, one skips with a rope, one sings with a note in the air, and one dances with an arm up.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-language/week3-four-doing-words.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A verb tells us what somebody ____ . A) does B) owns C) looks like D) is called
2. Which of these is a doing word? A) green B) table C) clap D) small
3. We say "I sing" but "she ____" . A) sings B) sing C) singing D) singed
4. "Upon" means much the same as ____ . A) under B) upon the old C) on D) off
5. Fill in: The asking word that begins "____ do you do?" is What.
6. Name eight doing words you can show with your body.
7. Write three lines about today, each with one doing word in it.

**My own work**

Draw four children in four boxes doing four different things, then write the doing word for each under its box.

---

### WEEK 4 — A NAMING WORD AND A DOING WORD MAKE A SENTENCE

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Language Domain) · Third Term W4.*

**You will learn to**

• say the two parts every sentence must have
• put the right doing word with the right naming word
• tell who the line is about and what it does
• build a line that carries three kinds of word at once

**Things to know**

• A sentence needs **two** things: who or what it is about (the naming word) and what happens
  (the doing word).
• **The dog runs.** — the dog is who, runs is what. **The girl jumps.** — same shape.
• A line with only one of the two is not finished: "The happy child" is not a sentence, but "The happy child
  **laughs**" is.
• Pairing matters: a **bird flies**, a **fish swims**, a **teacher teaches**, a **farmer farms**, a
  **driver drives**.
• Find the two parts in any line: ask **who?** then ask **what did they do?**
• Our three sight words are **yellow**, **there** and **your**: yellow is a colour word, there shows a place,
  your tells that a thing belongs to somebody.
• Put all the kinds together: **"Your yellow bird flies."** — that line has your, yellow, bird and flies.
• Do not mix **there** with **their**: there shows a place, their says a thing belongs to somebody.
• Read your built sentence aloud and count the words on your fingers; a sentence may be two words:
  **"Dogs run."**

**Words for my notebook**

Every sentence has a naming word and a doing word: the dog runs, the girl jumps. A bird flies, a fish swims, a
teacher teaches. Our words: yellow is a colour, there is a place, your means it belongs to you.

**Let us talk**

1. What two parts must a line have before it is a sentence?
2. Say the doing word that belongs to a bird, and the one that belongs to a teacher.
3. Read "Your yellow bird flies" and point out the colour word and the naming word.
4. Make a two-word sentence and then make it longer.


![A bird flies in the first box, a fish swims under three waves in the middle, and a goat stands on the bank with tufts of grass at its feet.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-language/week4-the-bird-the-fish-the-goat.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A sentence must have a naming word and a ____ word. A) doing B) colour C) long D) plural
2. Which doing word goes with "bird"? A) swims B) flies C) teaches D) drives
3. "____ yellow sun hat" — the word that shows it belongs to you is ____ . A) There B) Your C) They D) Then
4. Which of these is NOT yet a sentence? A) Dogs run. B) The happy child. C) The girl jumps. D) Fish swim.
5. Fill in: "There" is a word about a ____, not about who owns a thing.
6. Match five naming words to the doing word that suits them.
7. Write one line that carries a colour word, a naming word and a doing word.

**My own work**

Draw a bird in the air, a fish in water and a goat on the bank, and let one line of words go under each
picture.

---

### WEEK 5 — LONGER LINES: WHO DOES WHAT, AND WHY

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Language Domain) · Third Term W7.*

**You will learn to**

• build the line who-does-what-to-whom
• add a describing word and a second thing to a line
• join two small lines with "and" and give a reason with "because"
• let a small word stand in for people already named

**Things to know**

• Grow the sentence to **naming + doing + naming**: **The boy kicks the ball** — who, does what, to what.
• Add one describing word and the line gets a picture in it: **The big boy kicks the red ball.**
• Two lines can be joined with **and**: **I run and I jump.** — each half could stand alone.
• **Because** carries the reason: **I sleep because I am tired**; **She smiles because she won.**
• Ask and answer about the line: **"What does the cat do?"** — **"The cat sleeps."**
• **Them** is the small word we use instead of the people we already named: "I see the boys" becomes
  **"I see them."**
• Our three sight words are **because**, **them** and **put**; put is a doing word: **"Put the book on the
  shelf."**
• Word order is not free in English: **The dog bites the man** and **The man bites the dog** are both correct
  lines with different meanings, and only one of them is true.
• Check your line with the marks we learnt: big letter first, room between the words, full stop at the end, a
  question mark if it asks.
• A long line is only two short lines that learned to hold hands; say the short one first, then add to it.

**Words for my notebook**

A line can grow: the boy kicks the ball; the big boy kicks the red ball. And joins two lines, because gives the
reason, and them stands in for people we already named. Put is a doing word.

**Let us talk**

1. Make the line "The girl eats" into one with three parts.
2. Which word carries a reason — and or because?
3. Change "I see the goats" so that one small word stands for the goats.
4. Say one line about yourself with "because" in it.


![A boy puts a ball in the basket, a girl lifts a book onto the shelf, and two children sit down under a tree because their legs are tired.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-language/week5-put-things-away.png)
**Worksheet**

1. "The boy kicks the ball" has a naming word, a doing word and ____ . A) another naming word B) no other word C) only a colour D) a plural mark
2. The word that brings a reason is ____ . A) and B) because C) there D) them
3. "I see the boys" can be said as "I see ____" . A) there B) put C) them D) they're
4. Which line is joined with "and"? A) I run and I jump. B) I run fast. C) Why do I run? D) I run now.
5. Fill in: "____ the cup on the table" — the doing word we need is Put.
6. Grow this line twice: "The dog barks." — add a describing word, then add a second thing.
7. Write two lines about your evening, one with "because" in it.

**My own work**

Draw three small scenes — a boy putting balls in a basket, a girl putting a book on a shelf, two children
sitting because they are tired — and write one line under each.

---

### WEEK 6 — READING ON OUR OWN, AND SAYING WHAT WE HEARD

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 2 · Literacy (Language Domain) · Third Term W8.*

**You will learn to**

• read a page that carries the patterns we learnt
• answer the five questions a reader is asked
• find the naming words and the doing words inside a story
• say what will happen next before the page is turned

**Things to know**

• Our page for this week: **"Sam has a small boat. He puts the boat in the water. The wind blows and the boat
  goes fast. Ada claps her hands and runs to the shore."**
• Five questions open a story: **Who?** (Sam and Ada) **What?** (a boat on the water) **Where?**
  (at the shore) **When?** (a windy day) **Why?** (the wind blew).
• Read with the eyes, not the finger, and stop at the full stop so the listener can breathe.
• The **main character** is the person the page is mostly about; the **events** are the things that happen in
  order.
• Pick out the naming words in the first line, then the doing words; a page is built from nothing else.
• The sight words we learnt sit in every page: **has, in, and, to, he, she, there, what, should** — find
  them before you sound them.
• Guess ahead: cover the last line and say what you think Ada will do; a reader who predicts is a reader who
  is thinking.
• Tie it to yourself: **"When the wind blew my boat away, I ran after it."**
• Retell the page in your own words afterwards — your line may be shorter than the book's, and that is right.
• Long pages get easier the way a long walk does: a little further each day, read a page more than yesterday.

**Words for my notebook**

We ask who, what, where, when and why about any page. The main character is who the page is mostly about, and
the events are what happens in order. I can guess the next line before I read it.

**Let us talk**

1. Who is our page mostly about, and what does he have?
2. Where does the story happen, and when?
3. Find two doing words in the second line.
4. What do you think happens after the last line?


![Our boat story: a boy sets the boat in the water, the wind lines push it fast over the waves, and a girl claps for it at the shore.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-language/week6-the-boat-in-three-boxes.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The person a story is mostly about is its ____ character. A) main B) small C) last D) quiet
2. Which question asks for the reason? A) Who? B) When? C) Why? D) Where?
3. In "The wind blows", the doing word is ____ . A) wind B) blows C) the D) air
4. Before we turn the page we can ____ what will happen. A) guess B) forget C) erase D) count
5. Fill in: The things that happen in a story, in order, are its ____.
6. Read the page about the boat twice — once slowly, once smooth — and say which time was better.
7. Write two lines about a time the wind took something from you.

**My own work**

Draw our boat page in three boxes — the boat in the water, the wind blowing it fast, Ada clapping at the shore
— and write one line under each box.

---

### WEEK 8 — WRITING A STORY OF OUR OWN, AND THE WORDS THAT START ONE

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Language Domain) · Third Term W9.*

**You will learn to**

• begin a story with the words that open a tale
• write a beginning, a middle and an end of our own
• keep "there" and "their" apart
• read what we wrote to the class

**Things to know**

• Stories often open with the old door-word: **"Once upon a time…"** and **once** is our sight word for that.
• Three lines make a story: what was the start, what happened, how it ended.
• **Want** is a doing word for a wish in the heart: **"They want their toys."** — that line has both want and
  their in it.
• **Their** tells that things belong to people: **their toys**, **their books**; it is the family of "they".
• **There** shows a place: **the ball is there**. The two sound the same and are not the same word.
• Put a describing word in the middle line and the listener sees the picture: **the small dog**,
  **the long road**.
• Write about your own life too, and it is already a story: **"What I did"**, **"My family"**,
  **"My favourite toy"**.
• Do not stop at the first line: a story may be wrong in its letters and still be yours; we fix marks after,
  not while.
• If a word will not come, draw it in the box above and ask for its letters when you finish.
• Read your story out with your finger under each word; the class may ask you one question about it.

**Words for my notebook**

Once begins a tale. Want is a wish word, and their means things belong to people — there is about a place and
sounds the same. My story has a beginning, a middle and an end.

**Let us talk**

1. What word often opens a story?
2. Say a line with "want" and "their" in it.
3. Which one is about a place — there or their?
4. What will you write about: your day, your family, or your favourite toy?


![A boy sits at a table with three empty boxes drawn on his sheet, pencil in hand, while a teddy and a small drum wait at the corner.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-language/week8-three-empty-story-boxes.png)
**Worksheet**

1. "____ upon a time" begins a story with which word? A) Once B) One C) Only D) Over
2. "They ____ their shoes" — the wish word is ____ . A) there B) want C) their D) when
3. The words that mean things belong to the people spoken of are ____ . A) there B) they C) their D) then
4. "The ball is there" — there tells us ____ . A) a place B) a colour C) a number D) a reason
5. Fill in: "there" and "their" sound the same but do not mean the same ____ .
6. Write the three lines of your story, each with one naming word and one doing word.
7. Add one describing word to your middle line and read it to the class.

**My own work**

Draw three empty boxes for the beginning, the middle and the end of your own story, then write one line under
each box.

---

### WEEK 9 — THE WHOLE OF IT ON ONE PAGE

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Language Domain) · Third Term W10.*

**You will learn to**

• read a page that mixes every pattern we learnt
• say what kind of word each word in a line is
• write a clean page with the marks in their places
• look back over your own work and mend what is wrong

**Things to know**

• Our last page carries all of it at once: **"Kemi finds a little black cat under a bench. She takes it home
  and gives it milk. The cat drinks, jumps up and sleeps upon her bed."**
• Sounds hidden in it: the **bl** and **ck** of black, the **nd** of and, the **sh** in she, the **tch** in
  bench, the **air** we met in hair.
• Name the kinds of word: **Kemi, cat, bench, home, milk, bed** are naming words; **finds, takes, gives,
  drinks, jumps, sleeps** are doing words; **little, black** describe.
• Marks to check on every line of yours: big letter first and on every name, room between words, full stop at
  the end, question mark if it asks.
• Proofreading is reading your own work **slowly with your finger**, looking for the four things above and
  nothing else.
• The sight words we took all term are here too: **upon, little, what, there, their, should, old, young,
  because, them, put, once, want, your, yellow, shirt, clock, hair**.
• Reading on and on without stopping is a muscle, not a trick: a page more each day, and the arm does not tire.
• When a word will not come, say its sounds, count them on your fingers, and write one letter for each.
• Read your page to a friend and let them ask you **who, what, where, when and why** about it.
• What you can do on the last day of this term — read a page, name the kinds of word, write three clean lines —
  is what the next class will build on.

**Words for my notebook**

On one page I can find the naming words, the doing words and the describing words, and the sounds we learnt. I
check my own lines for a big letter, room between words and the right mark at the end. That is what I take to
Primary 1.

**Let us talk**

1. Read Kemi's page once with your finger and once with your eyes.
2. Name three doing words and two describing words in it.
3. Which four things do you look for when you proofread your own line?
4. What will you still want to be better at when you are in Primary 1?


![A girl stands at the front and reads her own blank page to the class while three children sit on the bench listening, one holding a closed book.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-language/week9-reading-my-own-page.png)
**Worksheet**

1. In "the small black cat", the describing words are ____ . A) small and black B) the and cat C) cat only D) the only
2. Which word in Kemi's page is a doing word? A) bench B) drinks C) milk D) bed
3. The **tch** in "bench" is a sound made of ____ letters. A) two B) four C) three D) one
4. Proofreading your own line means reading it ____ . A) slowly with your finger B) aloud to nobody C) very fast D) with your eyes shut
5. Fill in: Every name of a person or a place begins with a ____ letter.
6. Write three clean lines about Kemi and the cat, with the marks we learnt.
7. Say two things you can do now that you could not do in the first term.

**My own work**

Write three lines about the little cat on your own page, then draw one thing from your story beside it and read
both to the class.

## LITERACY — LETTER WORK

### WEEK 1 — THE WORDS WE KNOW AND THE SENTENCES WE BUILD

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy · Third Term W1.*

**You will learn to**

• read the sight words we learnt, without stopping
