• put words in a line and read the whole sentence out
• begin a sentence big, leave room between words, and end with a full stop
• match a sentence to the picture it tells about

**Things to know**

• A **sight word** is a word we know at once, the way we know a face: **I, a, the, is, am, and, it, in, at,
  on, he, she, we, me, my, to, go, no, up, can**.
• More we add this term: **see, look, here, there, come, have, like, play, run, jump**.
• A sentence gives a whole talk: **"I can see the dog."** — five words, one full stop.
• Three things make a sentence neat: a **big letter** first, a **space** between every word, a **full stop**
  at the end.
• The word **the** is the one we meet most; say it without spelling it out.
• Read with your finger along the line, and do not jump to the next line before this one is finished.
• A picture and a sentence are friends: for a boy with a ball, we may write **"He can play."**
• The same words can make different sentences: **"She is here."** and **"Here is she."** — the first one is the
  way we say it.
• When we write, we sit well, hold the pencil lightly, and keep the line under the words.

**Words for my notebook**

The sight words of this term: I, a, the, is, am, and, it, in, at, on, he, she, we, me, my, to, go, no, up, can,
see, look, here, there, come, have, like, play, run, jump. A sentence begins big, has room between its words,
and ends with a full stop.

**Let us talk**

1. Which word do we meet most in a sentence?
2. Read the line "I can see the red ball." with your finger on it.
3. Name the three things that make a sentence neat.
4. What could we write under a picture of a girl with a book?


![A boy pours water from a jug into a basin on a stand, with a towel on a peg above and one long blank strip below for the sentence he will write.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-literacy/week1-the-morning-washstand.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A word we know at once, without spelling it, is a ____ word. A) sight B) long C) new D) hard
2. Every sentence ends with a ____ . A) comma B) space C) full stop D) big letter
3. In "the boy can run", which word do we meet most in English? A) run B) the C) can D) boy
4. A space is left ____ . A) before the full stop only B) at the start of the line C) inside a word D) between every two words
5. Fill in: The first letter of a sentence is a ____ letter.
6. Read these words out: come, play, here, see, jump.
7. Write one sentence about what you did this morning.

**My own work**

Draw one picture of what you do in the morning, then write one sentence about it under the picture.

---

### WEEK 2 — FIVE-LETTER WORDS AND WORDS LONGER STILL

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy · Third Term W2.*

**You will learn to**

• count the letters in a word on your fingers
• put five sounds together and say the word
• read words where two consonants or two letters go together
• use a long word in a sentence of your own

**Things to know**

• **plant** has five letters: **p-l-a-n-t**; we say the five sounds and join them at once.
• Words can begin with **two consonants joined**: **pl**ant, **br**ush, **st**amp, **st**and, **dr**ink,
  **fr**ost — the two run together, they are not pulled apart.
• Two letters can also make **one sound** between them: **sh** in sheep, **th** in three and think, **ch** in
  chain, **wh** in while, **qu** in queen.
• Say them slowly, then fast: **p… l… a… n… t** — **plant**; **sh… ee… p** — **sheep**.
• Five-letter words we read this term: **plant, brush, stamp, stand, drink, frost, sheep, three, chain,
  queen, while, think, trend**.
• A longer word is only a short word with more sounds; nothing in it is frightening.
• Put the new word in a line you know: **"I put the plant in the sun."**
• Word hunt: when we see one of our words in a page, we touch it and say it out.
• Writing a long word: say it, count the sounds on your fingers, then write one letter for each sound.

**Words for my notebook**

Five-letter words: plant, brush, stamp, stand, drink, frost, sheep, three, chain, queen, while, think. Two
consonants at the start run together, and sh, th, ch, wh and qu are two letters that make one sound.

**Let us talk**

1. How many letters has "brush"? Count them on your fingers.
2. Which two letters make one sound in "sheep"?
3. Say the sounds of "plant" slowly, then say the word fast.
4. Make a sentence with the word "drink".


![Five boxes hold five things to sound out: a young plant, a brush, a stamp with a round handle, an empty cup, and a boy standing upright.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-literacy/week2-five-boxes-to-sound-out.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The word "stamp" has ____ letters. A) four B) five C) six D) three
2. In "three", the two letters that make one sound at the start are ____ . A) th B) re C) ee D) hr
3. The two letters at the start of "brush" say ____ . A) two long sounds apart B) no sound C) one joined sound D) a full stop
4. "Sheep" is spelled s-h-e-e-p: how many sounds joined make it? A) two B) three C) five D) six
5. Fill in: In "rain + bow = rainbow" the two parts join to make one ____.
6. Read five of our long words out loud and put one in a sentence.
7. Write three five-letter words and underline the two letters that start them.

**My own work**

Draw the five things — a plant, a brush, a stamp, a cup to drink from and a boy standing — one in each box.

---

### WEEK 3 — TWO WORDS JOINED: COMPOUND WORDS

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy · Third Term W3.*

**You will learn to**

• say the two small words inside a big word
• join two words to make a new one
• part a joined word and say what each part meant
• read a line that carries a joined word

**Things to know**

• Two words can hold hands and become **one word**: this is a **compound word**.
• **sun + set = sunset** — the time the sun goes down; **rain + bow = rainbow** — the colour band after rain.
• **foot + ball = football** — the game the foot plays; **cup + cake = cupcake** — a small cake baked in a
  cup shape.
• Places are named this way: **bed + room = bedroom**, **class + room = classroom**,
  **play + ground = playground**, **note + book = notebook**.
• Position words too: **out + side = outside**, **in + side = inside**.
• The morning plate has a name made the same way: **break + fast = breakfast** — the meal that breaks the fast
  of the night.
• Say the parts first and the whole after: **sun — set — sunset**.
• The whole word often means more than its parts: a **playground** is not only a ground that plays.
• Some joined words keep two beats of the voice; clap the word: **bed-room** is two claps, **plant** is one.
• Make your own and test it on the class: **pan + cake = pancake**, **snail + shell = snailshell** — the
  class says whether the word is used or only made.

**Words for my notebook**

A compound word is two words joined into one: sun + set = sunset, rain + bow = rainbow, foot + ball =
football, bed + room = bedroom, note + book = notebook, break + fast = breakfast.

**Let us talk**

1. Which two words make "rainbow"?
2. What is a bedroom?
3. Clap "classroom" — how many claps?
4. Say a joined word you made yourself and what it would mean.


![Four small scenes: the sun half gone behind the hills, a rainbow over the clouds, a ball at a girl's shoes, and a bed with its lamp in a room.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-literacy/week3-four-scenes-one-word.png)
**Worksheet**

1. "Sun + set" joined makes ____ . A) sunset B) set C) sun D) Sunday
2. Which two words make "notebook"? A) note + bake B) node + book C) note + book D) note + bookcase
3. A playground is a place we ____ . A) sleep in B) play in C) cook in D) wash in
4. "Breakfast" is the meal that ____ the fast of the night. A) doubles B) keeps C) breaks D) buys
5. Fill in: rain + bow makes one word called a ____ word.
6. Part these words into two: bedroom, cupcake, outside.
7. Write two sentences, each with one joined word in it.

**My own work**

Draw a sunset with a rainbow over it, a ball at the foot of a child, and a bed inside a room.

---

### WEEK 4 — READING A STORY AND SAYING WHAT YOU HEARD

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy · Third Term W4.*

**You will learn to**

• read a short story of words we already know
• say who is in the story and where it happens
• tell what happened first, next and last
• answer with a whole line, not one word

**Things to know**

• A story has three parts: the **beginning**, the **middle** and the **end**.
• The **characters** are the people or animals the story is about; the **setting** is where and when it
  happens.
• Our story for this week, read line by line: **"The hen goes out. She sees a corn. She eats the corn. The
  rain comes. The hen runs home."**
• Three questions will open nearly any story: **Who?** (the hen) **What?** (she found corn) **Where?** (by the
  fence, then home).
• Beginning — the hen goes out; middle — she sees and eats the corn; end — the rain comes and she runs home.
• Retell it in your own words: it is not the same as reading it; your line may be shorter or longer.
• Say what you liked and why: **"I like the part with the corn because the hen is happy."**
• Tie it to your own day: **"When the rain came, I ran home too."**
• Look for the words we learnt inside the story — **the, she, goes, sees, comes, runs, home, out**.
• A story you have read is a story you can draw: three boxes, three parts, in order.

**Words for my notebook**

A story has a beginning, a middle and an end. The characters are who it is about and the setting is where it
happens. Who? What? Where? are the three questions we ask.

**Let us talk**

1. Who is in our story, and what did she find?
2. Where was the hen when the rain came?
3. Which part of the story happened first?
4. What part did you like, and why?


![Our hen in three boxes: she walks out at the fence, she eats five grains on the ground, and she runs home with rain falling and a leaf above her.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-literacy/week4-the-hen-in-three-boxes.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The people or animals a story is about are its ____ . A) setting B) characters C) letters D) pages
2. "Where and when a story happens" is its ____ . A) middle B) end C) setting D) title
3. In our hen story, what happened last? A) The hen runs home. B) She sees a corn. C) The hen goes out. D) She eats the corn.
4. The part of the story that comes first is the ____ . A) end B) middle C) beginning D) last line
5. Fill in: When we tell a story in our own words, we say it is a ____.
6. Answer in a whole line: what did the hen do when the rain came?
7. Write one sentence about a time the rain came while you were outside.

**My own work**

Draw our hen story in three boxes — beginning, middle and end — and put a number in the corner of each box.

---

### WEEK 5 — WORDS THAT TELL US WHAT A THING IS LIKE

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy · Third Term W7 (Nursery floor).*

**You will learn to**

• use a colour word before a thing
• say how big, how tall or how long a thing is
• use words for how a thing feels or is
• put one describing word in each of two sentences

**Things to know**

• A word that tells us what a thing is **like** is a **describing word**.
• Colour words: **red, blue, green, yellow, black, white, brown, pink, orange, purple**.
• Size words: **big, small, tall, short, long, fat, thin**.
• How a thing is: **good, bad, hot, cold, happy, sad, fast, slow**.
• The describing word usually comes **before** the thing: **"The big dog."** — **"A red ball."**
• Two things can share one word: a **tall** man and a **tall** tree; a **hot** pot and a **hot** day.
• Say the pair out loud and hear the difference: **"a big stone"** and **"a small stone"**.
• A line with one describing word is worth a line without: **"The dog runs."** and **"The fast dog runs."**
• Match the word to the picture: for a snail, **slow**; for a running child, **fast**.
• Do not pile them up: one good describing word is enough in a small sentence.

**Words for my notebook**

A describing word tells what a thing is like — its colour, its size, or how it is. It usually comes before the
thing: the big dog, a red ball, a cold drink.

**Let us talk**

1. What colour is your bag today?
2. Say one big thing and one small thing in this room.
3. Which word goes with a snail — fast or slow?
4. Make the line "The pot is hot" into a line with the word before the thing.


![Opposites drawn side by side: a big dog with a small dog, a tall tree beside a short one, and a slow snail near a boy who is running fast.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-literacy/week5-the-opposite-pairs.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A describing word tells us what a thing is ____ . A) like B) worth C) made of D) called
2. In "the tall tree", the describing word is ____ . A) the B) tree C) tall D) one
3. Which word is the opposite of "big"? A) tall B) long C) small D) thin
4. For a snail we say ____ . A) fast B) slow C) hot D) happy
5. Fill in: "A ____ ball" can be filled with a colour word such as red.
6. Name three colour words and three size words.
7. Write two sentences, each with one describing word in it.

**My own work**

Draw a big dog and a small dog, a tall tree and a short tree, and a snail with a running child near it.

---

### WEEK 6 — ACTION WORDS: THE WORDS THAT MOVE

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy · Third Term W8 (Nursery floor).*

**You will learn to**

• name the words that tell what somebody does
• show an action word with the body
• put an action word in a sentence about yourself
• match the action in a picture to its word

**Things to know**

• A word that tells what somebody or something **does** is an **action word**.
• Our first list: **run, jump, walk, sit, stand, eat, drink, sleep, play, read, write, draw**.
• More to add to it: **sing, dance, swim, fly, hop, skip, clap, throw, catch, kick**.
• "I can run" — run is the action; "Birds fly" — fly is the action.
• Say an action word and **do** it: the class is the picture of the word.
• One picture, one word: children with hands up clapping — **clap**; a boy and a ball in the air —
  **throw** and **catch**.
• Action words change a little with who does them: **I run**, but **she runs**.
• Write a line about your own day: **"I jump at play. I read in class. I sleep at night."**
• Guessing game: one child acts, the class names the action word.
• A story without action words would not move at all; that is why we keep them in mind when we write.

**Words for my notebook**

An action word tells what somebody does: run, jump, walk, sit, stand, eat, drink, sleep, play, read, write,
draw, sing, dance, swim, fly, hop, skip, clap, throw, catch, kick.

**Let us talk**

1. What action word is this picture of two hands together?
2. Say a line that begins "I can" and ends with an action word.
3. Which word changes when we say "she" instead of "I"?
4. Act an action word and let the class guess it.


![Four children, four actions: one runs, one jumps with both feet off the ground, one claps, and one leans forward with open hands to catch the ball.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-literacy/week6-four-actions-in-a-row.png)
**Worksheet**

1. An action word tells us what somebody ____ . A) looks like B) does C) owns D) is named
2. In "The girl can swim", the action word is ____ . A) can B) girl C) swim D) the
3. Which pair are both action words? A) red and big B) kick and catch C) hot and cold D) table and chair
4. We say "I run" but "she ____" . A) runs B) run C) running D) ran
5. Fill in: Two hands together, making a sound, shows the word ____.
6. Name eight action words and do three of them.
7. Write three lines about your day, each with one action word.

**My own work**

Draw four children doing four different things — running, jumping, clapping and catching a ball — and write
the action word for each.

---

### WEEK 8 — WRITING A STORY OF OUR OWN

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy · Third Term W9.*

**You will learn to**

• look at three pictures and say the story they hold
• write a beginning, a middle and an end
• use the sight words we know inside our lines
• read our story to the class

**Things to know**

• A story we write ourselves can be **three lines long** — that is already a story.
• First line: **who** and **where**. Second line: **what happened**. Third line: **how it ended**.
• Use what you can write: the sight words, the action words, and the describing words.
• Our three pictures this week: a boy **finds** a ball; the boy **kicks** the ball over a fence; a girl
  **gives** the ball back to him.
• A story from those boxes: **"I have a ball. I kick the ball. It goes over the fence. Ada sees the ball. She
  gives it to me. We play."**
• Say the line in your head first, then write it; do not leave the space out between the words.
• Begin every new sentence with a big letter and end it with a full stop.
• If a word will not come, draw it in the box above the line and ask for its letters after.
• Give your story a name: **"The Ball"**, **"Over the Fence"**, **"My Friend Ada"**.
• When you read it out, point at each word and let the class look at your picture boxes.

**Words for my notebook**

My story has a beginning, a middle and an end. I use the words I know, I begin big, I leave room between
words, and I end each line with a full stop.

**Let us talk**

1. What is happening in the first box of our three pictures?
2. Which line of your story tells how it ended?
3. What do you do with a word you cannot write yet?
4. What name will you give your story?


![A ball story in three boxes: a boy picks the ball up, he kicks it over the fence, and a girl hands it back to him with both hands out.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-literacy/week8-the-ball-in-three-boxes.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The first line of a small story tells us who and ____ . A) where B) why only C) how much D) when last
2. Three boxes for a story hold a beginning, a middle and an ____ . A) colour B) end C) name D) age
3. Each new sentence must begin with a ____ letter. A) small B) tall C) big D) round
4. If a word will not come to you, the note says to ____ . A) stop writing B) ask nobody C) draw it in the box D) leave the page empty
5. Fill in: We end every sentence with a ____ ____.
6. Write your three-line story about the ball, then give it a name.
7. Read your story to the class and say which line you like best.

**My own work**

Draw three boxes and tell your own story in them, then write one line of words under each box.

---

### WEEK 9 — THE MARKS THAT HELP A READER

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy · Third Term W10.*

**You will learn to**

• begin a sentence and a name with a big letter
• put a full stop where a sentence ends
• use a question mark and an exclamation mark rightly
• leave the right room between words so the page is neat

**Things to know**

• A **big letter** (a capital) begins every sentence: **"The dog runs."**
• A name takes a big letter too: **Ada**, **Tunde**, **Bola**, **Musa**, and the name of our own town.
• A **full stop** closes a sentence and tells the reader to breathe: **"I can jump."**
• A **question mark** opens a asking line: **"What is it?"** — it stands where the full stop would.
• An **exclamation mark** is for a strong call or a surprise: **"Look out!"**, **"What a big ball!"**
• A sentence never carries two of these marks at once; it is either told, asked or cried.
• Room between words is part of writing: **"Ican seeit"** is hard, **"I can see it"** is read at once.
• Neat writing is slow writing done twice: once to say it, once to make the letters sit on the line.
• Read your own page back with your finger; a missing full stop is felt before it is seen.
• The marks are not ornaments — they are the traffic lights of the page: stop, ask, shout.

**Words for my notebook**

A sentence begins with a big letter and ends with a full stop. A question mark ends an asking line and an
exclamation mark ends a strong call. Names take a big letter, and every word keeps its own room.

**Let us talk**

1. Which mark tells the reader that the sentence is finished?
2. What would you put at the end of "Where is my bag"?
3. Say your own name and tell me which letter in it is big.
4. Read this line the untidy way and then the neat way: "Icanrun."


![A girl writes on a ruled sheet whose first line ends with one small round dot, and two blank cards stand on the table, the first card taller than the other.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-literacy/week9-the-line-ends-with-a-dot.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A sentence begins with a ____ letter. A) big B) small C) round D) quiet
2. "Where is the boy ____" — the mark it needs is a ____ . A) full stop B) comma C) question mark D) space
3. "Look out ____" is a strong call, so it ends with an ____ mark. A) asking B) exclamation C) equal D) empty
4. The name of a friend takes which kind of letter at its start? A) a big one B) a small one C) none D) a figure
5. Fill in: Room between words makes a page easy to ____ .
6. Rewrite this line well: "ican see it"
7. Write three lines about your family, with the marks we learnt.

**My own work**

Write three lines about your family, then draw one small mark in each box — a dot, a hook and a straight
line with a dot under it.

## NUMERACY

### WEEK 1 — HALVES AND QUARTERS

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Mathematics · Third Term W1, held down to the Nursery floor.*

**You will learn to**

• share two things equally between two friends
• say what half of a thing is
• find half of a shape and shade it
• fold a shape into four equal parts and say the name of one part

**Things to know**

• **Equal** means the parts are the same size; two parts of different sizes are **not** halves.
• When one orange is shared between two children so that both get the same, each one has **half** — written
  **½**.
• A **half** is one of **two equal parts** of a thing; the whole thing is two halves put back together.
• A circle folded once and cut on the fold gives two halves; a square folded corner to corner gives two
  halves too — they are triangles.
• To **shade half** of a shape, divide it into two equal parts and colour one of them — one part only.
• A rectangle can be halved down the middle or across the middle; both are right, because the two parts match.
• Fold a paper circle twice, once and then again, and you have **four equal parts**: each part is a
  **quarter**, written **¼**.
• Four quarters make the whole; two quarters make a half; so a quarter is smaller than a half.
• Sharing game: eight counters between two children is **four each** — that is half of eight; eight between
  four children is **two each** — that is a quarter of eight.

**Words for my notebook**

Half is one of two equal parts, and it is written ½. Quarter is one of four equal parts, and it is written ¼.
Two halves make the whole, and four quarters make the whole.

**Let us talk**

1. What must the two parts be like before we can call them halves?
2. Show me how to fold a paper circle into two halves.
3. How many quarters make one whole?
4. If six biscuits are shared among two children, how many does each get?

![A cake with one piece cut out, a circle cut into two equal halves, and a circle cut into four equal quarters.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-numeracy/week1-shapes-cut-in-halves-and-quarters.png)
**Worksheet**

1. When a thing is cut into two equal parts, each part is a ____ . A) quarter B) whole C) half D) side
2. The mark **½** stands for ____ . A) one half B) two halves C) one quarter D) four wholes
3. Which of these is NOT a half? ____ . A) two equal parts B) a fold that matches C) two quarters joined D) the bigger unequal piece
4. A paper circle folded twice and cut gives ____ . A) two halves B) four quarters C) three parts D) one whole
5. Fill in: Four ____ make one whole.
6. Draw a square and shade half of it; then say how you divided it.
7. Write two sentences on sharing ten shells between two friends.

**My own work**

Fold a paper square into two equal parts and cut along the fold; then draw a circle, a square and a rectangle
and shade half of each one.

---

### WEEK 2 — LENGTH: HOW LONG IS IT?

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Numeracy · Third Term W7 and Primary 1 · Mathematics · Third Term W2.*

**You will learn to**

• say which of two things is longer or shorter
• measure with the body: the hand span and the foot
• measure with a pencil, a stick or a piece of thread
• put three things in order from the shortest to the longest

**Things to know**

• **Length** is how long a thing is from one end to the other.
• We compare before we measure: this strap is **longer**, that one is **shorter**, and two may be **the same
  length**.
• A **hand span** — thumb out to little finger out — is a body measure; so is the **foot**, the **arm** from
  elbow to fingertip, and one **step**.
• To measure with a hand span, we put the spans side by side with **no gap and no overlap**, and count them.
• A **pencil**, a **stick** or a piece of **thread** can carry a measure across, and then we count how many
  pencils the desk is.
• The same table measured with a hand and with a foot gives **different numbers**, because the units are not
  the same size — so people agree on one unit.
• The small unit for length is the **centimetre (cm)** and the big one is the **metre (m)**; one metre is a
  long step, and a ruler is marked in centimetres.
• On a ruler the little **lines** are the centimetres; we lay the thing on the line that begins at **zero**,
  not at the edge of the ruler.
• Order three things by length and say it out: "The stick is the longest, the crayon is the shortest, and the
  pencil is between them."
• Estimating is a skill: first say how many spans you think it will be, then measure and see how near you were.

**Words for my notebook**

Length is how long a thing is from end to end. We can measure with a hand span, a foot, a pencil or a stick,
and we can measure with a ruler in centimetres; one metre is 100 centimetres.

**Let us talk**

1. Which is longer, your exercise book or your pencil purse?
2. How many hand spans is your desk?
3. Why do we count the spans with no gap between them?
4. What do the little lines on a ruler stand for?


![A boy spans the desk with both hands and marks three spans along the top; on the floor a crayon, a pencil and a stick lie from shortest to longest.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-numeracy/week2-the-hand-span-desk.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Length of a thing is how ____ it is from end to end. A) long B) heavy C) hot D) loud
2. Measuring a desk with your hand span uses a ____ measure. A) paper B) water C) money D) body
3. One metre is the same as ____ centimetres. A) ten B) one hundred C) fifty D) five hundred
4. When two ropes are the same length, ____ . A) one is longer B) both are short C) they match end to end D) one is heavier
5. Fill in: We begin to measure on the line marked ____, not on the edge of the ruler.
6. Put three things in your class in order from shortest to longest and write the order.
7. Write two sentences on what you measured with your foot and how many steps it was.

**My own work**

Measure three things on your table with your hand span and write the number of spans beside each; then draw a
crayon, a pencil and a stick in a row from the shortest to the longest.

---

### WEEK 3 — WEIGHT: HEAVY AND LIGHT

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 2 · Numeracy · Third Term W8, with Primary 1 · Mathematics · Third Term W3.*

**You will learn to**

• say which of two things is heavier or lighter
• sort things into heavy, middle and light
• compare weights with a hanger balance
• know that a big thing is not always the heavier thing

**Things to know**

• **Weight** is how heavy a thing is — how hard your hand has to work to lift it.
• We say **heavier**, **lighter**, **the heaviest**, **the lightest**, and **the same weight**.
• Lifting two things in two hands is the first way to tell: the hand that goes down found the heavier thing.
• A **hanger balance** — a coat hanger with two cups on string — shows it without lifting: the heavier side
  goes down.
• When the two cups stand level, the two things are **the same weight**.
• We can weigh with **blocks**: how many blocks does it take to bring the cup level? That number is the
  weight in blocks.
• **Big is not always heavy**: a big balloon is lighter than a small stone; a bunch of cotton wool is bigger
  than a chalk and still lighter.
• In the market things are weighed: fruits, yam, rice and oil; the seller puts them on a scale.
• The small unit of weight is the **gram (g)** and the big one is the **kilogram (kg)**; a small stone is some
  grams, and a bag of rice is counted in kilograms.
• Weighing in real life: your school bag on Monday and on Friday may not be the same weight, because the books
  inside are different.
• Thinking question: if an orange is heavier than a grape, which one is lighter? — the grape.

**Words for my notebook**

Weight is how heavy a thing is. The heavier side goes down on a balance, and when the cups are level the two
things weigh the same. A big thing is not always heavier: a balloon is bigger than a stone and lighter.

**Let us talk**

1. Which is heavier, your chair or your bag?
2. What does a level balance tell you about the two cups?
3. Name one big thing that is light and one small thing that is heavy.
4. How many blocks does your pencil purse need to make the cups level?


![A hanger balance with two cups: one stone pulls its cup down, three blocks in the other cup sit higher, and a girl points at the difference.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-numeracy/week3-the-hanger-balance.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Weight tells us how ____ a thing is. A) long B) tall C) wide D) heavy
2. On a balance, the heavier side ____ . A) stays up B) goes down C) swings away D) disappears
3. A big balloon and a small stone: which is lighter? ____ . A) the stone B) neither C) the balloon D) both
4. To weigh with blocks, we count how many blocks make the cups ____ . A) level B) empty C) wet D) higher
5. Fill in: The big unit of weight is the kilo____ .
6. Sort six things in your class into heavy, middle and light and write the three lists.
7. Write two sentences on a thing you lifted that surprised you.

**My own work**

Draw a hanger balance with a stone in one cup and three blocks in the other, and let the stone side hang
lower; then colour the blocks.

---

### WEEK 4 — TIME: THE DAY, THE WEEK AND THE CLOCK

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Mathematics · Third Term W4, with Primary 2 · Mathematics · Second Term W9–W10.*

**You will learn to**

• say the parts of the day and the days of the week in order
• use yesterday, today and tomorrow rightly
• name the parts of a clock and read the hour on it
• say what o'clock and half past look like on the clock face

**Things to know**

• The day has parts: **morning**, **midday**, **afternoon**, **evening** and **night**.
• The week has **seven days**: Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, Friday, Saturday, Sunday.
• **Yesterday** is the day that is gone, **today** is the day we are in, **tomorrow** is the day that is coming.
• A clock is drawn as a **face** with the numbers **1 to 12** round it and **two hands** in the middle.
• The **short hand** counts the hours and the **long hand** counts the minutes; the short hand moves slowly,
  the long hand moves fast.
• It is **o'clock** when the long hand stands straight up at 12; where the short hand points is the hour — so
  long hand at 12 and short hand at 9 is **9 o'clock**.
• It is **half past** when the long hand points down at 6 and the short hand stands past a number: long hand
  at 6, short hand past 3, is **half past 3**.
• A quarter of the way round is **quarter past**; three quarters is **quarter to** the hour that is coming.
• **a.m.** is the morning side of noon and **p.m.** is from noon to night; school hours are a.m. and supper
  time is p.m.
• Things take time: sweeping the yard is short, a farm day is long; we say "it took the morning", "it took two
  hours".
• Our day has an order — wake, wash, eat, school, learn, play, bathe, eat, sleep — and the clock tells us
  when each part comes.

**Words for my notebook**

The week has seven days and the day has its parts. The short hand counts the hours and the long hand counts
the minutes; when the long hand is at 12 it is o'clock, and when it is at 6 it is half past.

**Let us talk**

1. Say the seven days, beginning from Monday.
2. What do we call the day that comes after today?
3. Where is the long hand when it is o'clock?
4. Point to the morning part of the day on the clock and say what we do then.


![A clock face with twelve marks and no numerals: the short hand points left, the long hand points up — nine o'clock — with a bag, a cup and a broom below.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-numeracy/week4-nine-oclock.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The day that has already gone is ____ . A) today B) yesterday C) tomorrow D) midday
2. How many days make a week? ____ . A) five B) ten C) seven D) twelve
3. At o'clock, the long hand stands at ____ . A) 12 B) 6 C) 3 D) 9
4. The short hand on a clock tells the ____ . A) minutes only B) days C) months D) hours
5. Fill in: When the long hand points down at 6, it is half ____.
6. Write the parts of the day in order, from the time you wake to the time you sleep.
7. Write two sentences on how long it takes you to come to school and what you pass on the way.

**My own work**

Draw a big clock face with twelve short marks round the edge — or the numbers 1 to 12 — and put the short
hand on 9 with the long hand straight up. Then draw three things you do at that hour in the morning.

---

### WEEK 5 — SHAPES YOU CAN HOLD: THREE-DIMENSIONAL SHAPES

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Mathematics · Third Term W7.*

**You will learn to**

• name five shapes that take up room
• count the flat faces, the edges and the corners of a box
• sort shapes into the ones that roll and the ones that stack
• find these shapes in the house and the class

**Things to know**

• A shape you can **hold** takes up room; we call it a **three-dimensional** shape.
• A **cube** has six flat faces and every face is a square; a die and a small carton of sugar are cubes.
• A **cuboid** also has six flat faces, but its faces are rectangles; a exercise book, a matchbox and a
  carton of milk are cuboids.
• A **sphere** is round all over, has no flat face and no corner, and it rolls any way you put it; a ball is a
  sphere.
• A **cylinder** has two round flat faces — one at the top and one at the bottom — and a curved side; a tin of
  milk, a cup and a bucket are cylinders.
• A **cone** has one round flat face at the bottom, a curved side and **one corner** at the top; a party cap
  and a heap-sieve are cones.
• A **face** is a flat surface, an **edge** is where two faces meet, and a **corner** is where edges meet.
• A cube has **12 edges** and **8 corners**; a cuboid has the same, because it is a stretched cube.
• Rolling test: the sphere and the cylinder roll; the cube and the cuboid do not roll, they slide — and those
  two can **stack** without falling.
• Building: cubes make a wall that stands; balls make a heap that runs away; a cylinder laid on its side
  becomes a roller.
• Look round the class: the clock on the wall is a cylinder seen from the front, the ball in the store is a
  sphere, the chalk box is a cuboid.

**Words for my notebook**

A cube has six square faces; a cuboid has six rectangular faces; a sphere rolls and has no flat face; a
cylinder has two round faces; a cone has one round face and one corner. Faces, edges and corners are what we
count.

**Let us talk**

1. Bring one thing in the class that is a cuboid and one that is a sphere.
2. How many flat faces has a cube?
3. Which two of our five shapes will roll?
4. Where is the one corner of a cone?

![Five things you can hold on a shelf: a cube, a ball, a round tin, a party cap and a long box.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-numeracy/week5-five-things-you-can-hold.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A ball is shaped like a ____ . A) sphere B) cube C) cone D) cuboid
2. How many flat faces has a cube? ____ . A) four B) eight C) ten D) six
3. Which shape has one round face and one corner? ____ . A) cylinder B) cone C) cube D) sphere
4. A matchbox and an exercise book are both ____ . A) spheres B) cones C) cuboids D) cylinders
5. Fill in: The place where two faces meet is called an ____.
6. Sort ten things at home into the five shapes and write two of each.
7. Write two sentences on the shape you can stack and the shape you cannot.

**My own work**

Set a cube, a ball, a tin and a cap on the desk, look at them, then draw all four in a row and colour the tin.

---

### WEEK 6 — FLAT SHAPES: TWO-DIMENSIONAL SHAPES

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Mathematics · Third Term W8.*

**You will learn to**

• name five flat shapes
• count the sides and the corners of each
• draw a square, a rectangle and a triangle with a ruler
• find flat shapes on the things about us

**Things to know**

• A **flat shape** lies on a surface; it has length and width but nothing to hold.
• A **triangle** has **three sides** and **three corners**; a **square** has four equal sides and four corners.
• A **rectangle** has four sides too, but only the opposite sides are equal — it is a stretched square.
• A **circle** is one curved line with no corner and no side; everything on it is the same distance from the
  middle.
• An **oval** is like a squashed circle: one curved line, no corner — an egg's outline is an oval.
• A **side** is the line between two corners; a **corner** is the point where two sides meet.
• To draw straight, lay the ruler down, draw along it, lift the ruler and turn it at the corner.
• Two triangles put side to side make a rectangle; a square cut from corner to corner makes two triangles.
• Shapes are on everything: the exercise book is a rectangle, the clock's face is a circle, a scarf folded is
  a triangle, a slate is a rectangle.
• Sorting: shapes with corners in one hoop, shapes with no corner in another; four-sided shapes in a third.

**Words for my notebook**

A triangle has three sides and three corners. A square has four equal sides and a rectangle has four sides
with the opposite ones equal. A circle and an oval are curved and have no corner.

**Let us talk**

1. How many corners has a triangle, and how many has a square?
2. Which two flat shapes have no corner at all?
3. Show me how you make a rectangle out of two triangles.
4. Name three things in this room with a rectangular face.


![A house pasted from one square and one triangle with a rectangle door, and beside it a circle, a triangle, a rectangle and an oval waiting to be used.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-numeracy/week6-the-shape-house.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A shape with three sides and three corners is a ____ . A) square B) circle C) oval D) triangle
2. How many corners has a rectangle? ____ . A) two B) four C) three D) six
3. Which of these has no corner? ____ . A) a square B) a triangle C) a circle D) a rectangle
4. The sides of a square are ____ . A) equal B) unequal C) curved D) only two
5. Fill in: An oval is like a squashed ____.
6. Draw a triangle, a square and a rectangle with your ruler and count the sides of each.
7. Write two sentences on one thing at home whose face is a circle.

**My own work**

Draw one row of five flat shapes — circle, triangle, square, rectangle, oval — then cut them out and paste two
of them into one new shape.

---

### WEEK 8 — SHAPES THAT GO ON AND ON: PATTERNS AND SYMMETRY

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Mathematics · Third Term W9.*

**You will learn to**

• say the next thing in a pattern of shapes
• make a pattern of your own with shapes and colours
• find the flat shape inside a shape you can hold
• tell a line of symmetry on a folded paper

**Things to know**

• A **pattern** is things repeated in the same order, so that we can say what comes next.
• In **square, circle, square, circle**, the part that repeats is two shapes; the next is **square**.
• In **triangle, triangle, circle, triangle, triangle, circle**, the part that repeats is three shapes.
• A pattern can grow: one block, then two, then three — the next line has **four** blocks.
• Colour takes part in a pattern too: red, blue, red, blue is as much a pattern as shape is.
• Take a cube and look at one face: it is a **square**; a **cylinder** has a **circle** at the top; a cone
  looks like a **triangle** from the side.
• A flat shape can be made from other shapes: two triangles make a square; four triangles make a bigger
  triangle; a square and two triangles make a house.
• **Symmetry** means the same on both sides: fold a paper heart and the two halves match all the way round.
• The fold line where the two halves match is the **line of symmetry**; a square can be folded to match in four
  ways, a rectangle in two.
• A circle has no corner to miss: every fold through the middle matches.
• Art with shapes: cut squares, triangles and circles from paper and make a road, a house and a boat on one
  page.

**Words for my notebook**

A pattern repeats the same order, so the next thing can be said. A cube hides a square on each face and a
cylinder hides a circle at the top. A fold that makes both halves match shows a line of symmetry.

**Let us talk**

1. Square, circle, square, circle — what comes next?
2. What shape do you see on the face of a cube?
3. Show me a fold on this paper heart where both halves match.
4. Which two shapes make one square?


![Square, circle, square, circle, square, circle — and one more square set a little apart to finish the pattern; below it, a folded heart with a dashed middle line.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-numeracy/week8-the-shapes-that-repeat.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Circle, square, circle, square — the shape that comes next is a ____ . A) circle B) square C) triangle D) cone
2. Two triangles placed side by side can make ____ . A) a circle B) an oval C) a square D) a cone
3. When a folded paper matches on both sides, the fold line is a line of ____ . A) symmetry B) length C) weight D) count
4. The top of a cylinder is shaped like a ____ . A) triangle B) square C) rectangle D) circle
5. Fill in: In one, two, three, the next number of blocks is ____.
6. Make a pattern of your own with six shapes and write the part that repeats.
7. Write two sentences on one thing you cut and pasted today.

**My own work**

Cut two triangles, one square and one circle from paper, paste them into a picture of a house or a boat, and
draw the line of symmetry on the square part.

---

### WEEK 9 — COUNTING WHAT THE CLASS SAID: GATHERING INFORMATION

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Mathematics · Third Term W10.*

**You will learn to**

• ask a question and write down the answers
• record counts with strokes that are easy to add up
• put the answers into a simple table
• read a picture chart and a block chart of the class

**Things to know**

• We **gather** a thing by asking and counting, and then we put the answers in order so that the question is answered.
• First the question, small and clear: **"Which fruit do you like best — mango, banana or orange?"**
• Then we go round the class and take one answer from each child, and write it as we hear it.
• A stroke stands for one child; four strokes together and a fifth stroke across them means **five** — that is
  why strokes are easier to count than a long line of single marks.
• Answers are put in a **table**: one column for the fruit, one column for the number of children.
• In a **picture chart** one picture stands for one thing: three orange pictures mean three children chose
  oranges.
• In a **block chart** the tower of blocks stands for the count; the **taller** the tower, the **more** the
  children.
• Reading a chart is answering with the eyes: which tower is the tallest, which is the shortest, and by how
  many one beats another.
• We can gather the same way about colours, about pets, about the way to school, and about the song the class
  likes best.
• The class that counted must **check its total**: the numbers in the table should come to the number of
  children present.

**Words for my notebook**

We ask one clear question, take an answer from each child, and record it with strokes. A table keeps the
answers in order, a picture chart lets one picture stand for one child, and in a block chart the taller tower
means more children.

**Let us talk**

1. What is the first thing we do before we count anything?
2. Why do we put a fifth stroke across four strokes?
3. In a block chart, what does a taller tower tell us?
4. Our table said 8, 7 and 5 for twenty children present — does that add up?


![Three towers of blocks stand on one line, five high, three high and two high, over an orange, a banana and a mango, while a girl holds up a blank slate.](assets/img/nursery-2/3rd-term/mathematics-english-numeracy/week9-three-towers-of-blocks.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Before we count, we first ____ . A) shout in the market B) sleep C) ask one clear question D) draw a clock
2. Four strokes with a fifth stroke across them stand for ____ . A) five B) four C) three D) eight
3. In a picture chart, one picture stands for ____ . A) the class B) a whole week C) ten children D) one child
4. In a block chart, the taller tower means ____ . A) fewer children B) more children C) no child D) a longer day
5. Fill in: The answers are put in a table with one column for the fruit and one for the ____.
6. Ask five friends their favourite colour and record the answers with strokes.
7. Write two sentences on what your class chart showed and what you would ask next.

**My own work**

Take the answers of ten children on their favourite fruit, record them with strokes, then draw a block chart
under the three fruit names and colour the tallest tower.


---

# General Knowledge

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. Week 7 is Mid-Term Break, Week 10 Revision, Week 11 Examination,
Week 12 Closing — those weeks carry no note.
*Depth: NERDC 2025 · Nursery 3 · General Knowledge · Third Term, from the school's own stream line
"My Big World & My Country's Story" — our state and town, water and air, the sky, the tools about us,
one Nigeria, counting and sorting, the people of great works, and the end-of-year games.*


### WEEK 1 — MY STATE AND MY TOWN

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · General Knowledge · Third Term W1.*

**You will learn to**

• say the name of my state and the town where I live
• put my state on the map of Nigeria, in a big first look
• name the famous things of my state: its food, its festivals and its places
• draw a picture map of my town with the five places we all know

**Things to know**

• A **state** is a big piece of the country with its own head; Nigeria has **thirty-six states** and one
  capital territory, and the capital territory is called **Abuja**.
• Our state is **Lagos**, and it sits at the **south-west** of Nigeria, where the land meets the sea.
• The big city of our state is called **Ikeja**; the town or village where my house stands is **my own town**.
• On the map we look for three things first: the **sea** at the bottom, the **bridge** that goes over the water,
  and the **lagoon** that lies between.
• Famous places in our state: the **beach** with its soft sand, the **harbour** where big ships stand, and the
  **bridge** long enough to cross the whole water.
• Famous food of our people: **àmàlà with ẹ̀fọ́**, and by the sea we also eat **fish** that came in a boat.
• Festival of our town: **Eyo** — the day the masked people walk in white through the streets and the whole town
  comes out to see.
• Five places every town has: the **market**, the **motor park**, the **school**, a **church or a mosque**, and
  the **stream or the water point**.
• A town is not only houses: it is the **people** in them — the seller, the driver, the guard, the teacher and
  my own family.

**Words for my notebook**

My state is Lagos, in the south-west of Nigeria, where the land meets the sea. My town has a market, a motor
park, a school, a church and a mosque, and a stream that runs past it.

**Let us talk**

1. What is the name of your state, and which town is your house in?
2. Point to the sea on the map and then point to our state.
3. Name two foods of our people and one food that comes from the sea.
4. Which four places did we say every town must have?

**Worksheet**

1. Our state in the south-west of Nigeria is ____ . A) Kano B) Lagos C) Borno D) Enugu
2. The big city of Lagos state is called ____ . A) Ikeja B) Zaria C) Lokoja D) Calabar
3. The festival where masked people walk in white is ____ . A) Durbar B) Ọ̀ṣun C) Eyo D) New Yam
4. On the map of Nigeria, the sea is at the ____ . A) top B) right side C) middle D) bottom
5. Fill in: Nigeria has thirty-six states and one capital territory called ____ .
6. Name the five places that a town has.
7. Write two sentences on the town where your house is.

**My own work**

Draw a picture map of our town: put the market on one road, the school on another, the motor park by the wide
road, and the stream running past behind them; colour the roads.

---

### WEEK 2 — WATER AND AIR: WHY THEY MATTER

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · General Knowledge · Third Term W2.*

**You will learn to**

• name where water comes from and what we use it for
• say why air matters even though we cannot see it
• keep water clean and not waste it
• do three small tests with water and air

**Things to know**

• Water comes to us from the **rain**, the **tap**, the **well**, the **stream** and the **river**.
• We use water to **drink**, to **bath**, to **cook**, to **wash** cloth and plates, and to give the **plants**.
• Air is **everywhere**; it is inside the room, inside the bag, and inside the ball we blow up.
• We cannot see air, but we can **feel it as wind**, and wind can move a leaf, a kite and a small boat.
• Living things **breathe**: we take air in and let it out; a plant takes in the part we throw out.
• Water stays clean when the vessel is **covered**, the cup is washed, and the hand does not go inside.
• Water for drinking is **boiled** by a grown-up and then kept in a clean covered keg.
• We do not waste it: the tap is **shut** while we rub the teeth, and one cup of water is enough for that.
• Test one — **float or sink**: a leaf floats, a stone sinks, a bottle top may do either.
• Test two — **bubbles**: blow water with a straw and the air comes out as round balls.
• Test three — a **wind chime**: hang shells on a thread by the window and the air plays the tune.

**Words for my notebook**

Water comes from rain, the tap, the well, the stream and the river; we drink it, bath in it, cook with it and
give it to plants. Air is everywhere, we breathe it, and wind is air that is moving.

**Let us talk**

1. Name four places water comes from.
2. What do we do with water every morning before school?
3. How can we know air is there when we cannot see it?
4. Say two ways we keep water clean and one way we waste it.

**Worksheet**

1. A stream and a river are places we get ____ from. A) air B) water C) fire D) sand
2. Wind is really ____ that is moving. A) rain B) dust C) air D) smoke
3. Before a child drinks it, water for the house is ____ . A) boiled B) frozen C) buried D) coloured
4. In the float-and-sink test, the thing that goes down is ____ . A) a dry leaf B) a feather C) a small stone D) a bottle top
5. Fill in: We shut the ____ while we rub our teeth, so that water is not wasted.
6. Say five things water is used for at home.
7. Write two sentences on what you saw in the bubble test.

**My own work**

Draw a tall glass of covered water, and beside it six small things — a leaf, a stone, a feather, a bead, a
bottle top and a bit of cloth — then ring the ones you think will float.

---

### WEEK 3 — THE SKY: SUN, MOON, STARS, DAY AND NIGHT

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · General Knowledge · Third Term W3.*

**You will learn to**

• say what the sun gives us and when it is up
• name what we see in the sky at night
• tell the parts of the day in order
• sort things into the day and the night

**Things to know**

• The **sun** gives us **light** and **warmth**; it is the reason the day is bright.
• The **daytime** is when we work, learn and play; the **night** is when the world rests and we sleep.
• At night we see the **moon** and the **stars**, and sometimes the moon is full and round.
• The parts of the day in order: **morning**, **midday**, **afternoon**, **evening**, **night**.
• In the **morning** the sun rises; at **midday** it is high and hottest; in the **evening** it goes down.
• The **sky** is everything above us; the clouds live in it and the **rain** comes down out of it.
• A cloud is a big crowd of very small drops of water; when they grow heavy, they fall as rain.
• Day and night are not the same: in the day we see colour, at night we see light from lamps, the moon and fire.
• Sorting game: a kite, a school bag and a market basket go to the **day**; a torch, a mosquito net and a night
  lamp go to the **night**.
• We do not look straight at the sun; its light is too strong for the eye.

**Words for my notebook**

The sun gives light and warmth and makes the day. The moon and the stars come with the night, when we rest. The
sky is above us, and the clouds and the rain live in it.

**Let us talk**

1. What two things does the sun give us?
2. Which two things do we see in the sky only at night?
3. Say the parts of the day, from morning to night.
4. Where does rain come from?

**Worksheet**

1. The thing that gives us light and warmth in the day is ____ . A) the moon B) the lamp C) the sun D) the star
2. At night we see the moon and the ____ . A) rainbow B) stars C) clouds at midday D) shadow at noon
3. The part of the day when the sun is high and hottest is ____ . A) morning B) evening C) night D) midday
4. Rain comes down out of the ____ . A) mountain B) well C) sky D) market
5. Fill in: When the sun goes down in the ____, we light the lamp.
6. Put two things in the day box and two things in the night box.
7. Write two sentences on what you do between evening and sleep.

**My own work**

Fold a page in two. In one half draw the sun and three things we do in the daylight; in the other half draw the
moon, three stars and three things we do at night.

---

### WEEK 4 — SIMPLE MACHINES AND TOOLS AROUND ME

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · General Knowledge · Third Term W4.*

**You will learn to**

• name the tools we use at home and at school
• say what each tool is for
• tell which things have wheels and what a wheel does
• say how a simple machine takes work off our hands

**Things to know**

• Tools at home: **spoon** for eating, **fork** for holding food, **knife** for cutting, **broom** for the
  floor, **rake** for the yard, **bucket** for water.
• Tools at school: **pencil** for writing, **crayon** for colouring, **ruler** for drawing a straight line,
  **scissors** for cutting paper.
• A **knife** and a pair of **scissors** are sharp, so they are carried with the point **down** and passed to a
  friend **by the handle**.
• Things with wheels: **bicycle**, **car**, **wheelbarrow**, and the **cart** at the market; a wheel **rolls**,
  it does not slide.
• A wheel makes a heavy thing easy to move; that is why a load goes in a wheelbarrow and not on the head
  alone.
• A **ramp** is a slope: a plank laid from the ground to a lorry lets a heavy bag slide up instead of being
  lifted.
• A **lever** is a strong stick: put one end under a stone, rest the middle on a small stone, and the big stone
  comes up.
• A tool does not do the work for us; it **takes part of the work off our hands**.
• Touch-and-tell: things go in a bag, a hand goes in, and the class names the tool without looking.
• Every tool has its place: the broom on the wall, the scissors in the box, the pencil in the purse.

**Words for my notebook**

Spoon, fork, knife, broom, rake and bucket are the tools of the house; pencil, crayon, ruler and scissors are
the tools of the class. A wheel rolls, a ramp is a slope, and a lever is a strong stick that lifts a stone.

**Let us talk**

1. Name four tools of the house and what each one is for.
2. How do you pass a pair of scissors to a friend?
3. What does a wheel do that makes a load easy?
4. Which two tools are kept out of the way of small hands?

**Worksheet**

1. The tool used for sweeping the floor is the ____ . A) rake B) bucket C) broom D) spoon
2. A wheelbarrow carries a load because it has a ____ . A) rope B) wheel C) lid D) blade
3. When we give a pair of scissors to somebody we hold it by the ____ . A) blades B) point C) spring D) handle
4. A plank laid from the ground up to a lorry is a ____ . A) ramp B) lever C) basket D) keg
5. Fill in: A strong stick used to lift a stone is called a ____ .
6. Match six tools to their work by drawing a line.
7. Write two sentences on the tool you use most in class.

**My own work**

Draw a box divided into six squares and put one tool in each square. Cut along the outside line with the
scissors, then match the tools to their work with a line.

---

### WEEK 5 — WE ARE ONE NIGERIA: BIG FAMILY, MANY PEOPLE

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · General Knowledge · Third Term W5.*

**You will learn to**

• say that Nigeria is one country of many peoples
• name foods and tongues that are different among us
• treat every person with the same respect
• take part in the class celebration of one Nigeria

**Things to know**

• Nigeria is **one country** made of **many peoples**; all of us are **Nigerians**.
• The country took its name from the **river Niger** that runs through it; the Niger and the **Benue** are the
  two big rivers, and they meet in the middle of the land.
• Among our peoples are the **Yoruba**, the **Igbo**, the **Hausa**, the **Tiv**, the **Edo**, the **Efik**, the
  **Ijaw**, the **Kanuri** and many more.
• Our foods are different and all of them are good: **àmàlà with ẹ̀fọ́** in the west, **pounded yam with
  soup** in the east, **tuwo with soup** in the north, and **fish** brought in a boat by the coast.
• Our tongues are different, but a smile, the word "hello" and a shared pencil need no translation.
• The flag is our one sign: **green, white, green** in three tall strips — the green of our land, the white of
  peace.
• **Respect** is for everybody: the old and the young, every tribe, boys and girls, a child who learns fast and
  a child who takes time.
• Kindness is a thing we **do**: sharing a biscuit, carrying a bag for a smaller child, saying "thank you" and
  "sorry".
• For the class celebration each child brings one thing of their people — a food, a cloth, a song — and we lay
  them on one table and walk round it singing **"One Nigeria, one people."**
• Different is not wrong; different is **more**. A pot with only one pepper in it has no taste.

**Words for my notebook**

Nigeria is one country of many peoples, many foods and many tongues. The flag is green, white and green. I
respect everybody the same, because different is not wrong — different is more.

**Let us talk**

1. What two rivers are the big rivers of Nigeria?
2. Name four peoples of Nigeria.
3. What are the colours of our flag, and how are they arranged?
4. Say three kind things you did this week and to whom.

**Worksheet**

1. Nigeria takes its name from ____ . A) a city B) a river C) a mountain D) a king
2. The colour in the middle of our flag is ____ . A) red B) green C) white D) gold
3. Our two big rivers, the Niger and the Benue, meet ____ . A) in the middle of the country B) at the sea C) in the north only D) at the market
4. Which of these is a people of Nigeria? ____ . A) the Zulu B) the Yoruba C) the Maya D) the Inuit
5. Fill in: Different is not wrong; different is ____ .
6. Name four foods of the different peoples of Nigeria.
7. Write two sentences on one kind thing you can do for a child who is different from you.

**My own work**

Draw the flag on a pole — green, white and green in three tall strips — and under it draw four children, each
holding one food of their people.

---

### WEEK 6 — COUNTING, SORTING AND EVERYDAY KNOWLEDGE

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · General Knowledge · Third Term W6.*

**You will learn to**

• count things about us from one to twenty
• sort things by size, colour, shape and kind
• say the days of the week and the routine of a day
• play the sorting games of the class

**Things to know**

• Counting is done with the **finger on the thing**: one bottle top, two bottle tops … twenty bottle tops, and
  then we put them in **two lines of ten**.
• Things in the house we can count: **cups, plates, spoons, chairs, the windows, the goats in the yard**.
• Sorting by **size**: big gari basin, small bowl; long cloth, small handkerchief.
• Sorting by **colour**: everything red in one heap, everything white in another, everything black in the last.
• Sorting by **shape**: a plate is **round**, a book is **square**, a ball is a **sphere**, a cone hat is a
  **cone**.
• Sorting by **kind**: fruits on one side — banana, mango, watermelon; vegetables on the other — ugu, spinach,
  carrot.
• The seven days come in order: **Sunday, Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, Friday, Saturday**.
• The routine of my day: **wake, wash, dress, eat, school, learn, play, bathe, eat, sleep** — and it is the
  same order every day, that is why the day feels easy.
• Games of the week: **shop play** (the seller, the buyer, the coins), **counting songs**, and a **sorting
  race** — who can put ten mixed beads into their colours first.
• A number is not only a word: five is five cups, five fingers, five beats of a drum.

**Words for my notebook**

I can count from one to twenty and put twenty into two lines of ten. I can sort by size, colour, shape and
kind. Sunday, Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, Friday, Saturday are the seven days of the week.

**Let us talk**

1. Count ten things on your table, out loud.
2. I have a plate, a book and a ball: which one is round?
3. Which day comes after Tuesday, and which comes before Sunday?
4. Say five things you do every day, in order.

**Worksheet**

1. Twenty bottle tops, put in two equal lines, make lines of ____ . A) five B) eight C) ten D) fifteen
2. A book is like which shape? ____ . A) a circle B) a square C) a cone D) a ball
3. The day that comes after Tuesday is ____ . A) Monday B) Thursday C) Sunday D) Wednesday
4. Which of these is a vegetable, not a fruit? ____ . A) ugu B) mango C) banana D) watermelon
5. Fill in: The day that comes right after Friday is ____ .
6. Sort these into fruits and vegetables: mango, carrot, banana, tomato, ugu, orange.
7. Write the five things you do before you come to school, in order.

**My own work**

Draw twenty bottle tops in two lines of ten and circle the tens. Under them, draw two baskets: write "fruit" on
one and "vegetable" on the other, then draw three things of each kind inside.

---

### WEEK 8 — PEOPLE WHO DID GREAT THINGS

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · General Knowledge · Third Term W8.*

**You will learn to**

• name the work-a-day people who keep a town going
• hear two stories of people of the past
• say what a hero does that a child can do too
• tell the class who you want to be when you grow up

**Things to know**

• The **farmer** rises before the sun and the whole town eats because of that work.
• The **teacher**, the **nurse**, the **driver**, the **cleaner**, the **seller** and the **guard** are the
  people who make a town move; none of them is a small work.
• In the old stories, **Ìjà̀pá the tortoise** is small and slow but he out-thinks the whole bush; cleverness is
  a thing he **uses**, and the stories also show where it takes him somewhere he did not want to go.
• **Queen Amina of Zaria** is remembered in the story as a **warrior queen**: the story says she rode with her
  people, made the roads safe for traders, and put up walls round the towns so that nobody could enter unasked.
• Old **kings and queens** — the Oba, the Emir, the Obi — sat on thrones, but the stories we love are the ones
  where they **listened** to an old woman at the market.
• The people who help **many** people are the **doctor**, the **nurse**, the **firefighter**, the **teacher** and
  the ones who dig a **well** in a village that had none.
• **Our own parents** are in this list too: they work, they cook, they pay school fees, they get up at dawn.
• What a hero does that a child can also do: **tell the truth, help the smaller one, share, stand up when
  somebody is being bullied, finish the work begun.**
• Show and tell this week: each child says **"When I grow up I want to be ____, because ____."**

**Words for my notebook**

The farmer, the teacher, the nurse, the driver and the seller keep a town going. Ìjà̀pá is the clever one of the
stories, and Queen Amina of Zaria is remembered as a warrior queen who made the roads safe. A hero tells the
truth and finishes the work begun.

**Let us talk**

1. Which worker feeds the whole town?
2. What did the story say Queen Amina did for the traders and the towns?
3. Which things does Ìjà̀pá use to get on in the stories?
4. Say who you want to be when you grow up and why.

**Worksheet**

1. The person whose work makes sure the whole town eats is the ____ . A) farmer B) driver C) guard D) barber
2. In the stories of our people, the small animal that out-thinks the bush is ____ . A) the elephant B) the tortoise C) the lion D) the bird
3. Queen Amina of Zaria is remembered as ____ . A) a warrior queen B) a market seller C) a boat man D) a drum maker
4. Which of these is a thing a child can do and be a hero for it? A) running fast home B) hiding a mistake C) telling the truth D) taking a big share
5. Fill in: The people who put out a fire are the ____ .
6. Name six workers who keep a town going.
7. Write two sentences on the work you want to do when you grow up and why you want it.

**My own work**

Draw the helper you want to be when you grow big — a nurse, a farmer, a driver, a teacher — and give the figure
one thing that person carries; colour the whole drawing.

---

### WEEK 9 — END-OF-YEAR QUIZ GAMES AND SHOW AND TELL

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · General Knowledge · Third Term W9.*

**You will learn to**

• answer quiz questions about what we have learnt all year
• play "Who am I?" and "Where does it live?"
• stand and show one thing to the class
• say what we are going to do next year

**Things to know**

• The quiz cards of this year carry five pictures: the **flag**, an **animal**, a **colour**, a **food** and a
  **helper**.
• In **"Who am I?"** the class is told three things and guesses: *"I give light and warmth in the day"* — the
  sun.
• In **"Where does it live?"** an animal is named and the child says its home: monkey — the **forest**;
  fish — the **water**; antelope — the **grassland**; hen — the **house yard**.
• **Show and tell**: each child brings **one thing** — a stone from the road, a fruit of the season, a photo of
  the family, a basket from the market — and says three lines about it.
• Three lines are enough: **"This is ____. It comes from ____. I like it because ____."**
• We sing the year's songs once more in a ring, and we say the rhymes in the order we learnt them.
• In the **GK games** the class takes two teams; a team that answers quietly gets the point, and a team that
  shouts does not.
• At the end we say it together: **"I am ready for Primary 1."** — new bag, new book, bigger school.
• What we take with us: the greetings, the counting, the names of the tools, the water rule, the flag, and the
  way we treat people who are different from us.

**Words for my notebook**

I answered the quiz about the flag, the animals, the colours, the foods and the helpers. I stood and showed one
thing to the class and said three lines about it. I am ready for Primary 1.

**Let us talk**

1. What do we say when a team wants a point in the quiz game?
2. Name an animal and say where it lives.
3. What are the three lines of a show-and-tell?
4. Say two things you will take with you into Primary 1.

**Worksheet**

1. In "Where does it live?", a monkey lives ____ . A) in the sea B) in the trees of the forest C) in a house D) at the motor park
2. The first line of a show-and-tell is ____ . A) This is … B) I am sorry C) Good morning D) How are you
3. Which of these is NOT one of our five quiz pictures? ____ . A) the flag B) a colour C) a planet D) a helper
4. A team gets the point in the GK game when it ____ . A) shouts first B) answers quietly C) takes the card D) sings last
5. Fill in: At the end of the day we say, "I am ready for Primary ____ ."
6. Name five things the quiz cards of this year carried.
7. Write your three show-and-tell lines about one thing you brought.

**My own work**

Make one quiz card: on one side draw an animal of our land, on the other side write its name, where it lives
and one thing it eats. Colour the animal and read your card to the class.

# EXAMINATION PRACTICE — BASIC SCIENCE (Nursery 2 · 3rd Term)

**Time:** 1 hour · **Total: 50 marks**

## Section A — Objective (30 marks)
Choose the correct option and ring the letter.

1. Water keeps best in a container that is ___
   A) clean and covered
   B) left in the sun
   C) made of paper
   D) bright in colour
2. Which of these is used to store water at home?
   A) a fishing net
   B) a writing slate
   C) a broom
   D) a jerry can
3. A lid on a water bucket keeps out ___
   A) the cold wind
   B) the light of day
   C) dirt and insects
   D) the smell of smoke
4. Before water is put into a container, the container is ___
   A) left where it is
   B) washed out with clean water
   C) warmed in the sun
   D) rubbed with dry sand
5. Water kept in an open bucket becomes dirty because ___
   A) the stand is too high
   B) the water is too cold
   C) the bucket is too heavy
   D) dust and insects fall in
6. A sickness that comes to us from dirty water is called ___
   A) a water-borne disease
   B) an ear disease
   C) a bone disease
   D) a skin disease
7. Which of these comes from dirty water?
   A) ring worm
   B) cholera
   C) measles
   D) tooth decay
8. Passing loose water many times in one day is ___
   A) cough
   B) sneezing
   C) rash
   D) diarrhoea
9. To make doubtful water safe for drinking we can ___
   A) boil it
   B) leave it open
   C) keep it in the shade
   D) add palm oil to it
10. After using the toilet, a child must wash the ___
   A) ears with sand
   B) bag in the sun
   C) hands with soap
   D) feet with chalk
11. Keeping people and property safe is called ___
   A) sleeping
   B) farming
   C) cooking
   D) security
12. Which gadget makes a loud sound when somebody forces a door?
   A) a dustbin
   B) a burglar alarm
   C) a hand broom
   D) a water tank
13. A CCTV camera is put up to ___
   A) record what happens
   B) cool the room
   C) pound the yam
   D) sweep the gate
14. The gadget that warns a house when there is smoke is a ___
   A) door bell
   B) hand fan
   C) smoke alarm
   D) cutlass
15. When an alarm sounds at night, we should ___
   A) open the door and look out well
   B) leave the house at once
   C) hide under the bed quietly
   D) run back inside for our toys
16. The home of a chicken is called a ___
   A) coop
   B) burrow
   C) den
   D) hive
17. A spider lives in a ___
   A) kennel
   B) stable
   C) nest
   D) web
18. The home of a cow is a ___
   A) hive
   B) web
   C) shed
   D) coop
19. Which of these animals lives in an anthill?
   A) the hen
   B) the ant
   C) the fish
   D) the goat
20. The young one of a goat is called a ___
   A) kid
   B) cub
   C) foal
   D) lamb
21. A puppy is the young one of a ___
   A) bee
   B) cow
   C) dog
   D) hen
22. The young one of a butterfly is called a ___
   A) chick
   B) tadpole
   C) duckling
   D) caterpillar
23. Which of these animals hatches from an egg?
   A) the horse
   B) the duck
   C) the dog
   D) the goat
24. A machine is something that makes work ___
   A) darker
   B) longer
   C) easier
   D) wetter
25. Which of these is used for sweeping the floor?
   A) a broom
   B) a cup
   C) a plate
   D) a spoon
26. A hammer is used to ___
   A) cut the cloth
   B) drink the water
   C) iron the shirt
   D) knock in a nail
27. In the class, two sheets of paper are joined with a ___
   A) bell
   B) duster
   C) stapler
   D) ruler
28. Which machine makes a round hole in a page?
   A) a hole puncher
   B) a brush
   C) an eraser
   D) a stick of chalk
29. Everything around me that I can see, hear and smell is my ___
   A) lunch box
   B) environment
   C) exercise book
   D) school bell
30. Which sense tells me that the water from the tap is cold?
   A) seeing
   B) hearing
   C) smelling
   D) touching

## Section B — Sub-objective (10 marks)
Answer briefly.

31. Name the four water-borne diseases you know.
32. Mention four containers that can be used for storing water.
33. Give the home of each of these animals: the dog, the bird, the lion and the rabbit.
34. Write the young one of the dog, the cat, the cow and the sheep.
35. Mention four security gadgets that keep a house safe.
36. Name four simple machines used in the home.
37. Mention four simple machines we use in the classroom.
38. Name the five senses I use to explore my environment.
39. Say three things that make an environment unpleasant.
40. Mention three places you can find inside your school compound.

## Section C — Theory (10 marks)
Answer in full sentences.

41. What is a water-borne disease? Name four of them and state two ways of keeping it away. (2 marks)
42. Describe how water is kept clean at home. Name the containers that may be used for it. (2 marks)
43. What is security? Name four security gadgets and say the work each one does. (2 marks)
44. Write the home and the young one of each of these animals: the dog, the hen, the cow, the goat and the lion. (2 marks)
45. What is a machine? Name five simple machines used in the home and say what each one is used for. (2 marks)

# EXAMINATION PRACTICE — CHRISTIAN RELIGIOUS STUDIES (Nursery 2 · 3rd Term)

**Time:** 1 hour · **Total: 54 marks**

## Section A — Objective (30 marks)
Choose the correct option and ring the letter.

1. A saviour is somebody who ___ us
   A) forgets
   B) paints
   C) saves
   D) frightens

   A) sins
   B) sleep
   C) school
   D) soup

   B) cross
   C) horse
   D) table

   B) fifth
   C) third
   D) tenth

   A) heaven
   B) my school
   C) the river
   D) the market

11. The greatest commandment is to love ___
   A) the market on Sunday
   B) God with all my heart
   C) sleeping in the morning
   D) my own new ball
12. The second commandment is to love ___
   A) the compound gate
   B) my morning
   C) my neighbour as myself
   D) my teacher's stick
13. Who is my neighbour?

   B) only rich people
   C) only grown-ups
   D) only old people
14. We show love to God by praying, thanking Him and ___ Him
   A) losing
   B) hiding from
   C) teasing
   D) obeying
15. Jesus said, "Love ___ as I have loved you."
   A) strangers only
   B) one another
   C) the sun only
   D) nobody at all
16. God's rules for living are called ___
   A) commandments
   B) birthdays
   C) market days
   D) playgrounds
17. The commandments were brought to the people by ___ on a mountain
   A) Pharaoh
   B) Noah
   C) Goliath
   D) Moses
18. Which of these is one of God's rules?
   A) do not sing
   B) do not pray
   C) do not steal
   D) do not share
19. We obey God because He ___
   A) loves us and knows what is best
   B) is too far away to hear me
   C) lives next door and is busy
   D) does not know what I need
20. Jesus obeyed God ___ all His life
   A) by mistake
   B) perfectly
   C) never at all
   D) once a year
21. Prayer is talking to ___
   A) the farm
   B) the moon
   C) the door
   D) God
22. We can pray ___
   A) only at night
   B) only in the church
   C) in any place
   D) only on Sunday
23. Before a meal we ___
   A) thank God for the food
   B) sleep on the mat
   C) count the plates
   D) wash the cup
24. When we are sad or afraid we should ___
   A) run and shout
   B) tell a long lie
   C) hide under the bed
   D) ask God for help
25. When somebody is sick we ___
   A) take their food
   B) pray for them
   C) leave them alone
   D) laugh at them
26. "Pray without ceasing" means ___
   A) sleep and dream
   B) never pray at all
   C) keep talking to God all day
   D) pray once in a year
27. Which of these is a way to pray?
   A) kneeling with the head bowed
   B) shouting at my friends
   C) closing the door tight
   D) jumping up and down
28. God's rules were written on two tables of ___
   A) mud
   B) stone
   C) cloth
   D) wood
29. At night we ask God for ___
   A) new shoes
   B) a long class
   C) peaceful sleep
   D) another plate
30. A prayer is not better because it is ___
   A) short
   B) true
   C) quiet
   D) long

32. Mention three things a friend does for you

35. Name three places where we can pray

39. Say two ways we show love to God
